feeding them. Dynamic admissibility activates after the validation
program (Chapter 23) demonstrates that the bound functions are stable
under perturbation (Section 23.6) — the same discipline applied to
covariance shrinkage (Section 6.2) and to ensemble coefficients (Section
7.6).

  Fidelity note [MS §32]: the Modification Specification defines the
  dynamic-bound functional form symbolically — f of base bound, quality
  score, risk and liquidity. The concrete f (monotone in Q_i and
  Liquidity_i, inverse in Risk_i, bounded by the constitutional
  envelope) is a quantitative-implementation deliverable of the research
  program (Chapter 23) and is published with each methodology version.

8.3 The Weight-Velocity Constraint

The second line of defense operates on the rate of change rather than
the level. Even a weight vector that is perfectly admissible level-wise
can be dangerous if it arrives too fast: sudden reallocations amplify
execution costs, signal instability to users, and interact badly with
stressed markets. The constraint is simple and absolute — prevent sudden
changes [MS §33]:

|w_(i,t) - w_(i,t-1)| ≤ Δ_(i)

The bound is per component, so the total movement Σ_i |w_{i,t} -
w_{i,t-1}| is bounded by Σ_i Δ_i even though no separate portfolio-level
cap is needed (the sum-to-one constraint already forces offsetting
moves). The allowed change may itself depend on conditions [MS §33]:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Driver [MS §33]         Effect on Δ_{i,t}       Mechanism
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Liquidity               Δ shrinks for           Velocity inherits the
                          components with shallow same liquidity data as
                          execution capacity.     the MARP no-trade bands
                                                  (Chapters 10–11).

  Stress                  Δ shrinks in crisis     The crisis score
                          regimes.                (Section 5.5) raises
                                                  the smoothing parameter
                                                  (Section 8.4) and
                                                  tightens velocity
                                                  together — nonessential
                                                  movement slows on both
                                                  dimensions at once.

  Transaction costs       Δ shrinks when spreads, The optimizer already
                          slippage and impact     internalizes expected
                          widen.                  costs through the
                                                  turnover penalty
                                                  (Section 7.3); the
                                                  velocity cap makes the
                                                  bound hard rather than
                                                  priced.

  Structural change       Δ may temporarily widen Constituency
                          for a specific          transitions (Section
                          component.              4.6) ramp entering and
                                                  exiting components
                                                  along scheduled
                                                  transition windows —
                                                  widened by rule, never
                                                  by discretion.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

|w_(i,t) - w_(i,t-1)| ≤ Δ_(i,t) Δ_(i,t) = g(Liquidity_(i,t), Stress_(t),
Cost_(i,t), StructuralEvent_(i,t))

The deployed per-update velocity limits are the MAX_VELOCITY arrays of
Listing 1 (Section 2.7) and Listing 2 (Section 7.7): USD and EUR 0.50%,
JPY and GBP 0.30%, CNY and CHF 0.20%, and gold 0.50% per accepted
update. These are Risk-Council parameters (4-of-7), deliberately
distinct in governance weight from the constitutional envelopes:
velocity is an execution-risk control and may be tightened quickly under
stress, whereas the envelopes define the shape of the unit and move only
through the constitutional path. The constraint applies to the published
weight series — the smoothed live weights — and the genesis snapshot
(Section 3.4) supplies the initial condition w_{i,0} from which the
first velocity measurement is taken.

Velocity composes with the other anti-oscillation defenses rather than
duplicating them. Hysteresis (Section 4.7) prevents membership
oscillation; the rebalancing direction lock (Section 11.5) prevents
thrash inside MARP; the smoothing rule (Section 8.4) paces adoption of
every new target; the ensemble temperature (Section 7.6) bounds how fast
model influence can shift. The velocity cap is the finest-grained
defense of the set: it operates at the publication timescale, bounding
what may appear in the published series between two accepted updates
(Section 24.3). Note also the boundary of its scope: velocity constrains
the calculation output, while trading frequency is governed separately
by the MARP justification rules — daily calculation does not imply daily
trading (invariant I11, Section 10.1).

8.4 Stress-Adaptive Smoothing

After MASE calculates a target weight vector W_t^{Target} [MS §34], the
target does not become the live basket instantly. The third line of
defense is an explicit smoothing operator applied between the target
state and the live state, in the four-state weight distinction W^{Prior}
/ W^{Target} / W^{Smooth} / W^{Execution} (Section 2.3):

  W_(t)^(Smooth) = ρ_(t) W_(t-1) + (1 - ρ_(t)) W_(t)^(Target)

0 < ρ_(t) < 1

The interpretation is exponential blending: ρ_t is the persistence
parameter. ρ_t = 0 would mean instant adoption of every target; ρ_t
approaching 1 means near-frozen weights. The live vector is a convex
combination of yesterday's live vector and today's engine output, so
every published weight change is at most (1 - ρ_t) times the distance
from the live state to the target — smoothing is therefore also a
velocity reducer, and the velocity constraint of Section 8.3 applied to
the smoothed series is automatically easier to satisfy than the same
constraint applied to raw targets. The smoothed vector is what the
registry publishes as live (Section 7.7) and what MARP tracks toward
(Chapters 10–11); the target vector is published alongside it (Section
24.3), so observers can always distinguish what the engine wants from
what the basket holds.

The constitutional rule is direction-dependent: ρ_t may become larger
during unstable market conditions [MS §34]:

  More stress → slower nonessential changes. [MS §34]

The deployed values are smoothingRhoNormal = 0.50 and smoothingRhoStress
= 0.75 (1e18 scale, Listing 2, Section 7.7), with the stress flag
sourced from the crisis-score oracle path of Section 5.5 (threshold
0.70; conservative default to stress when the score is unavailable). The
economic logic: in stressed markets both the input signals (volatility
and correlation estimates, Sections 5.1 and 6.2) and the execution
conditions (spreads and depth, Chapters 10–11) are degraded; slowing
routine adaptation avoids chasing noise and avoids trading into illiquid
markets. The word nonessential is load-bearing: smoothing paces drift
toward targets, not safety-critical response. Risk-reducing corrections
under crisis conditions are handled by the MARP crisis mode (Section
13.7) and the defensive posture machinery, which operate on the
execution layer and are not slowed by ρ_t.

Smoothing does not change the target — it paces adoption. This
separation is deliberate: the target series W^{Target} is the
reproducible output of the published methodology (Section 24.1), the
smoothed series W^{Smooth} is the constitutional live state, and the
execution series W^{Execution} is what actually trades (Chapters 10–11).
Because all three are published, the cost of smoothing is always
visible: the tracking difference between W^{Smooth} and W^{Target} is an
attribution item, not a hidden drag (Section 9.5).

  Implementation note: Listing 2 (Section 7.7) applies the smoothing
  rule inside submitTargetWeights — the accepted registry state is
  W^{Smooth} = ρ·W_{prev} + (1 - ρ)·W_{Target} with ρ selected by the
  stress flag, and the target vector is stored alongside for publication
  and audit.

8.5 The Complete Constraint Set (Consolidated)

The consolidated MASE program is now stated in full [COO-12]. The
objective of Section 7.4 is minimized subject to the following
constraint system — this is the complete mathematical constitution of
the weighting layer, and every published weight vector must be feasible
with respect to all of it:

  ∑_(i) w_(i) = 1 w_(i) ≥ 0 L_(i,t) ≤ w_(i,t) ≤ U_(i,t) |w_(i,t) -
  w_(i,t-1)| ≤ Δ_(i,t) Coverage_(t) ≥ 100% LiquidityCoverage_(t) ≥
  LCR_(min)

The first four constraints govern the weight program itself and are
verified on-chain at submission time by the registry gate of Section 7.7
(Listing 2): sum-to-one and positivity are checked element-wise, the
admissibility envelopes are checked against the constitutional arrays,
and the velocity limit is checked against the previous live weights. The
last two couple the weight program to the reserve layer: Coverage_t ≥
100% is the full-backing requirement — the reserve-ratio hard floor RR_t
≥ 1.00 of invariant I2 — and LiquidityCoverage_t ≥ LCR_{min} is the
liquidity coverage requirement. Both live in the reserve architecture:
the coverage floor is enforced at Section 14.2 and the liquidity regime
operates in Chapter 13. They are monitored continuously, not only at
weight-submission time, and a target vector whose execution would breach
them is not executable — MARP (Chapters 10–11) refuses trades that
violate reserve or liquidity rules, so an infeasible-in-practice
solution never leaves the computation.

  ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Constraint            Type              Meaning           Enforcement
  --------------------- ----------------- ----------------- -----------------
  ∑_i w_i = 1           Constitutional    Full investment:  Section 8.5;
                                          weights are       on-chain gate
                                          shares of exactly §7.7 (Listing 2)
                                          one unit basket.  

  w_i ≥ 0               Constitutional    Long-only: no     Section 8.5;
                                          short positions,  on-chain gate
                                          no leverage in    §7.7 (Listing 2)
                                          the reference     
                                          basket.           

  L_{i,t} ≤ w_{i,t} ≤   Constitutional    Admissibility     §2.7 and §7.7
  U_{i,t}                                 envelopes (static (Listings 1–2);
                                          Section 8.1,      7/7 + timelock to
                                          dynamic Section   change
                                          8.2).             

  |w_{i,t} - w_{i,t-1}| Risk              Weight velocity:  §7.7; MARP
  ≤ Δ_{i,t}                               bounded rate of   execution gates,
                                          change per        Chapters 10–11
                                          component.        

  Coverage_t ≥ 100\%    Monetary          Full reserve      Chapter 13; hard
                                          backing of every  floor §14.2
                                          unit (RR hard     
                                          floor, invariant  
                                          I2).              

  LiquidityCoverage_t ≥ Risk              Liquidity         Chapter 13 (LCR
  LCR_{min}                               coverage of       regime)
                                          stress redemption 
                                          demand.           
  ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

This table is the consolidated reference for the whole constraint
system: wherever later chapters enforce, test or publish constraints,
they cite back to this set. The typing matters. Constitutional
constraints define the shape of the unit and change only through 7-of-7
governance with timelock; risk constraints bound how the system may move
and are Risk-Council parameters; monetary constraints tie the weighting
layer to the promise that every unit is fully backed and liquid
(Chapters 13–14). The optimizer of Section 7.4 is free anywhere inside
this polytope — and nowhere else.

Feasibility and robustness of the constraint set are themselves
validation objects (Chapter 23). The static envelopes admit a non-empty
interior (lower bounds sum to 79%, upper bounds to 125%); the
perturbation study (Section 23.6) tests that the feasible set survives
stressed inputs and that no single constraint becomes accidentally
binding under realistic data; the stress program (Section 23.9) tests
the joint system — envelopes, velocity, coverage and liquidity coverage
together — under crisis scenarios. A constraint set that is only
satisfiable in calm markets is a failure, and the validation program
exists to find that out before production authorization (Chapter 25).

  Lineage [COO-12]: the review's completion of the MASE program — 'Your
  current equation is good, but I would make it complete' — added the
  admissibility, velocity, coverage and liquidity constraints to the
  bare sum-to-one/non-negativity pair, exactly as consolidated above.
  Chapter 9 completes the other half of the review's demand: the
  index-level mathematics that turns constrained weights into a
  published reference value.

9. Chain-Linked Index Valuation and Attribution

9.1 Reference Basket Valuation

  Weights are only half of the definition of MTQ Σ; the other half is
  the equation that turns weights and prices into a published reference
  value. This chapter specifies that equation — and the chain-link
  machinery that keeps it honest when weights change [MS §73–§78,
  COO-16–COO-18].

Let W_{i,t} be the current weight of component i and P_{i,t} the
reference price of asset i at time t [MS §73] — the canonical
multi-source prices of Chapter 17, USD-quoted, with the gold price taken
from the canonical gold feed of Section 17.4. The reference unit value
can be represented as [MS §73]:

NAV_(t) = G_(t) ∑_(i) W_(i,t) (P_(i,t))/(P_(i,0))

where G_t is a transparent normalization factor [MS §73] and P_{i,0} are
the immutable base-date fixings of the genesis snapshot (Section 3.4).
The initial reference level can be set to a chosen institutional unit
[MS §73]:

NAV₀ = 1

so that one unit of the reference index is worth 1.0000 at genesis; 'or
another defined reference unit' [MS §73] is a publication constant,
chosen once and never silently changed — a re-denomination would be a
methodology-version event with full governance (Section 24.2), not a
recalibration. This value is the I_t (NAV_t) variable of the core
variable table (Section 2.5); the monetary price and liability machinery
that consumes it — P_{MTQ,t} = I_t / I_{base} — is specified in Chapter
18.

The denomination should not be confused with a promise that MTQ Σ
remains at a fixed market price [MS §73]. NAV_0 = 1 is a normalization
choice, exactly like an index being set to 100 at its base date: it
fixes where the series starts, not where it goes. The reference index is
a weighted basket of currencies and gold, and it moves with its
constituents; users minting at index value 1.12 receive fewer tokens per
USD than users minting at 0.92 (Sections 3.5 and 19.2). Any
communication that presents the denomination as a peg is a category
error of the same kind as presenting the strategic prior as the
composition (Section 3.1).

One obligation is stated now and discharged across Sections 9.2 and 9.3:
the aggregate form above — a weighted sum of price relatives with a
normalization divisor — must be formally reconciled with the chain-link
methodology during quantitative implementation [COO-17]. The
reconciliation is not cosmetic: the aggregate form and the chain-linked
recursion are different mathematical objects that must be made to
produce one published I_t, and the divisor mechanism G_t is precisely
where that reconciliation lives.

  Fidelity note: this equation adapts the v1.2 normalized index formula
  [BP §2.2] — a ratio of fixed-quantity basket values — to the adaptive
  design: fixed quantities q_i are replaced by live weights W_{i,t}, and
  the base-date denominator is replaced by the base fixings P_{i,0} plus
  the divisor G_t. The full BP lineage and the adapted construction are
  carried in Section 9.7.

9.2 The Chain-Linked Index Calculation

Because weights change, MTQ Σ should use a chain-link methodology rather
than allowing arbitrary weight changes to create artificial returns [MS
§74]. The problem is structural: any index defined directly as a
weighted price aggregate would jump whenever the weights are re-set — a
purely compositional event that has nothing to do with economic
performance. A system whose 'return' contains rebalancing jumps is not a
measurement instrument; it is an accounting artifact. The specification
therefore defines the index by recursion. At rebalance time t [MS §74]:

I_(t) = I_(t-1) ( ∑_(i) W_(i,t) (P_(i,t))/(P_(i,t-1)) )

The COO review reconstructs the same recursion with the weights in force
at the start of the period [COO-16] — the form in which the period
return is a weighted average of price relatives:

  I_(t) = I_(t-1) [ ∑_(i) W_(i,t-1) (P_(i,t))/(P_(i,t-1)) ]

COO-16 calls this 'the biggest missing equation' of the original
specification, and the label is accurate. First, it is the equation that
defines how the reference value itself evolves: the weighting
methodology of Chapters 5–7 is meaningless without a level equation to
apply it to. Second, it structurally prevents weight changes from
creating artificial returns: the return over any period is computed with
the weights in force at the start of that period, so a weight change
enters only future periods' returns and can never masquerade as a gain
or a loss — precisely the property a reference unit needs. Third, it
makes the contribution mathematics of Section 9.4 exact: each
component's contribution is its start-of-period weight times its price
relative, and the contributions sum to the index return by construction.
Fourth, it is the computational core of the on-chain implementation
(Listing 3, Section 9.8).

The two written forms — W_{i,t} in the specification [MS §74], W_{i,t-1}
in the review reconstruction [COO-16] — are the same equation under two
conventions, and the operative convention is fixed per methodology
version exactly like the signal-direction convention of Section 5.2:
period returns are computed with the weights in force at the start of
the period. Within a period that contains an accepted weight update, the
weights in force are the pre-update weights; the update itself is
neutralized by the divisor of Section 9.3 and begins governing returns
from the next period. Listing 3 implements exactly this convention.
Beyond the convention, the specification requires appropriate
divisor/normalization methodology [MS §74]: between accepted updates the
recursion runs with a fixed weight vector and the index is a pure
weighted price-relative aggregate; at an accepted update, the chain-link
adjustment of Section 9.3 preserves continuity. The index advances on
the daily calculation cadence (Section 10.1) — daily calculation does
not imply daily trading (invariant I11) — and every increment of the
published series is independently recomputable from published data
(Sections 24.1 and 24.3).

9.3 The Chain-Link Adjustment at Rebalance

At a rebalance, the basket changes composition. Let the pre-rebalance
basket value be B_t^- and the post-rebalance basket value be B_t^+ [MS
§75]. Set a chain-link factor [MS §75]:

D_(t) = (B_(t)⁻)/(B_(t)⁺)

and preserve continuity [MS §75]:

  I_(t)⁺ = D_(t) · B_(t)⁺ = B_(t)⁻

The clarifying point is what the divisor does: it rescales the index
basis so that the rebalance itself produces no index jump. Immediately
before the weight change, the index level equals B_t^-; immediately
after, the new-basis computation B_t^+ is multiplied by D_t, and the
product equals B_t^- by construction. Whatever numerical difference
exists between valuing the current prices with the old composition and
with the new composition is absorbed into the divisor rather than
appearing as a return. 'This is crucial for a continuously evolving
index' [MS §75]: an evolving index that did not chain-link would report
phantom gains on every rebalance, and an auditor comparing published
returns to constituent price moves would find unexplainable residuals —
exactly the failure mode COO-16 was written to close.

Both B_t^- and B_t^+ are valued at the same, post-move prices, so D_t
isolates the pure compositional change. In the recursion of Section 9.2
the neutrality is already structural — the weight change enters only
future period returns — and D_t is the equivalent adjustment expressed
in the value-aggregate form of Section 9.1. This is where the formal
reconciliation obligation lands [COO-17]: the aggregate form NAV_t = G_t
Σ_i W_{i,t} (P_{i,t}/P_{i,0}) and the chain-linked recursion must be
made to produce one published I_t during quantitative implementation,
and G_t is the natural home of the accumulated chain-link history — the
cumulative product of the D_t factors, plus any re-normalization such as
a constituency addition that changes the component set. The precise
valuation basis of B_t^- and B_t^+ — raw USD values of the two
compositions, or base-relative aggregates against the genesis fixings —
is one of the conventions quantitative implementation must pin, because
both bases deliver the same continuity guarantee but different numerical
divisors; the pinned choice is published with the methodology version
and tested in the validation program (Chapter 23).

Every applied D_t is published: Listing 3 (Section 9.8) emits a
ChainLinkAdjusted event carrying D_t, B_t^- and B_t^+ at each weight
change, and the divisor series is part of the publication obligations
(Section 24.3). One further honesty rule: the reference index measures
the frictionless reference basket. Real rebalances executed by MARP
incur execution costs (Chapters 10–11); those costs are never folded
into the index level — they appear as the cost effect in the attribution
of Section 9.5, so the gap between the reference unit and realized
portfolio performance is always visible rather than hidden inside the
divisor.

  Reconciliation note [COO-17]: 'This needs to be formally reconciled
  with the chain-link methodology during quantitative implementation.'
  The deliverables are: (i) the pinned functional form of G_t; (ii) the
  pinned valuation basis of B_t^- and B_t^+; and (iii) reconciliation
  tests demonstrating that the aggregate form and the recursion produce
  the identical published series, included in the validation program
  (Chapter 23). Until pinned, both forms are carried side by side,
  exactly as the source documents leave them.

9.4 Contribution Mathematics — Gold and Currency

Contribution mathematics answers the most basic question about any
period: which component moved the index, and by how much? Gold's
contribution to MTQ Σ return is [MS §76]:

Contribution_(G) = W_(G,t-1) ( (P_(G,t))/(P_(G,t-1)) - 1 )

Analogously for every currency component [MS §76]:

Contribution_(i) = W_(i,t-1) ( (P_(i,t))/(P_(i,t-1)) - 1 )

The start-of-period weight W_{i,t-1} is the same convention as the
recursion of Section 9.2 — this is what makes the decomposition exact
rather than approximate. As a concrete illustration: if gold's weight in
force at the start of a period is 26% and the canonical gold price
(Section 17.4) rises 3% over the period, gold's contribution is 0.26 ×
0.03 = +0.78%, and no other term is needed to explain that portion of
the index return. The period return of the reference index is the sum of
contributions [MS §77]:

R_(MTQ,t) = ∑_(i) Contribution_(i)

subject to the chain-link/rebalance methodology [MS §77]. The qualifier
is not decorative: in a period containing a rebalance, the divisor D_t
of Section 9.3 guarantees that the compositional change contributes
exactly zero, so the sum of contributions equals the published index
return to the tick. This is the additivity property the attribution
engine of Section 9.5 depends on: R_{MTQ,t} can be published as a sum of
seven numbers, one per component, with nothing left over.

Two honest observations belong in the record. First, prices are
USD-quoted (Chapter 17), so the USD component's price relative
P_{USD,t}/P_{USD,t-1} is identically 1: in USD-valuation terms the USD
sleeve contributes no price return — its role in the index is allocation
and diversification, and its presence shows up in the allocation and
rebalancing effects of Section 9.5, not in the movement effect. Second,
the gold contribution uses the canonical gold price of Section 17.4 —
the multi-source quorum price, not any single venue's quote — so the
contribution decomposition inherits the oracle system's independence
guarantees (Section 17.3). Per-component contributions are published
every period alongside the weights (Section 24.3).

9.5 The Attribution Engine

Every period, the protocol publishes the full decomposition of the
reference index return [MS §78]:

R_(MTQ) = R_(USD) + R_(EUR) + R_(JPY) + R_(GBP) + R_(CNY) + R_(CHF) +
R_(Gold)

where each R_i is the contribution of Section 9.4, plus four effects
that separate what the market did from what the system did [MS §78]:

Allocation effect. The value added or subtracted by the choice of
weights, measured against a defined baseline — typically the strategic
prior (Section 3.2) or the previous published basket. A positive
allocation effect means the engine's deviations from the baseline were
subsequently rewarded: components that were over-weighted outperformed,
and components that were under-weighted underperformed. It is the
primary evidence for whether adaptivity is earning its complexity, and
it is the quantity the out-of-sample scoring discipline (Sections 7.6
and 23.4) is designed to keep honest.

Movement effect. The pure price movement of the constituents with
weights held at their start-of-period values — the market's
contribution, independent of any decision the system made. It equals the
sum of the component contributions (Section 9.4) and answers the
question of what the world did to the basket in the period. Separating
movement from allocation shows how much of a period's outcome is
environment and how much is methodology.

Rebalancing effect. The consequence of the weight changes themselves:
the divergence between valuing current prices with the old composition
and with the new one — neutralized in the index level by the divisor D_t
(Section 9.3) — together with any rebalancing premium or drag realized
by MARP when it actually executes (Chapters 10–11). It isolates the
cost-and-benefit of the system's own turnover, distinct from price
movement. Because D_t forces the compositional jump to zero, a nonzero
rebalancing effect in the published attribution reflects realized
execution behavior, not index arithmetic.

Cost effect. The friction of operating the system: spreads, slippage,
fees, market impact and operational costs of the trades that rebalances
required — the Cost_i decomposition of the MARP net-benefit test
(Spread + Slippage + Fees + Impact + Operational, Section 10.8).
Publishing it keeps the difference between the frictionless reference
index and realized performance visible at all times. A persistent cost
effect exceeding the allocation effect is the system's own signal that
adaptivity is being over-traded — the same discipline the turnover
penalty internalizes at optimization time (Section 7.3).

  The system should be completely explainable. [MS §78]

The COO review states the purpose plainly [COO-18]: the decomposition
'allows everyone to see: was MTQ Σ movement caused by USD, EUR, gold, or
the weighting algorithm?'. That question — market or methodology — is
the question every auditor, user and regulator asks first about an
adaptive unit, and the attribution engine answers it with arithmetic
instead of narrative. Attribution is published every period together
with the weights, contributions and divisors (Section 24.3); the
attribution ledger is part of the permanent audit trail (Section 9.6,
Chapter 24) and is reproducible from published data (Section 24.1).

9.6 Genesis Verification and Audit Trail

The base denominator of the index — the value that makes I_0 = 1.0000 —
is the single most audited constant in the system: if it were wrong or
mutable, every published level after it would be meaningless. The v1.2
genesis verification machinery is carried forward essentially unchanged
[BP §2.6]: to ensure the denominator is auditable and independently
verifiable, the contract emits a genesis event once, during the genesis
transaction, and provides a verification function that any external
observer can call. The genesis snapshot itself — quantities and fixings
— is specified in Section 3.4; this section specifies the verification
layer on top of it.

9.6.1 The Genesis Verification Event [BP §2.6.1]

The event is emitted at the end of the genesis() function, immediately
after the base denominator is assigned [BP §2.6.1]. The v1.2 form,
reproduced as specified:

  // v1.2 lineage [BP §2.6.1] — names retained for traceability.

  event GenesisVerification(

  uint256 indexed gfbBaseDenominator,

  uint256 eurUsdBase,

  uint256 gbpUsdBase,

  uint256 jpyUsdBase,

  uint256 cnyUsdBase,

  uint256 timestamp

  );

Purpose [BP §2.6.1]: the event allows off-chain observers — auditors,
indexers, explorers — to reconstruct the denominator and verify that it
matches the hard-coded base fixings. Under v2.0 the event is extended to
the seven-component basket and renamed GenesisVerified: the fields
become indexBaseDenominator, eurUsdBase, gbpUsdBase, jpyUsdBase,
cnyUsdBase, chfUsdBase, goldUsdBase and timestamp (Listing 3, Section
9.8). The extension is mechanical — CHF and gold were added to the
basket by the Modification Specification (Sections 3.3 and 3.4), so
their base fixings join the event; the mechanism, the one-time emission
and the reconstruction purpose are unchanged.

9.6.2 Off-Chain Verification Function [BP §2.6.2]

The v1.2 verification function, reproduced as specified (the dimensional
conventions are the v1.2 ones; the v2.0 adaptation recomputes under the
USD-equivalent notional convention of Section 3.4):

  // v1.2 lineage [BP §2.6.2] — verifyGFBBase().

  /**

  * @dev Allows anyone to verify that the stored GFB_BASE_DENOMINATOR

  * matches the base-date FX fixings defined in §2.3.

  * @return true if the stored denominator equals the recomputed value.

  */

  function verifyGFBBase() external view returns (bool) {

  uint256 recomputed = Q_USD * 1e18

  + Q_EUR * BASE_EUR_USD

  + Q_GBP * BASE_GBP_USD

  + Q_JPY * BASE_JPY_USD

  + Q_CNY * BASE_CNY_USD;

  return recomputed == GFB_BASE_DENOMINATOR;

  }

Usage [BP §2.6.2]: anyone can call this function to confirm the
denominator is correct; a false return means the genesis transaction was
tampered with or the hard-coded base values are inconsistent. The v2.0
adaptation — verifyGenesis() in Listing 3 — recomputes the base basket
value from the genesis constants and additionally checks the calibration
invariant of Section 3.4: the USD-equivalent genesis notionals sum to
exactly 1.0000 per MTQ unit, which is what makes I_0 = 1.0000 exact. The
deeper audit is off-chain, exactly as the blueprint intends: auditors
take the GenesisVerified event fields, cross-check them against the
published base-fixing table (Section 3.4) and the deployment record
(Appendix D), and recompute the calibration independently. A mismatch
between the event fields and the contract constants indicates a tampered
or mis-deployed genesis transaction.

9.6.3 Testnet Implementation Note [BP §2.6.3]

On testnets (Arc, Monad, Solana Devnet), the deployer must emit the
verification event manually after the genesis transaction by calling the
internal _emitGenesisVerification() helper, so that the event is present
in the testnet logs for audit testing [BP §2.6.3]. Listing 3 integrates
the helper into genesis() directly, so a single call seeds the index and
produces the event; the testnet deployment scripts, mock oracles and
per-chain checklists are specified in Appendix D and are outside this
chapter's scope.

9.6.4 Developer Checklist [BP §2.6.4]

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Step                                Description
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  1                                   Ensure verifyGenesis() is included
                                      in the index contract.

  2                                   Call _emitGenesisVerification() at
                                      the end of genesis().

  3                                   After deployment, call
                                      verifyGenesis() to confirm the
                                      denominator is correct.

  4                                   Record the emitted event in the
                                      deployment documentation for audit
                                      trail.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The checklist is carried verbatim from the blueprint with the v2.0
function names substituted [BP §2.6.4]. The audit trail does not end at
deployment: the genesis event is the first entry in a ledger that
continues with every IndexUpdated and ChainLinkAdjusted event (Listing
3), every published weight vector (Section 24.3) and every data version
(Section 24.2), so the entire history of the index level from genesis to
any current timestamp is reconstructible from public information.

  Fidelity note [BP §2.6]: the v1.2 names — GenesisVerification event,
  verifyGFBBase() function, GFB_BASE_DENOMINATOR constant — are retained
  above as lineage; the v2.0 names are GenesisVerified, verifyGenesis()
  and INDEX_BASE_DENOMINATOR, with CHF and gold fixings added to the
  event. The mechanism is otherwise unchanged, including the one-time
  emission rule and the manual testnet emission rule.

9.7 Index Construction Details

This section carries the v1.2 index construction machinery — the
normalized formula, the base fixings, the oracle feed mapping and the
reference/exposure separation — in the places where it remains
authoritative, and marks the places where v2.0 changes the mathematics
[BP §2.2, §2.3, §2.4, §2.5]. The fidelity change runs through all four
subsections: the v1.2 index was a fixed-quantity basket (exactly the IMF
SDR construction), while the v2.0 reference index I_t is weight-defined
and chain-linked; quantities survive only as the immutable genesis
snapshot of Section 3.4.

9.7.1 The Normalized Index Formula [BP §2.2]

The v1.2 construction ensures the index starts at exactly 1.0000 by
dividing the current basket value by the base-date basket value [BP
§2.2]:

GFB_(t) = (q_(USD)· 1.00 + q_(EUR)· FX^(EUR/USD)_(t) + q_(GBP)·
FX^(GBP/USD)_(t) + q_(JPY)· FX^(JPY/USD)_(t) + q_(CNY)·
FX^(CNY/USD)_(t))/(q_(USD)· 1.00 + q_(EUR)· FX^(EUR/USD)_(base) +
q_(GBP)· FX^(GBP/USD)_(base) + q_(JPY)· FX^(JPY/USD)_(base) + q_(CNY)·
FX^(CNY/USD)_(base))

where FX^{i/USD}_t is the spot price of currency i against the US Dollar
at time t (source: Chainlink/Pyth, Chapter 17) and FX^{i/USD}_base is
the spot price at the genesis timestamp, January 1, 2026, 00:00 UTC [BP
§2.2]. The base-date calibration objective was to set the initial
quantities q_i so that the index base equals 1.0000 — one MTQ unit
started at exactly 1.00 USD-equivalent [BP §2.1].

The v2.0 adaptation replaces fixed quantities with live weights and the
base-value denominator with the base fixings plus a divisor:

I_(t) = G_(t) ∑_(i) W_(i,t) (P_(i,t))/(P_(i,0)) (value-aggregate form,
Section 9.1)

with evolution governed by the chain-linked recursion of Section 9.2
rather than by direct recomputation of the ratio. The denominator role
of the v1.2 formula splits in two under v2.0: the immutable
INDEX_BASE_DENOMINATOR (set at genesis from the Section 3.4 snapshot;
Listing 3) anchors the normalization, and the divisor G_t absorbs
everything that has happened since — the accumulated chain-link history
of Section 9.3. This is why the reconciliation obligation [COO-17] of
Section 9.3 is stated as a first-class deliverable and not a footnote.

  Fidelity note: fixed quantities → weights. BP §2.1 defines the basket
  as 'fixed notional quantities of five major currencies, exactly as the
  IMF SDR basket is constructed' [BP §2.1]; the Modification
  Specification supersedes this with the adaptive, MASE-weighted basket
  of seven components (Section 3.1). The genesis snapshot (Section 3.4)
  is the bridge: it is the one moment at which the fixed-quantity
  picture is literally true, which is why it is immutable and why its
  verification (Section 9.6) is permanent.

9.7.2 Base-Date Fixings (Genesis Oracles) [BP §2.3]

The denominator is computed once at contract deployment using hard-coded
reference values. These are only used to normalize the index — they are
not updated [BP §2.3]:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Currency Pair           Base FX Rate (Jan 1,    Source Reference
                          2026)                   
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  EUR/USD                 1.05000                 Chainlink Base Feed

  GBP/USD                 1.25000                 Chainlink Base Feed

  JPY/USD                 0.00670                 Chainlink Base Feed

  CNY/USD (CNH)           0.14000                 Chainlink Base Feed
                                                  (Offshore Yuan)
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

CNY note [BP §2.3]: the index uses the offshore Yuan (CNH) rate, which
is freely tradeable; the on-chain asset representing CNY is resolved via
the Asset Admission Registry (Chapter 15) — the index itself is
currency-exposure defined, independent of the specific token issuer.
Under v2.0 the fixing table gains two rows carried in the genesis
snapshot of Section 3.4: CHF/USD = 1.13000 and the gold base fixing of
USD 2,500.00 per reference gold unit. All six fixings are immutable
constants in Listing 3 (BASE_EUR_USD through BASE_GOLD_USD) and are
published in the GenesisVerified event (Section 9.6.1); the
cross-reference for the complete table, including asset implementations
and genesis notionals, is Section 3.4.

9.7.3 On-Chain Oracle Feed Mapping [BP §2.4]

The v1.2 contract fetched live FX rates from verified oracle
aggregators, with a primary Chainlink feed, a Pyth fallback and a
60-second staleness threshold per pair [BP §2.4]. The addresses below
are the blueprint's Arbitrum Mainnet placeholders; they are replaced
with the correct addresses for the target chain, and on testnets all
feeds are mocks (Appendix D):

  ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Currency Pair     Primary Oracle        Fallback (Pyth)   Staleness
                    (Chainlink)                             Threshold
  ----------------- --------------------- ----------------- -----------------
  EUR/USD           0x…EURUSD_Chainlink   0x…EURUSD_Pyth    60 seconds

  GBP/USD           0x…GBPUSD_Chainlink   0x…GBPUSD_Pyth    60 seconds

  JPY/USD           0x…JPYUSD_Chainlink   0x…JPYUSD_Pyth    60 seconds

  CNY/USD           0x…CNYUSD_Chainlink   0x…CNYUSD_Pyth    60 seconds
  ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

Implementation rule [BP §2.4]: if the primary oracle is stale (no update
within 60 seconds) or deviates more than 2.5% from the fallback, the
contract reverts to the fallback; if both are stale or unavailable, the
protocol pauses minting and rebalancing. Under v2.0 this machinery is
superseded by the multi-source canonical oracle system of Chapter 17:
the pair mapping extends to CHF/USD, the canonical gold price requires
an independent-source quorum (Section 17.4), and the pause semantics are
governed by the oracle safety rules (Sections 17.3–17.7) together with
the crisis framework (Section 13.7). The mapping is summarized here for
completeness of the index construction; Chapter 17 is authoritative for
feed selection, deviation logic and failover.

9.7.4 Derivative Asset Value and Exposure [BP §2.5]

The reference index is a measurement object, not a mandate on holdings
[BP §2.5]. The actual collateral held in the vault (USDC, EURC, PAXG and
other admitted instruments, Chapter 15) does not need to match the
basket weights; it is managed separately by the rebalancing engine —
MARP under v2.0 (Chapters 10–11) — under the reserve architecture of
Chapters 13–14. This is the two-system architecture of Section 2.4: the
reference basket defines the unit, the reserve backs it, and MARP keeps
the reserve tracking the reference within economically justified bounds
rather than by mechanical replication.

The v1.2 contract method getGFB() implemented the valuation: fetch live
FX rates from the oracle aggregator; compute the numerator (current
basket value) as the quantity-weighted sum; divide by the immutable
GFB_BASE_DENOMINATOR computed once at deployment from the base fixings;
and return the normalized value scaled to 1e18, so that 1.0000 is
represented as 1e18 [BP §2.5]. Developer notes carried from the
blueprint: the denominator is computed once at deployment using the base
fixings of Section 9.7.2, and getPrice() returns 18-decimal prices per
the oracle layer [BP §2.5]. Under v2.0, getGFB() is superseded by
getIndex() of Listing 3 (Section 9.8), which computes the chain-linked
level from live registry weights and canonical prices rather than from
fixed quantities. The monetary-unit consumers of the index — the
reference price, liability and circulating-supply getters (BP §3.1–§3.3)
— belong to Chapter 18 and are cross-referenced there, not duplicated
here.

9.8 Smart Contract Implementation — Chain-Linked Index and Genesis
Verification

The contract below is the on-chain half of this chapter. It implements
the recursion of Section 9.2 (the getIndex machinery requested by
Listing 1, Section 2.7), records the chain-link adjustment of Section
9.3, sets the immutable base denominator at genesis and emits the
verification event of Section 9.6 — adapting the v1.2 genesis
verification and base-denominator storage [BP §2.6, BP §3.3.1] to the
adaptive design. Live weights are read from the MASE weight registry of
Listing 2 (Section 7.7), so the index can never drift from the
constitutionally verified weight state; prices are read from the
canonical oracle layer (Chapter 17), including the quorum gold price of
Section 17.4. The contract never re-solves anything on-chain: it is a
chain-state machine whose every transition is checkable against the
published formulas, whose every event is an audit-trail entry, and whose
keeper role is permissionless so that no single actor controls when the
index rolls forward. The monetary-unit machinery that consumes
getIndex() — the reference price, liability and circulating-supply
getters of BP §3.1–§3.3 — belongs to Chapter 18 and is not duplicated
here.

Listing 3 — Chain-linked index, base denominator and genesis
verification (adapts BP §2.6 and §3.3.1)

  // SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT

  pragma solidity ^0.8.19;

  /// @title MTQChainLinkIndex — the chain-linked reference index I_t
  (v2.0).

  /// @notice Adapts BP §2.6 (genesis verification) and BP §3.3.1 (base
  denominator

  /// and index getter) to the adaptive, chain-linked design of Chapter
  9.

  /// @dev Authoritative recursion (§9.2, COO-16):

  /// I_t = I_{t-1} * [ sum_i W_{i,t-1} * P_{i,t} / P_{i,t-1} ]

  /// Weights come from the MASE registry (Listing 2, §7.7); prices come
  from

  /// the canonical multi-source oracle layer (Chapter 17). The contract
  is a

  /// chain-state machine: it never re-solves the optimization on-chain.

  contract MTQChainLinkIndex {

  // ---- Component order (fixed at genesis; XAU = index 6; Listing 1
  §2.7) ----

  bytes3[7] public COMPONENTS =
  ["USD","EUR","JPY","GBP","CNY","CHF","XAU"];

  // ---- Genesis snapshot q_i: USD-equivalent notionals (§3.4, Listing
  1 §2.7) ----

  // SNAPSHOT ONLY. After genesis the basket evolves by weights, not

  // quantities; these constants anchor the base denominator.

  uint256 public constant Q_USD = 0.27e18;

  uint256 public constant Q_EUR = 0.20e18;

  uint256 public constant Q_JPY = 0.09e18;

  uint256 public constant Q_GBP = 0.08e18;

  uint256 public constant Q_CNY = 0.05e18;

  uint256 public constant Q_CHF = 0.05e18;

  uint256 public constant Q_GOLD = 0.26e18;

  // ---- Base fixings: immutable normalization values (BP §2.3; §3.4)
  ----

  uint256 public constant BASE_EUR_USD = 1.0500e8; // 8 decimals
  (Chainlink style)

  uint256 public constant BASE_GBP_USD = 1.2500e8;

  uint256 public constant BASE_JPY_USD = 0.0067e8;

  uint256 public constant BASE_CNY_USD = 0.1400e8; // offshore CNH

  uint256 public constant BASE_CHF_USD = 1.1300e8;

  uint256 public constant BASE_GOLD_USD = 2500.00e8; // USD per
  reference gold unit

  // ---- The denominator for normalization, set at genesis (BP §3.3.1)
  ----

  // v1.2 name: GFB_BASE_DENOMINATOR. v2.0 name: INDEX_BASE_DENOMINATOR.

  // Under the §3.4 convention the q_i are USD-equivalent notionals, so

  // the base basket value is sum(q_i) = 1.0000e18 by calibration; it is

  // computed explicitly (not assumed) so that it is auditable.

  uint256 public immutable INDEX_BASE_DENOMINATOR;

  // ---- Chain state: the authoritative recursion bookkeeping (§9.2)
  ----

  uint256 public indexValue; // I_t, 1e18 scale; 1.0000 = 1e18

  uint256[7] public lastPrices; // P_{i,t-1}, 1e18-scale USD-quoted

  uint256[7] public lastWeights; // W_{i,t-1} in force, 1e18 scale

  uint256 public chainLinkDivisor; // cumulative product of D_t (§9.3),
  1e18

  uint256 public lastUpdate;

  // ---- External dependencies ----

  IMASEWeightRegistry public weightRegistry; // Listing 2 (§7.7)

  IIndexOracle public oracle; // canonical prices (Chapter 17)

  address public deployer;

  // ---- Events ----

  // BP §2.6.1 GenesisVerification, extended to seven components,
  renamed.

  event GenesisVerified(

  uint256 indexed indexBaseDenominator,

  uint256 eurUsdBase,

  uint256 gbpUsdBase,

  uint256 jpyUsdBase,

  uint256 cnyUsdBase,

  uint256 chfUsdBase,

  uint256 goldUsdBase,

  uint256 timestamp

  );

  event IndexUpdated(

  uint256 newIndex,

  uint256[7] prices,

  uint256[7] weights,

  bytes32 methodologyVersion,

  bytes32 dataVersion,

  uint256 timestamp

  );

  event ChainLinkAdjusted(uint256 D, uint256 bMinus, uint256 bPlus,
  uint256 timestamp);

  constructor(address registry, address oracle_) {

  deployer = msg.sender;

  weightRegistry = IMASEWeightRegistry(registry);

  oracle = IIndexOracle(oracle_);

  INDEX_BASE_DENOMINATOR =

  Q_USD + Q_EUR + Q_JPY + Q_GBP + Q_CNY + Q_CHF + Q_GOLD; // = 1.0000e18

  }

  /// @notice One-time genesis: seeds I_0 = 1.0000 and emits the audit
  event.

  /// @dev Requires the registry to already hold the genesis weights
  (§3.4).

  /// Prices are seeded from the immutable base fixings, not live feeds,

  /// so I_0 = 1.0000 exactly, independent of feed noise at genesis.

  function genesis() external {

  require(msg.sender == deployer, "Only deployer");

  require(indexValue == 0, "Genesis already executed");

  (uint256[7] memory w, bytes32 mVersion, bytes32 dVersion) =

  weightRegistry.getLiveWeights();

  uint256 sum = 0;

  for (uint256 i = 0; i < 7; i++) {

  sum += w[i];

  lastWeights[i] = w[i];

  lastPrices[i] = _basePrice18(i);

  }

  require(sum == 1e18, "Registry weights must sum to 1");

  indexValue = 1e18;

  chainLinkDivisor = 1e18;

  lastUpdate = block.timestamp;

  _emitGenesisVerification(); // BP §2.6.1 / §2.6.3

  emit IndexUpdated(indexValue, lastPrices, w, mVersion, dVersion,
  block.timestamp);

  }

  /// @notice Advance the chain-linked index one period (§9.2, §9.3).

  /// @dev Permissionless keeper call on the daily calculation cadence

  /// (§10.1). The pause rules of Chapters 13/17 stop updates when feeds

  /// are unusable: the oracle layer reverts instead of returning bad

  /// prices, so this function cannot advance on stale data.

  function updateIndex() external {

  require(indexValue != 0, "Genesis not executed");

  (uint256[7] memory wNew, bytes32 mVersion, bytes32 dVersion) =

  weightRegistry.getLiveWeights();

  uint256[7] memory p = _fetchPrices();

  // 1. Price return over the period, weighted by the weights IN FORCE

  // over the period (COO-16: W_{i,t-1}). A weight change accepted

  // during the period enters only FUTURE period returns.

  uint256 growth = 0;

  for (uint256 i = 0; i < 7; i++) {

  require(p[i] > 0 && lastPrices[i] > 0, "Bad price");

  growth += (lastWeights[i] * p[i]) / lastPrices[i];

  }

  indexValue = (indexValue * growth) / 1e18;

  // 2. Chain-link adjustment at weight change (§9.3, MS §75).

  // B^- / B^+ are base-relative aggregates: the value of the old and

  // new compositions measured against the genesis fixings. D_t is

  // RECORDED and published, NOT multiplied into indexValue — under

  // the recursion the level is already continuous, and applying D_t

  // here as well would double-count the adjustment. The cumulative

  // divisor exists for the NAV-form reconciliation (§9.1, COO-17).

  uint256 bMinus = 0;

  uint256 bPlus = 0;

  bool changed = false;

  for (uint256 i = 0; i < 7; i++) {

  uint256 rel = (p[i] * 1e18) / _basePrice18(i); // P_{i,t} / P_{i,0}

  bMinus += (lastWeights[i] * rel) / 1e18;

  bPlus += (wNew[i] * rel) / 1e18;

  if (wNew[i] != lastWeights[i]) { changed = true; }

  }

  if (changed) {

  require(bPlus > 0, "Zero basket");

  uint256 newD = (bMinus * 1e18) / bPlus;

  chainLinkDivisor = (chainLinkDivisor * newD) / 1e18;

  lastWeights = wNew; // new weights govern future period returns

  emit ChainLinkAdjusted(newD, bMinus, bPlus, block.timestamp);

  }

  // 3. Commit the period end-state.

  for (uint256 i = 0; i < 7; i++) { lastPrices[i] = p[i]; }

  lastUpdate = block.timestamp;

  emit IndexUpdated(indexValue, p, lastWeights, mVersion, dVersion,
  block.timestamp);

  }

  /// @notice Returns the current chain-linked index level I_t (1e18
  scale).

  /// @dev This is the value consumed by the monetary-unit machinery of

  /// Chapter 18. It reflects the last committed updateIndex(); a keeper

  /// must call updateIndex() to roll the index forward.

  function getIndex() external view returns (uint256) {

  return indexValue;

  }

  /// @notice Value-aggregate form: NAV_t = sum_i W_{i,t} *
  P_{i,t}/P_{i,0} (§9.1).

  /// @dev Uses the committed chain weights (equal to the registry's
  live

  /// weights after the last updateIndex()). Exposed for the COO-17

  /// reconciliation: the normalization factor is G_t = I_t / NAV_t; its

  /// exact functional form is pinned during quantitative implementation

  /// (§9.3) and published (§24.3), not assumed here.

  function getNavForm() external view returns (uint256) {

  uint256[7] memory p = _fetchPrices();

  uint256 nav = 0;

  for (uint256 i = 0; i < 7; i++) {

  uint256 rel = (p[i] * 1e18) / _basePrice18(i);

  nav += (lastWeights[i] * rel) / 1e18;

  }

  return nav;

  }

  /// @notice Off-chain verification hook (adapts BP §2.6.2
  verifyGFBBase).

  /// @dev Recomputes the base basket value from the immutable genesis

  /// constants and checks the §3.4 calibration invariant: the

  /// USD-equivalent genesis notionals sum to exactly 1.0000 per unit,

  /// which is what makes I_0 = 1.0000 exact.

  function verifyGenesis() external view returns (bool) {

  uint256 recomputed =

  Q_USD + Q_EUR + Q_JPY + Q_GBP + Q_CNY + Q_CHF + Q_GOLD;

  return recomputed == INDEX_BASE_DENOMINATOR && recomputed == 1e18;

  }

  /// @dev BP §2.6.1 / §2.6.3: emit once, at the end of genesis(),
  immediately

  /// after the denominator is committed. On testnets the deployer must

  /// ensure this event lands in the logs for audit testing (Appendix
  D).

  function _emitGenesisVerification() internal {

  emit GenesisVerified(

  INDEX_BASE_DENOMINATOR,

  BASE_EUR_USD,

  BASE_GBP_USD,

  BASE_JPY_USD,

  BASE_CNY_USD,

  BASE_CHF_USD,

  BASE_GOLD_USD,

  block.timestamp

  );

  }

  /// @dev Canonical prices, 1e18-scale USD-quoted. The USD component is

  /// quoted against itself (exactly 1.0000e18): its price relative is

  /// always 1, so in USD-valuation terms the USD sleeve contributes no

  /// price return (§9.4) — only allocation and rebalancing effects.

  /// Staleness, deviation and the gold quorum are enforced upstream

  /// (Chapter 17); this call reverts when no canonical price exists.

  function _fetchPrices() internal view returns (uint256[7] memory p) {

  p[0] = 1e18; // USD identity

  for (uint256 i = 1; i < 7; i++) {

  p[i] = oracle.price(i); // canonical feed

  }

  }

  /// @dev Base fixings normalized to 1e18 (BP §2.3 constants are
  8-decimal).

  function _basePrice18(uint256 i) internal pure returns (uint256) {

  if (i == 0) return 1e18; // USD

  if (i == 1) return BASE_EUR_USD * 1e10; // EUR

  if (i == 2) return BASE_JPY_USD * 1e10; // JPY

  if (i == 3) return BASE_GBP_USD * 1e10; // GBP

  if (i == 4) return BASE_CNY_USD * 1e10; // CNH

  if (i == 5) return BASE_CHF_USD * 1e10; // CHF

  return BASE_GOLD_USD * 1e10; // XAU

  }

  }

  interface IMASEWeightRegistry {

  /// @notice Live smoothed weights plus version identifiers (Listing 2,
  §7.7).

  function getLiveWeights()

  external view returns (uint256[7] memory weights, bytes32
  methodologyVersion, bytes32 dataVersion);

  }

  interface IIndexOracle {

  /// @notice Canonical USD-quoted price of COMPONENTS[i], 1e18 scale.

  /// @dev Chapter 17: staleness, deviation and multi-source quorum
  checks

  /// (including the canonical gold price, §17.4) are enforced upstream;

  /// this call reverts when no canonical price is currently available.

  function price(uint256 componentIndex) external view returns
  (uint256);

  }

  Fidelity note: Listing 3 adapts BP §3.3.1 (GFB_BASE_DENOMINATOR →
  INDEX_BASE_DENOMINATOR, immutable, set at genesis) and BP §2.6
  (GenesisVerification → GenesisVerified with CHF and gold fixings;
  verifyGFBBase → verifyGenesis). The fixed-quantity numerator of BP's
  getGFB() [BP §2.5] is replaced by the chain-linked recursion [COO-16]
  over registry weights and canonical prices; the chain-link divisor D_t
  [MS §75] is recorded and published rather than silently applied,
  pending the COO-17 reconciliation of Section 9.3. Keepers call
  updateIndex() on the daily calculation cadence (Section 10.1);
  getIndex() returns the last committed level, which is the value
  Chapter 18 consumes.

10. MARP: The Monetary Adaptive Rebalancing Protocol

10.1 Daily Calculation vs Actual Rebalancing

  MTQ Σ calculates every day but does not necessarily trade every day.
  This distinction is constitutional [MS §35].

MARP (Monetary Adaptive Rebalancing Protocol) is Layer 3 of the
three-layer architecture (Section 2.2): it receives target weights from
MASE, compares them against the observed reserve composition, and
decides — component by component, day by day — whether a correction is
worth executing. Invariant I11 (Section 2.6) states the rule directly:
daily calculation does not imply daily trading; trading requires an
economically justified trigger under MARP rules. The calculation
pipeline therefore runs unconditionally on the daily cadence, while the
execution pipeline is gated by the no-trade zone (Section 10.4), the
urgency test (Section 10.2), the cost-benefit gate (Section 10.8) and
the execution constraints (Section 11.5).

The daily calculation set includes all of the following quantities [MS
§35]:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Quantity                Meaning                 Produced By
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  W_t^{Target}            Current MASE ensemble   Chapter 7, constrained
                          target weight for every by Chapter 8
                          component.              

  Risk_t                  Portfolio risk          Sections 5.1, 6.1–6.4
                          estimates (variance,    
                          risk contributions,     
                          drawdown state).        

  CVaR_t                  95% tail-risk estimate  Section 6.6
                          with the 99% level      
                          monitored.              

  ReserveCoverage_t       Reserve ratio and       Chapter 13
                          coverage of the MTQ Σ   
                          liability.              

  Liquidity_t             Venue depth and market  Section 11.4, Chapter
                          liquidity state for     12
                          each execution leg.     

  OracleHealth_t          Staleness, deviation    Chapter 17
                          and quorum status of    
                          the price feeds.        

  TransactionCost_t       Prevailing spread,      Section 10.8, Section
                          slippage, fee and gas   11.11
                          estimates.              
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Calc_(t) = { W_(t)^(Target), Risk_(t), CVaR_(t), ReserveCoverage_(t),
Liquidity_(t), OracleHealth_(t), TransactionCost_(t) }

No trade is required merely because the calendar changed [MS §35]. The
economic logic is asymmetric: execution costs (spread, slippage, fees,
impact, gas) are certain and immediate, while the stability benefit of a
small correction is uncertain and gradual. Separating calculation from
trading lets the protocol accumulate measurement continuity — every
daily snapshot enters the weight publication and the decision log
(Section 24.4) — without paying the market a rent for the privilege of
being observed. The distinction also decouples the two cadences that are
easiest to conflate: trading frequency is an output of the gates
(Section 10.10), while the deeper methodology review runs on its own
quarterly cadence regardless of whether a single trade occurred. A
calculated-but-untraded day is therefore not an idle day; it is a day on
which the system verified that its current position is already inside
the justified region, and that verification is itself a published,
auditable output.

10.2 Rebalancing Urgency

When a component drifts, MARP does not ask a binary question. It scores
the situation. For each asset i the engine computes a rebalancing
urgency [MS §38]:

  U_(i) = a D_(i) + b RiskDeterioration_(i) + c PPDeterioration_(i) + d
  Crisis_(i) - e TransactionCost_(i)

Trade_(i) > 0 only if U_(i) > U_(min)

Each term is a measured quantity with a defined source, so the score is
reproducible from published data:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Term                    Meaning                 Source
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  D_i                     Absolute deviation of   Section 10.4
                          component i from its    
                          execution reference     
                          weight.                 

  RiskDeterioration_i     Adverse change in the   Sections 5.1, 6.4
                          component's risk        
                          contribution and        
                          volatility state.       

  PPDeterioration_i       Purchasing-power        Sections 5.2, 6.8
                          deterioration of        
                          component i against the 
                          GPP basket.             

  Crisis_i                Crisis-score loading of Section 5.5
                          the component (stress   
                          scaling of urgency).    

  TransactionCost_i       Full estimated          Section 10.8
                          execution cost of       
                          correcting component i. 
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The coefficients a, b, c, d, e and the floor U_{min} are Risk-Council
parameters within constitutional envelopes (Section 22.4), published
with each methodology version so that any urgency score can be
recomputed by an independent auditor. The first four coefficients are
positive drivers — deviation, risk deterioration, purchasing-power
deterioration and crisis stress all raise urgency — while the negative
cost term makes the score net-of-cost: a component whose correction is
expensive must drift further, or deteriorate more, before it clears the
same bar. Urgency is continuous, and the hierarchy of Section 10.3 is
its discrete readout: low urgency keeps the component at Level 0 or
Level 1, moderate urgency authorizes a Level 2 partial correction, and
material risk or solvency pressure escalates to Level 3 or higher.

  Urgency is a ranking device, not a trade instruction. A positive U_i >
  U_{min} is necessary but not sufficient: the component must also be
  outside the no-trade zone (Section 10.4), and the sized trade must
  survive the cost-benefit gate (Section 10.8). The three tests are
  deliberately redundant — cheap, coarse filters first, expensive
  economic verification last.

10.3 The Rebalancing Hierarchy

MARP organizes every possible intervention into six levels, from pure
observation to emergency action [MS §42]. The level is selected per
component per day as the highest level whose trigger condition is met; a
Level 5 event suspends the normal ladder for the affected components
until governance stands the system down. The levels are:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Level             Name              Trigger           Response
  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- -----------------
  0                 Monitor           Deviation inside  No trade; state
                                      the no-trade      logged.
                                      zone; no other    
                                      signal active.    

  1                 Natural flow      Inflows or        Direct flows
                                      outflows can      first (Section
                                      close part of the 10.7).
                                      gap.              

  2                 Drift trigger     D_{i,t} > θ_{i,t} Partial
                                      with moderate     correction, β < 1
                                      urgency.          (Section 10.6).

  3                 Risk trigger      Material risk     Larger, staged
                                      deterioration or  correction.
                                      reserve-ratio     
                                      stress.           

  4                 Structural change Constituency or   Ramped
                                      methodology       reallocation
                                      transition.       (Section 4.6).

  5                 Emergency         Reserve, oracle,  Crisis-mode
                                      liquidity or      execution
                                      systemic          (Section 13.7).
                                      integrity event.  
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Level 0 — Monitor

Level 0 is the default state, not a failure state. The engine runs the
full daily calculation (Section 10.1), observes that D_{i,t} \leq
\theta_{i,t} with no risk, purchasing-power or crisis escalation, and
trades nothing. Every monitored day is recorded in the decision log
(Section 24.4) with the deviation, the active band and the reason code,
so the historical width of the no-trade zone is itself published
evidence about regime conditions. A basket can legitimately spend weeks
at Level 0; that persistence is the visible signature of a well-sized θ
and a stable target, not of a stalled engine.

Level 1 — Natural Flow

At Level 1 the deviation is real but the cheapest corrector is already
inside the system: incoming or outgoing flows. Minting delivers the
reference basket (Chapter 19), redemptions withdraw from it, and fee or
interest accruals land on specific components; before the engine sells
anything, it checks how much of the gap these flows will close
organically (Section 10.7). Level 1 interventions are bookkeeping
operations, not market operations — they incur no spread, no slippage
and no market impact. The engine escalates to Level 2 only for the
residual gap that flows cannot close within an acceptable horizon.

Level 2 — Drift Trigger

Level 2 fires when a component breaches its dynamic no-trade threshold
with moderate urgency. The response is a partial correction: the engine
executes a fraction \beta < 1 of the full gap (Section 10.6), sized
additionally by liquidity and daily-turnover caps (Sections 11.4 and
11.5). Partial correction is the workhorse of MARP because it prices
uncertainty honestly — if the deviation is signal, the residual will
persist and be corrected again tomorrow; if it was noise, the engine
only paid to correct part of it. Level 2 trades are the primary input to
the churn statistics that the validation program monitors (Chapter 23).

Level 3 — Risk Trigger

Level 3 responds to material risk deterioration rather than plain drift:
a component whose volatility, correlation or tail contribution is
degrading the portfolio risk profile, or a reserve ratio moving toward
its stress threshold (RR_t approaching 1.10, Section 13.3). The response
is a larger correction — \beta closer to one, possibly spanning several
components and executed in stages over multiple days under the same
liquidity caps. Level 3 escalation is driven by the same urgency score
of Section 10.2, with the risk-deterioration term carrying the dominant
weight; the declaration of Level 3 is logged with the risk evidence that
justified it.

Level 4 — Structural Change

Level 4 is reserved for structural events: a currency entering or
retiring through the Constituency Engine (Section 4.6), a methodology
version change approved through governance, or a data-source revision
that alters the published inputs (Section 24.2). Structural changes are
never executed as block trades; they enter as ramped target paths over
transition windows, and each step of the ramp must pass through the same
MARP gates as any ordinary correction — no-trade zone, urgency, sizing,
cost-benefit. This guarantees that even a deliberate architectural
decision pays only justified execution costs on the way to its new
steady state.

Level 5 — Emergency

Level 5 covers reserve, oracle, liquidity or systemic integrity events:
a reserve breach of the hard floor (RR_t at 1.00, Section 13.3), an
oracle failure or manipulation pattern (Chapter 17), a liquidity
collapse in a primary venue, or a declared systemic event. Under Level 5
the execution layer enters crisis mode (Section 13.7): no-trade
thresholds widen, execution sizes shrink, liquidity requirements rise,
reserve preservation is prioritized over tracking fidelity, oracle
confirmation requirements increase, and the direction lock may be
overridden to protect solvency (Section 11.5). Level 5 entry and exit
are governance events with full audit trails; the engine cannot silently
declare or silently exit an emergency.

10.4 The No-Trade Zone

The first and coarsest execution gate is a band around the target inside
which no trade is ever placed [MS §36]. For component i at time t,
define the deviation:

  D_(i,t) = | W_(i,t)^(Actual) - W_(i,t)^(Target) | Trade_(i) = 0 if
  D_(i,t) ≤ θ_(i,t)

This is the default [MS §36]. Trading is the exception that must justify
itself, not the norm that must excuse itself. The rule has three
properties worth making explicit. First, it is symmetric: an underweight
of 2\theta and an overweight of 2\theta are equally far from action, so
the protocol does not systematically buy or sell under noise. Second, it
is stateless per day but persistent across days: a deviation that stays
outside the band remains eligible for correction every day, so the zone
delays rather than cancels justified trades. Third, it composes with the
direction lock (Section 11.5) and the hysteresis of the Constituency
Engine (Section 4.7), giving the basket layered defenses against
oscillation at the execution, membership and weighting timescales
respectively.

The D_{i,t} defined here is the same quantity that enters the urgency
score (Section 10.2, term aD_i) and the deviation \Delta_t of the
deployed engine (Section 11.2) — one deviation measure, consumed by
every downstream gate. The threshold \theta_{i,t} is not a constant; its
dynamic construction is the subject of Section 10.5. The comparison
D_{i,t} \leq \theta_{i,t} is the contract-level check implemented as the
no-trade band in Listing 4 (Section 11.11), which returns before any
sizing, quoting or cost estimation is performed.

10.5 Dynamic No-Trade Thresholds

A fixed band would be wrong twice: too tight in expensive markets
(churning small deviations at high cost) and too loose in cheap markets
(letting tracking error accumulate needlessly). The band is therefore
dynamic, a function of the component's volatility and the prevailing
cost of trading it [MS §37, COO-15]:

  θ_(i,t) = θ_(base) + f(Volatility_(i), Liquidity_(i), Spread_(i),
  Slippage_(i))

  This is one of the strongest parts of the design and should be
  mathematically explicit [COO-15].

The functional form f is a governance-published, methodology-versioned
map. The validated instantiation is linear in normalized inputs, which
keeps the band interpretable and its sensitivity auditable:

θ_(i,t) = θ_(base) + κ₁ widetildeσ_(i) + κ₂ (1 -
widetildeLiquidity_(i)) + κ₃ widetildeSpread_(i) + κ₄
widetildeSlippage_(i)

where each tilde-marked input is normalized to the [0,1] range against
its trailing cross-sectional distribution (the same normalization
discipline as Section 4.5), and the \kappa coefficients are Risk-Council
parameters within constitutional envelopes. Every term moves the band in
the economically correct direction: higher volatility widens the band
because deviations are more likely to be noise; thinner liquidity, wider
spreads and higher expected slippage widen it because correcting the
deviation is more expensive. In compact form: the system becomes less
aggressive when trading becomes expensive [MS §37].

The band is component-specific by construction — the gold leg and a
small-currency leg carry different spreads, different pool depths and
different slippage curves, so their \theta_{i,t} differ even when their
deviations are identical. Under an elevated CrisisScore_t (Section 5.5)
the band is widened by the crisis-mode rules of Section 11.6, which in
Listing 4 doubles the base band when the crisis oracle exceeds its
threshold. \theta_{base}, the \kappa vector and the crisis widening
factor are all published parameters (Section 22.4); a worked numerical
example of band construction and a resulting skip decision is traced
step by step in Appendix C.

10.6 Partial Rebalancing

When the gates clear and a trade is triggered, MARP still does not jump
to the target. The executed correction is a fraction of the full gap [MS
§39]:

  Trade_(i) = β_(i,t) ( W_(i,t)^(Target) - W_(i,t)^(Actual) ), 0 <
  β_(i,t) ≤ 1

Normal conditions should generally use \beta < 1, while extreme reserve
or risk events may permit larger corrections [MS §39]. The
partial-fraction logic prices uncertainty the same way the no-trade zone
prices noise: if the deviation is persistent signal, tomorrow's
evaluation will correct the residual; if it was transient, the protocol
paid to correct only a fraction of a mirage. Partial execution also
softens market impact — the executed quantity \beta (Target - Actual) is
smaller, so its slippage footprint is smaller and the liquidity cap of
Section 11.4 binds later.

The correction fraction is itself state-dependent. The validated
instantiation takes the form:

β_(i,t) = clip( β₀ + β₁ U_(i) - β₂ widehatImpact_(i), β_(min), 1 )

so urgency raises the fraction and estimated market impact lowers it,
with \beta_{min} a constitutional floor that prevents corrections from
becoming so partial they never converge. When even a \beta-sized
correction exceeds the liquidity cap, the engine stages it across days
rather than accelerating it: the residual re-enters tomorrow's
evaluation, the direction lock still applies, and the full sequence is
visible in the decision log (Section 24.4). Level 3 risk triggers and
Level 5 emergencies are the sanctioned paths to \beta close to one, and
each such escalation is logged with its justification.

10.7 Natural Cash-Flow Rebalancing

The cheapest rebalance is the one the system performs with money that is
already moving. Before selling an asset, MARP uses incoming flows [MS
§40]. Let CF_{i,t} be the net natural cash flow into component i —
minting inflows delivered in basket proportions, redemption outflows
drawn from the reserve, fee accruals, and interest or yield on held
instruments (Chapter 19). The engine computes:

TradeNeeded_(i) = TargetGap_(i) - CF_(i,t)

Trade_(i) = f(TradeNeeded_(i))

and only executes the remaining amount. If the day's natural flows fully
cover the gap, the market trade is zero even though the gap was nonzero
— this is Level 1 of the hierarchy, and it minimizes unnecessary market
turnover [MS §40]. Every natural-flow netting decision is recorded with
its components, so an auditor can verify that CF_{i,t} was measured, not
assumed.

The practical consequence is that MARP treats the mint and redeem
machinery of Chapter 19 as the first-priority rebalancing instrument.
Minting delivers the reference basket, so sustained net minting pulls
the observed composition toward the target composition without a single
DEX trade; redemptions, priced against NAV (Section 19.3), can be
settled from overweight components first where the redemption queue
allows. The market-execution path of Sections 10.8 and 11.4 exists for
the residual that flows cannot close in an acceptable horizon — the
design deliberately routes the bulk of ordinary corrections through
bookkeeping rather than through the market.

10.8 The Cost-Benefit Gate

The final gate before any trade is economic: the expected stability
benefit of the correction must exceed its expected execution cost by a
margin. For component i, the expected execution cost is [MS §41,
COO-14]:

  Cost_(i) = Spread_(i) + Slippage_(i) + Fees_(i) + Impact_(i) +
  OperationalCost_(i)

  NetBenefit_(i) = Benefit_(i) - Cost_(i) execute only when
  NetBenefit_(i) > ε

Each cost term is a measured or estimated quantity with an owner:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Cost Term               Meaning                 Estimated From
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Spread_i                Bid-ask cost of the     Venue quotes, rolling
                          execution venue for     spreads (Chapter 12)
                          component i.            

  Slippage_i              Expected price movement Pool-depth and slippage
                          caused by the trade     curves (Section 11.4)
                          itself.                 

  Fees_i                  Aggregator/router fees  Listing 4 router fee;
                          and protocol fees on    Chapter 19 fee schedule
                          the leg.                

  Impact_i                Expected market impact  Participation-rate
                          beyond immediate        models (Chapter 12)
                          slippage.               

  OperationalCost_i       Gas, keeper             Dynamic gas estimator
                          infrastructure and      (Section 11.11)
                          monitoring cost.        
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The benefit B_i is the expected stability benefit of the correction: the
risk reduction, purchasing-power improvement and diversification
improvement of moving toward the target, valued in the common
USD-equivalent units of the objective function (Section 10.9). The
margin \epsilon is a predefined economic margin [MS §41] — a positive
buffer that absorbs estimation error in both B_i and Cost_i, so the gate
never trades on an advantage smaller than the noise in its own
measurement. \epsilon is a Risk-Council parameter within a
constitutional floor and is published with each methodology version.

  That turns the cost-aware claim into an actual mathematical rule
  [COO-14].

The gate runs after the no-trade zone (cheap comparison), after the
urgency test (Section 10.2) and after sizing (Section 11.4), because
cost estimation is the most expensive step and should only price trades
that already survived the coarse filters. In Listing 4 (Section 11.11)
the gate is the condition benefit > costTerm + BENEFIT_MARGIN, with the
dynamic gas-plus-slippage-plus-fee estimator supplying Cost_i at
execution time; a failed gate emits RebalanceSkipped with a
machine-readable reason, so every declined trade contributes to the same
audit trail as an executed one (Section 11.7). Appendix C works a full
numerical example of a trade that passes the no-trade zone, sizes
correctly, and is then declined by the margin.

10.9 The Rebalancing Objective

The MARP decision problem has a single sentence at its center: a trade
is desirable when the net benefit is positive and sufficiently large [MS
§66]. Formally:

  NetBenefit = RiskReduction + PPImprovement +
  DiversificationImprovement - ExecutionCost

The blueprint's Rebalancing Engine supplies the implementable form of
the same idea: instead of a fixed threshold (for example the v1.2 2.25%
gold band), the engine evaluates a composite cost function J(\Delta W)
that balances four objectives [BP §7.3]:

  J(Δ W) = λ₁ (W^(obs) - W^(Target) - Δ W)² + λ₂ (Spread · Δ W +
  FixedGas) + λ₃ Urgency + λ₄ |Δ W|

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Term                    Formula                 Purpose
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Deviation^2             (W^{obs} - W^{Target} - Measures how much
                          Δ W)^2                  deviation remains after
                                                  the trade.

  Cost                    Spread × Δ W + FixedGas Execution cost
                                                  (slippage + gas).

  Urgency                 1/RR_t if RR_t < 1.10,  Penalises deviation
                          else 0                  when the reserve ratio
                                                  is low.

  Turnover                |Δ W|                   Penalises excessive
                                                  trading volume.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
