  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Symbol                  Value                   Description
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  λ_1                     0.40                    Prioritises accuracy
                                                  (reduce deviation).

  λ_2                     0.30                    Prioritises cost
                                                  efficiency.

  λ_3                     0.20                    Prioritises solvency
                                                  during stress.

  λ_4                     0.10                    Penalises excessive
                                                  turnover.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The engine searches for the optimal trade size \Delta W^* that minimizes
J(\Delta W):

Δ W^(*) = argmin_(Δ W) J(Δ W)

The two formulations are the same doctrine at two levels of precision.
The MS net-benefit identity is the sign condition — trade when the
weighted benefit terms dominate execution cost by a margin; the J
functional is the decision machinery that also selects the size, because
its deviation term rewards completing the correction while its cost,
urgency and turnover terms restrain it. The mapping is direct:
RiskReduction corresponds to the deviation reduction captured by the
\lambda_1 term (with risk measured as in Chapter 6), PPImprovement and
DiversificationImprovement are embedded in the MASE target the deviation
is measured against (Sections 6.8 and 6.5), and ExecutionCost is the
\lambda_2 term estimated at execution time. The trigger form of the same
condition — the marginal-benefit inequality — is carried in Section
11.3.

  Units and validation. The four J terms are heterogeneous
  (weight-squared, USD, ratio, weight), so the deployed contract
  evaluates them in a common fixed-point normalization (1e18 scale,
  Listing 4); the normalization weights are exactly the \lambda
  coefficients and are therefore published and perturbation-tested
  (Section 23.6). The 0.40/0.30/0.20/0.10 split is the blueprint's
  governance-controlled default [BP §7.3], reproduced in Listing 4 as
  LAMBDA_1 through LAMBDA_4 and adjustable only by the Risk Council
  (Section 11.10).

10.10 Strategic Review Cadence and Dynamic Frequency

Independently of trading, the methodology itself is reviewed on a fixed
cadence [MS §43]:

Review_(t) = Quarterly

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Review Scope                        Content
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  Structural data                     COFER, BIS and institutional-source
                                      refreshes and revisions (Section
                                      4.3).

  Eligibility                         Re-testing of Q_i scores,
                                      entry/exit candidates and
                                      hysteresis bands (Chapter 4).

  Parameters                          All Risk-Council parameters against
                                      their envelopes and validation
                                      evidence (Section 22.4).

  Methodology                         Model families, ensemble weights,
                                      objective coefficients and
                                      validation results (Chapter 23).
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The review does not force a trade [MS §43]. A quarterly review can
conclude that nothing should change; it can also produce a new
methodology version, in which case the change enters as a Level 4
structural event with ramped transitions (Sections 4.6 and 10.3) — never
as an immediate block reallocation. The review cadence is also the
natural evaluation cadence of the Constituency Engine (Section 4.1),
which is why both default to quarterly.

The review cadence must not be confused with a rebalancing frequency.
There is no fixed statement 'rebalance daily' and no fixed statement
'rebalance quarterly' [MS §67]. Instead:

  Frequency_(t) = f(Drift, Risk, Liquidity, Cost, StructuralChange)

This is event-driven dynamic rebalancing [MS §67]: the realized trading
frequency is an output of the gates, not an input to them. In calm,
liquid, cheap markets the dynamic band narrows and corrections occur
more often at small size; in stressed, illiquid or expensive conditions
the band widens, urgency's cost term rises, and the system trades less
while calculating just as much. The evaluation cadence itself adapts to
data quality: when oracle staleness or feed degradation is flagged
(Chapter 17), the engine pauses execution rather than trading on
degraded inputs, and the operational state machine (Section 18.5)
records the pause — frequency therefore falls exactly when measurement
quality falls, which is the conservative direction.

11. Execution Mechanics: Deviations, Triggers and the MARP Contract

11.1 Observed and Target Weights

  MARP's comparison state is a pair: what the reserve actually holds,
  and what the MASE registry says it should hold. Everything downstream
  — deviation, trigger, sizing, gate — is arithmetic on this pair [BP
  §7.2.1–7.2.2, adapted].

The engine first computes the current observed weight of each component
in the reserve [BP §7.2.1]:

  W_(i,t)^(obs) = (V_(i,t))/(V_(net,t)) W_(gold,t)^(obs) =
  (V_(gold,t))/(V_(net,t))

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Variable                Meaning                 Source
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  V_{gold,t}              USD value of all gold   Reserve accounting
                          holdings (PAXG, etc.)   (Chapters 13–14)
                          in the vault.           

  V_{i,t}                 USD value of component  Section 2.5; Chapter 14
                          i holdings after        
                          haircuts.               

  V_{net,t}               Total Net Asset Value.  V_{net,t} = ∑_j Q_{j,t}
                                                  P_{j,t} (1 - H_j)
                                                  (Section 2.5)
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

On the target side, the historical engine retrieved the smoothed target
gold weight from the Adaptive Macro Engine via getTargetGoldWeight() (BP
§6.7), with the fiat bloc defined residually as W_{fiat,t}^{target} =
1 - W_{gold,t}^{target}. Under v2.0 that source is retired: target
weights are read from the MASE weight registry (Listing 2, Section 7.7),
which stores the live smoothed vector, the accepted MASE target and the
governing methodology and data versions. The registry's getLiveWeights()
accessor returns the live (smoothed) weight vector, and XAU occupies
component index 6 in the fixed COMPONENTS order.

W^(Target) ← MASEWeightRegistry.getLiveWeights() W_(fiat,t)^(target) =
1 - W_(gold,t)^(target)

  Weight-state reconciliation. The four published weight states (Section
  2.3) are W^{Prior}, W^{Target}, W^{Smooth} and W^{Execution}. The
  registry's live vector is W^{Smooth} — the MASE target after
  stress-adaptive smoothing (Section 8.4) — and it is this state that
  plays the role of W^{Target} in the deviation formulas of Sections
  10.4–10.6, because it is the constitutionally admissible target the
  executor is bound to track. W^{Execution} is the post-decision state:
  the observed weight after the gated, partially corrected trade has
  actually been applied. Per-currency targets for the six fiat
  components are read from registry indices 0–5; the deployed engine
  instantiates the gold leg and the fiat bloc, which is the
  highest-impact execution venue, while the per-component generalization
  runs the same control flow with component-specific pools and bands.

11.2 The Deviation

The signed difference between observed and target is the deviation [BP
§7.2.3]:

  Δ_(t) = W_(gold,t)^(obs) - W_(gold,t)^(Target) D_(gold,t) = |Δ_(t)|

The sign carries the direction of the required correction:

-   If \Delta_t > 0, the vault holds excess gold: the correction sells
    gold and buys fiat.

-   If \Delta_t < 0, the vault holds deficient gold: the correction
    sells fiat and buys gold.

The unsigned magnitude D_{gold,t} is exactly the D_{i,t} of Section
10.4: the no-trade zone compares it against the dynamic band
\theta_{i,t}, the urgency score consumes it as its leading term, and the
J functional of Section 10.9 penalizes its post-trade residual. One
deviation measure feeds every gate, which is what makes the pipeline
auditable end to end — a reviewer can follow a single number from
observation through band comparison, urgency, sizing, cost gate and
final event emission, and reproduce every intermediate value from
published data (Section 24.4). The deviation is recomputed at every
evaluation from the current registry target, so a target change between
evaluations re-bases it immediately rather than being discovered late
[BP §7.7]. Deviations are also persistent state in the transparency
layer: the daily published D_{i,t} series is the primary input to the
tracking-error statistics that the validation program monitors (Chapter
23), and a persistent one-signed deviation outside the band is the
canonical early symptom of a blocked execution path — which is precisely
the condition the skip-reason stream of Section 11.7 is designed to
surface.

11.3 Dynamic Trigger Conditions

The engine executes a rebalance if and only if the marginal benefit of
reducing deviation exceeds the marginal cost [BP §7.4]:

  (∂ J)/(∂ Δ W) > ExecutionCost_(estimated)

ExecutionCost = GasPrice × GasUnits + Slippage(|Δ W|)

In practice, the engine simulates the trade at the current oracle price,
applies the pool-specific slippage curve, and computes the net benefit;
if the net benefit is positive — the reduction in deviation is worth the
gas and slippage — the trade is executed [BP §7.4]. This is the marginal
form of the same inequality that the cost-benefit gate of Section 10.8
enforces in level form, and both are instantiations of the MS
net-benefit doctrine (Section 10.9).

Under v2.0 the trigger condition is composed with the six-level
hierarchy of Section 10.3, so the single BP inequality becomes a
level-indexed decision:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Hierarchy Level                     Trigger Condition (all required at
                                      this level)
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  0 — Monitor                         D_{i,t} ≤ θ_{i,t} and U_i ≤
                                      U_{min}: no trade.

  1 — Natural flow                    TargetGap_i > 0 with CF_{i,t} > 0
                                      available: net flows first,
                                      residual deferred.

  2 — Drift trigger                   D_{i,t} > θ_{i,t}, U_i > U_{min},
                                      and ∂ J/∂ Δ W >
                                      ExecutionCost_{estimated}.

  3 — Risk trigger                    Level 2 conditions plus material
                                      risk deterioration or RR_t < 1.10
                                      urgency active.

  4 — Structural change               Approved transition ramp active
                                      (Section 4.6); each ramp step must
                                      still pass Level 2 tests.

  5 — Emergency                       Crisis-mode override (Section
                                      13.7): gates widened or overridden
                                      under governance logging.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The composition rule is strictest-first: a Level 4 ramp step that fails
the Level 2 cost test is deferred, not forced, and only Level 5
emergency declarations can bypass the economic gates — and those
declarations are themselves governed events with full audit trails
(Section 22.4). The trigger evaluation is recomputed at every rebalance
evaluation; if the registry target has changed since the last
evaluation, the engine adapts immediately, so trigger conditions never
reference a stale target [BP §7.7].

11.4 Trade Sizing — Liquidity-Sensitive

To avoid moving the market against itself, the trade size is dynamically
capped by the available pool depth [BP §7.5]:

  TradeSize_(max) = min ( |Δ_(t)| × V_(net,t), 0.10 × PoolDepth_(24h) )

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Quantity                            Meaning
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  |Δ_t| × V_{net,t}                   The absolute surplus value to
                                      rebalance, in USD.

  PoolDepth_{24h}                     The 24-hour rolling average
                                      liquidity of the primary DEX pool
                                      (for example PAXG/USDC).

  0.10 cap                            The protocol never consumes more
                                      than 10% of the pool's daily volume
                                      in a single trade.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The 10% cap is what prevents excessive slippage: participation above
that fraction of rolling depth begins to move the pool price against the
protocol's own fill, degrading the very execution the trade was meant to
improve. If the required rebalance exceeds the cap, the engine executes
multiple staged trades over several days, spreading the execution cost
[BP §7.5] — which is the staged-execution path of Section 10.6, not an
override of it. Sizing composes with the partial-correction fraction:
the candidate size is \beta (W^{Target} - W^{Actual}) scaled to USD,
then min-capped by pool depth, then further capped by the daily turnover
limit of Section 11.5.2, and the residual re-enters tomorrow's
evaluation. Execution scheduling beyond the daily granularity —
time-slicing within a day, venue split across aggregators, and
private-mempool submission — is specified in Chapter 12.

11.5 Execution Constraints and the Direction Lock

Three hard constraints govern every executed trade: a both-sided
slippage guard, a maximum daily turnover, and the direction lock. The
direction lock is the anti-oscillation centerpiece of the execution
layer and is referenced from the hysteresis discussion of Section 4.7;
all three are enforced inside Listing 4 (Section 11.11).

11.5.1 Slippage Guard

All trades are executed via a DEX aggregator (1inch, Paraswap) with
both-sided slippage protection [BP §7.6.1]. The guard compares the
executed rate against the canonical oracle rate (Chapter 17):

ExecRate ≥ OracleRate × (1 - τ) (selling gold)

ExecRate ≤ OracleRate × (1 + τ) (buying gold)

with \tau = 0.01 (1%) by default, adjustable by the Risk Council.
Selling gold must not fill below 1% under the canonical price, and
buying gold must not fill above 1% over it; a violation reverts the
trade rather than filling at the degraded price. The guard is two-sided
because the protocol is a repeat participant in both directions —
protecting only the sell side would leave the buy side exposed to
exactly the extraction the guard exists to prevent. The oracle side of
the comparison is the multi-source canonical gold price (Chapter 17),
never an AMM spot price, so the guard inherits the independent-source
quorum and staleness checks of the oracle layer.

11.5.2 Maximum Daily Turnover

To prevent excessive trading, the engine enforces a maximum daily
turnover of 5% of the total reserve value [BP §7.6.2]. If the required
rebalance exceeds this, the remaining amount is deferred to the next
day. The cap binds on the sum of executed trade values per UTC day,
tracked on-chain by the dailyTurnover counter of Listing 4 and reset at
rollover with a DailyTurnoverReset event. The turnover cap is a
system-level brake that composes with the per-trade liquidity cap of
Section 11.4: the pool-depth cap protects a single venue fill, while the
daily cap protects the protocol's aggregate market footprint and
guarantees that no single day of authorized corrections can consume more
than a bounded fraction of the reserve's liquidity.

11.5.3 Direction Lock (Whipsaw Guard)

To prevent oscillation — selling gold one day and buying it back the
next — the engine implements a 24-hour direction lock [BP §7.6.3]:

-   If the engine sold gold in the last 24 hours, it cannot sell gold
    again (but it can buy gold if the deviation reverses direction).

-   If the engine bought gold in the last 24 hours, it cannot buy gold
    again (but it can sell gold).

The lock blocks repetition of the same direction, not reaction to a
genuine reversal: if the deviation flips sign within the window, the
opposite-direction trade remains available, so the protocol is never
trapped away from its target. The lock exists because whipsaw is the
purest form of execution waste — spread, slippage, fees and gas paid
twice for zero net correction, with the second trade partially undoing
the first. It composes with the no-trade zone (Section 10.4) and the
Constituency Engine's hysteresis (Section 4.7) as the
execution-timescale member of the layered anti-oscillation defenses.

  Exception. The direction lock is overridden if the reserve ratio drops
  below 1.05 (Stress Mode) [BP §7.6.3]. In that case the engine may
  reverse direction regardless of the lock to protect solvency —
  solvency outranks anti-churn. The override condition is mechanical
  (RR_t < 1.05, Section 13.3), not discretionary, and every override
  execution is emitted with its deviation and cost so the exception is
  auditable (Section 11.7).

11.6 Integration with Regime and Crisis Signals

The rebalancing engine consumes the smoothed target weights as its
primary reference and recalculates the target at every evaluation — if
the registry changed since the last evaluation, the engine adapts
immediately [BP §7.7]. The v2.0 flow is:

1.  The MASE weight registry (Listing 2, Section 7.7) returns the live
    smoothed vector (for example, gold at 27.1%).

2.  The engine computes \Delta_t = W^{obs} - W^{Target} per component
    (Section 11.2).

3.  It evaluates J(\Delta W), the no-trade zone, and the trigger
    conditions (Sections 11.3, 10.9).

4.  A gated trade is sized (Section 11.4) and executed through the DEX
    aggregator under the constraints of Section 11.5.

On top of the target reference, execution parameters are modulated by
the crisis-regime state. CrisisScore_t (Section 5.5) is the aggregation
of stress evidence, and when it crosses its threshold the execution
layer enters crisis mode (Section 13.7), which modulates exactly the
parameters this chapter defines:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Execution Parameter     Normal Mode             Crisis Mode (Section
                                                  13.7)
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  No-trade band θ_{i,t}   Base dynamic band       Widened (Listing 4
                          (Section 10.5)          doubles the base band)

  Trade size              Liquidity-capped β      Shrunk toward smaller,
                          correction              staged executions

  Liquidity requirement   10% of 24h pool depth   Raised; deeper-venue
                                                  requirements

  Direction lock          24-hour lock enforced   Overridable when RR_t <
                                                  1.05 (Stress Mode)

  Oracle confirmation     Standard quorum         Increased confirmation
                          (Chapter 17)            requirements

  Objective priority      Balanced J terms        Reserve preservation
                                                  prioritized over
                                                  tracking fidelity
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The modulation is deliberately one-directional: crisis conditions only
ever make execution more conservative — wider bands, smaller sizes,
stricter confirmations — because the failure mode being defended against
is trading aggressively into a dislocated market. Reserve-ratio stress
(RR_t below 1.10, with 1.05 as the stress floor and 1.00 as the hard
floor, Section 13.3) feeds the urgency term of the J functional
directly, so solvency pressure raises the value of correcting deviations
precisely when solvency is at risk. All mode transitions are recorded in
the operational state machine (Section 18.5) and the decision log
(Section 24.4).

11.7 Event Emissions and Decision Logging

Every MARP decision — executed or declined — emits a structured event
[BP §7.9]. The event set of Listing 4 is:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Event                   Payload                 Meaning
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  RebalanceExecuted       timestamp, deviation,   A gated trade was
                          amount, estimatedCost   executed with its
                                                  pre-trade deviation and
                                                  cost estimate.

  RebalanceSkipped        timestamp, deviation,   A candidate trade was
                          reason                  declined, with a
                                                  machine-readable reason
                                                  string.

  DailyTurnoverReset      timestamp,              The daily turnover
                          previousTurnover        counter rolled over,
                                                  publishing the prior
                                                  day's total.

  GoldSold / GoldBought   amount, output,         DEX leg fill details
                          execPrice               for the gold execution
                                                  path.

  SlippageWarning         tradeType, oraclePrice, An executed or quoted
                          execPrice, timestamp    price approached the
                                                  slippage-guard boundary
                                                  (Section 11.5.1).
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The skip reasons are enumerable by design: 'No-trade zone' (Section
10.4), 'Direction lock' (Section 11.5.3), 'Net benefit not positive'
(Section 10.8) and the turnover/liquidity truncations. A skipped trade
is as informative as an executed one — the stream of skips with reasons
is the direct evidence that the gates work, and it is what allows an
auditor to reconstruct, for any historical day, why the protocol did not
trade (Section 24.4). Events feed the decision log with the same
versioned data discipline as the weight publications (Section 24.2):
each entry binds the deviation, the decision, the governing registry
state (methodology and data versions) and the realized cost, so the full
pipeline from W^{Target} to W^{Execution} is reproducible after the
fact.

11.8 Summary of Developer-Facing Constants

The complete developer-facing constant set of the execution layer [BP
§7.10], extended with the v2.0 gate constants:

  -------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Parameter         Symbol            Value              Governance
  ----------------- ----------------- ------------------ ------------------
  Lambda 1          λ_1               0.40               Risk Council (4/7)
  (Deviation)                                            

  Lambda 2 (Cost)   λ_2               0.30               Risk Council (4/7)

  Lambda 3          λ_3               0.20               Risk Council (4/7)
  (Urgency)                                              

  Lambda 4          λ_4               0.10               Risk Council (4/7)
  (Turnover)                                             

  Slippage          τ                 1%                 Risk Council (4/7)
  Tolerance                                              

  Gas Fallback      GAS_FALLBACK      $50                Constitutional
                                                         (immutable)

  Max Daily         —                 5% of NAV          Risk Council (4/7)
  Turnover                                               

  Max Pool Fraction —                 10% of 24h volume  Risk Council (4/7)

  Direction Lock    —                 24 hours           Immutable (Risk
                                                         Council can
                                                         override in
                                                         stress)

  No-Trade Band     θ_{base}          0.75% base; 1.5%   Risk Council
  (v2.0)                              stress             (4/7),
                                                         validation-stage

  Benefit Margin    ε                 1%                 Risk Council
  (v2.0)                              (BENEFIT_MARGIN)   (4/7),
                                                         validation-stage
  -------------------------------------------------------------------------

-   The Gas Fallback constant is only used when block.basefee is
    unavailable (for example on certain L2 testnets). On mainnet, the
    protocol uses the live block.basefee multiplied by a 20% buffer.

-   The Direction Lock prevents the rebalancing engine from reversing
    direction within the same 24-hour period. It can be overridden when
    the Reserve Ratio falls below 1.05 (Stress Mode) [BP §7.10].

-   The v2.0 no-trade band and benefit margin values are
    validation-stage defaults: they enter the perturbation study of
    Section 23.6 before any production authorization, and the published
    methodology version fixes the operative values.

11.9 Design of the On-Chain Execution Layer

MARP follows the same off-chain/on-chain split as the MASE registry
(Listing 2's note, Section 7.7): computation that is iterative or
data-heavy happens off-chain from published data, and the contract
verifies and enforces. The optimization layer — the full J evaluation
across candidate sizes, the regime and crisis classification, the
slippage-curve estimation against live pool state — is computed by the
execution keeper from published inputs, while the on-chain engine
independently re-verifies every constitutional and economic bound at
execution time: the no-trade band, the direction lock, the liquidity and
turnover caps, the slippage guard against the canonical oracle price,
and the cost-benefit margin. A trade that passes off-chain analysis but
fails any on-chain check simply does not execute.

The keeper path is intentionally simple: a permissioned keeper bot (or,
under the testnet deployment, any authorized operator) calls
executeRebalance(), which re-derives the decision from current on-chain
state rather than trusting a submitted decision. This is stronger than a
submit-and-verify pattern because there is nothing to submit — the
trigger math that runs on-chain is coarse but self-contained, so an
untrusted keeper cannot smuggle in an unjustified trade; it can only
propose an evaluation. The keeper's economic incentive (execution fees,
if enabled) is governed with the same parameter authority as the
execution constants (Section 22.4).

Verification duties are distributed. The keeper is responsible for
calling the evaluation and for gas-paid execution; the contract is
responsible for enforcing every gate and emitting the decision events;
independent verifiers — and ultimately the transparency layer of Chapter
24 — are responsible for checking that each emitted execution satisfies
its gates, that the deviation series is consistent with the published
registry states, and that the realized costs match the estimation model.
Verification failures feed the state machine's pause conditions (Section
18.5): repeated gate reverts, oracle staleness beyond tolerance (Chapter
17) or reserve-ratio stress (Section 13.3) halt execution until
governance resolves the condition, and the halt is itself a logged
state.

11.10 Governance of Execution Parameters

Execution parameters determine how the protocol trades, never what it
holds: no governance body may set an individual live weight by
discretion (Invariant I8, Section 2.6), and the execution layer's
authority is confined to thresholds, coefficients and constraints. The
authority split follows the layered governance hierarchy (Section 22.4):

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Parameter Group         Authority               Change Path
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  λ_1–λ_4 objective       Risk Council (4/7)      Within constitutional
  coefficients                                    envelopes; published
                                                  with methodology
                                                  version;
                                                  perturbation-tested
                                                  (Section 23.6)

  Slippage tolerance τ;   Risk Council (4/7)      24h timelock;
  turnover cap; pool                              event-emitted change
  fraction                                        

  No-trade band θ_{base}  Risk Council (4/7)      Within constitutional
  and crisis widening;                            floors; versioned
  margin ε; U_{min};                              publication
  urgency coefficients                            

  Direction lock          Constitutional          Immutable in normal
  duration; GAS_FALLBACK                          operation (BP §7.10)

  Direction-lock stress   Mechanical,             Automatic under the
  override (RR_t < 1.05)  pre-authorized          declared stress
                                                  condition; audited post
                                                  hoc

  Gas oracle; execution   Risk Council (4/7)      Governed setters in
  venues and assets                               Listing 4; venues
                                                  resolved via the Asset
                                                  Admission Registry
                                                  (Chapter 15)
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The constitutional items are deliberately the ones whose violation would
create systematic extractable value: a direction lock that could be
waived at will would let an insider frontrun the protocol's own whipsaw,
and a mutable gas fallback would corrupt the cost side of the
cost-benefit gate. Everything else is Risk-Council territory because it
is calibration, not constitution. All parameter values, current and
historical, are part of the parameter registry (Section 22.3), and every
change to an execution parameter is emitted, versioned and reproducible
— the same discipline the weight publications follow (Section 24.2).

11.11 Smart Contract Implementation — MARP Execution Engine

The contract below is the complete execution engine announced in Section
2.7, carrying the blueprint's rebalancing engine substantially intact —
objective coefficients, dynamic gas estimation with the three-tier
Chainlink/basefee/fallback path, direction-sensitive slippage estimates,
both-sided slippage guards, turnover and pool-depth caps, the 24-hour
direction lock with its stress override, and the DEX integration with
quote validation and deadline enforcement [BP §7.8]. The v2.0
adaptations are: target weights are read from the MASE weight registry
(Listing 2, Section 7.7) instead of the retired macro engine; the
no-trade zone and the cost-benefit margin are checked before any trade
is executed; the crisis oracle widens the band under stress; and venue,
asset and module references resolve through governed configuration
rather than hard-coded addresses. The fidelity note after the listing
records every deviation from the blueprint text.

Listing 4 — MARP rebalancing engine: triggers, sizing, constraints and
DEX execution (adapts BP §7.8)

  // SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT

  pragma solidity ^0.8.19;

  contract MTQSigmaMARP {

  // ============================================================

  // 11.11.1 CONSTANTS & STATE (carries BP §7.8.1)

  // ============================================================

  // ---- Objective function coefficients (1e18 scale) ----

  uint256 public constant LAMBDA_1 = 0.40e18; // Deviation priority

  uint256 public constant LAMBDA_2 = 0.30e18; // Cost priority

  uint256 public constant LAMBDA_3 = 0.20e18; // Urgency priority

  uint256 public constant LAMBDA_4 = 0.10e18; // Turnover penalty

  // ---- Slippage tolerance (base, adjustable by Risk Council) ----

  uint256 public slippageTolerance = 0.01e18; // 1% (default)

  // ---- Daily turnover cap (5% of NAV) ----

  uint256 public constant MAX_DAILY_TURNOVER = 0.05e18; // 5%

  // ---- Max trade size as fraction of pool depth ----

  uint256 public constant MAX_POOL_FRACTION = 0.10e18; // 10%

  // ---- Direction lock duration ----

  uint256 public constant DIRECTION_LOCK_DURATION = 24 hours;

  // ---- Gas fallback (used if block.basefee is zero and no oracle)
  ----

  uint256 public constant GAS_FALLBACK = 50e18; // $50 USD equivalent

  // ---- Gas unit estimate (simplified; adjust per chain) ----

  uint256 public constant GAS_UNITS_ESTIMATE = 150000; // 150k gas

  // ---- Slippage estimates (basis points, 1 bp = 0.01%) ----

  // Conservative defaults; Risk Council can override.

  uint256 public constant SLIPPAGE_BPS_SELL = 15; // 0.15%

  uint256 public constant SLIPPAGE_BPS_BUY = 20; // 0.20%

  // ---- No-trade zone (§10.4-§10.5): dynamic band, 1e18 weight scale
  ----

  uint256 public noTradeBand = 0.0075e18; // 0.75% base
  (validation-stage)

  uint256 public noTradeBandStress = 0.015e18; // widened under crisis
  (§11.6)

  // ---- Cost-benefit gate margin (§10.8): NetBenefit must exceed
  epsilon ----

  uint256 public constant BENEFIT_MARGIN = 0.01e18; // 1%
  (validation-stage)

  // ---- Gas Oracle (Chainlink, set by Governance) ----

  address public gasOracle; // Chainlink gas price feed
  (AggregatorV3Interface)

  // ---- Weight registry (Listing 2, §7.7) and crisis oracle (§5.5)
  ----

  IMASEWeightRegistry public weightRegistry;

  address public crisisOracle;

  uint256 public crisisThreshold = 0.70e18;

  // ---- Execution venues and assets (Asset Admission Registry, Ch. 15)
  ----

  address public PAXG;

  address public USDC;

  address public PAXG_USDC_POOL;

  address public poolDepthOracle;

  address public goldOracle; // §17 canonical gold price feed

  IReserveState public reserveModule; // v1.2 §4.4.2 -> Chapters 13-14

  // ---- Last trade metadata ----

  struct LastTrade {

  uint256 timestamp;

  int256 direction; // +1 for buy, -1 for sell, 0 for none

  }

  LastTrade public lastTrade;

  // ---- Daily turnover tracking ----

  uint256 public dailyTurnover; // USD value traded today

  uint256 public lastTurnoverReset;

  // ---- DEX Aggregator address (immutable, governed) ----

  address public immutable AGGREGATOR;

  address public riskCouncil; // 4/7 Multi-Sig (placeholder check, BP
  §7.8.4)

  // ---- Constructor ----

  constructor(address _aggregator, address _weightRegistry, address
  _riskCouncil) {

  AGGREGATOR = _aggregator;

  weightRegistry = IMASEWeightRegistry(_weightRegistry);

  riskCouncil = _riskCouncil;

  }

  // ---- Events ----

  event GasOracleUpdated(address indexed oldOracle, address indexed
  newOracle);

  event RebalanceExecuted(uint256 indexed timestamp, int256 deviation,
  uint256 amount, uint256 estimatedCost);

  event RebalanceSkipped(uint256 indexed timestamp, int256 deviation,
  string reason);

  event DailyTurnoverReset(uint256 indexed timestamp, uint256
  previousTurnover);

  event GoldSold(uint256 amount, uint256 output, uint256 execPrice);

  event GoldBought(uint256 amount, uint256 output, uint256 execPrice);

  event SlippageWarning(string tradeType, uint256 oraclePrice, uint256
  execPrice, uint256 timestamp);

  // ============================================================

  // 11.11.2 CORE REBALANCING LOGIC (carries BP §7.8.2)

  // ============================================================

  /// @dev Evaluates whether a rebalance is needed and executes it if

  /// profitable. Called by the keeper bot (§11.9).

  function executeRebalance() external {

  // 1. Update reserve state (from §4.4.2, v1.2)

  reserveModule.refresh();

  uint256 nav = reserveModule.nav();

  uint256 rr = reserveModule.reserveRatio();

  // 2. Get current target gold weight from the MASE weight registry

  // (Listing 2, §7.7; replaces the retired macro-engine source)

  uint256 targetGold = getTargetGoldWeight();

  // 3. Compute observed gold weight

  uint256 goldValue = reserveModule.goldValue();

  uint256 observedGold = (goldValue * 1e18) / nav;

  // 4. Compute deviation

  int256 deviation = int256(observedGold) - int256(targetGold);

  if (deviation == 0) return; // Perfectly balanced

  // 4b. NO-TRADE ZONE (§10.4-§10.5): skip when inside the dynamic

  // band. The band widens under an elevated crisis score (§11.6).

  uint256 band = isStress() ? noTradeBandStress : noTradeBand;

  if (abs(deviation) <= band) {

  emit RebalanceSkipped(block.timestamp, deviation, "No-trade zone");

  return;

  }

  // 5. Direction lock check (except in stress mode)

  if (rr >= 1.05e18) {

  if (block.timestamp - lastTrade.timestamp < DIRECTION_LOCK_DURATION) {

  if ((deviation > 0 && lastTrade.direction == -1) ||

  (deviation < 0 && lastTrade.direction == 1)) {

  emit RebalanceSkipped(block.timestamp, deviation, "Direction lock");

  return; // Locked in same direction

  }

  }

  }

  // 6. Compute required surplus value

  uint256 surplusValue = abs(deviation) * nav / 1e18;

  // 7. Cap by liquidity (pool depth)

  uint256 poolDepth = getPoolDepth(PAXG_USDC_POOL);

  uint256 maxTrade = (poolDepth * MAX_POOL_FRACTION) / 1e18;

  if (surplusValue > maxTrade) surplusValue = maxTrade;

  // 8. Cap by daily turnover

  resetDailyTurnover();

  uint256 remainingDaily = (nav * MAX_DAILY_TURNOVER / 1e18) -
  dailyTurnover;

  if (surplusValue > remainingDaily) surplusValue = remainingDaily;

  if (surplusValue == 0) return;

  // 9. Estimate execution cost (dynamic gas + slippage)

  uint256 estimatedCost = estimateExecutionCost(surplusValue,
  deviation > 0);

  uint256 urgency = (rr < 1.10e18) ? (1e18 / rr) : 0;

  // 10. Compute net benefit (objective function improvement)

  uint256 currentDeviation = abs(deviation);

  uint256 newDeviation = abs(deviation - (int256(surplusValue) * 1e18 /
  int256(nav)));

  uint256 benefit = (currentDeviation - newDeviation) * LAMBDA_1 / 1e18;

  benefit += urgency * LAMBDA_3 / 1e18;

  uint256 costTerm = estimatedCost * LAMBDA_2 / 1e18;

  // 11. COST-BENEFIT GATE (§10.8): execute only when NetBenefit >
  epsilon

  if (benefit > costTerm + BENEFIT_MARGIN) {

  if (deviation > 0) {

  // Sell gold

  uint256 sellAmount = (surplusValue * 1e18) / getGoldPrice();

  sellGold(sellAmount);

  lastTrade.direction = -1;

  } else {

  // Buy gold

  uint256 buyAmount = (surplusValue * 1e18) / getGoldPrice();

  buyGold(buyAmount);

  lastTrade.direction = 1;

  }

  lastTrade.timestamp = block.timestamp;

  dailyTurnover += surplusValue;

  emit RebalanceExecuted(block.timestamp, deviation, surplusValue,
  estimatedCost);

  } else {

  emit RebalanceSkipped(block.timestamp, deviation, "Net benefit below
  margin");

  }

  }

  /// @dev Target gold weight read from the MASE weight registry (§7.7).

  /// XAU is component index 6 in the fixed COMPONENTS order (Listing
  2).

  function getTargetGoldWeight() internal view returns (uint256) {

  (uint256[7] memory weights, bytes32 methodologyVersion, bytes32
  dataVersion) =

  weightRegistry.getLiveWeights();

  return weights[6]; // "XAU"

  }

  /// @dev Stress flag consumed by the no-trade band (§11.6). Sourced
  from

  /// the crisis-score oracle path (§5.5). Conservative default is

  /// stress = true when the oracle is unset (mirrors Listing 2).

  function isStress() internal view returns (bool) {

  if (crisisOracle == address(0)) return true;

  return ICrisisOracle(crisisOracle).score() > crisisThreshold;

  }

  /// @dev Estimates execution cost (gas + slippage) using live data.

  /// @param amount The USD value of the trade.

  /// @param isSell True if selling gold, false if buying.

  /// @return Total estimated cost in USD (1e18 scale).

  function estimateExecutionCost(uint256 amount, bool isSell) internal
  view returns (uint256) {

  // 1. Dynamic gas cost

  uint256 gasPrice = getGasPrice(); // Uses block.basefee, oracle, or
  fallback

  uint256 gasUnits = estimateGasUnits(amount, isSell);

  uint256 gasCost = gasPrice * gasUnits;

  // 2. Slippage cost (dynamic, based on trade direction)

  uint256 slippageBps = getSlippageEstimate(isSell);

  uint256 slippage = (amount * slippageBps) / 10000;

  // 3. Router fee (0.10% fixed, adjustable by governance)

  uint256 routerFee = (amount * 10) / 10000; // 0.10%

  return gasCost + slippage + routerFee;

  }

  /// @dev Returns a realistic gas price estimate.

  /// Prioritises:

  /// 1) Chainlink Gas Oracle (if set via governance)

  /// 2) block.basefee (mainnet and modern L2s) with a 20% buffer

  /// 3) GAS_FALLBACK (chains where block.basefee is unavailable)

  function getGasPrice() internal view returns (uint256) {

  // 1. Try Chainlink Gas Oracle (production mode, if set)

  uint256 oracleGasPrice = getGasPriceFromOracle();

  if (oracleGasPrice > 0) return oracleGasPrice;

  // 2. Try block.basefee (standard on mainnet / modern L2s)

  uint256 basefee = block.basefee;

  if (basefee > 0) {

  // Add 20% buffer to avoid underpricing

  return (basefee * 120) / 100;

  }

  // 3. Fallback for chains where block.basefee is not supported

  // (older L2s, devnets, or chains with zero basefee)

  return GAS_FALLBACK;

  }

  /// @dev Fetches gas price from the Chainlink oracle (if set).

  /// Returns 0 if oracle is not set, feed is stale, or price <= 0.

  /// Assumes the feed returns a price in Gwei; adjust scaling as
  needed.

  function getGasPriceFromOracle() internal view returns (uint256) {

  if (gasOracle == address(0)) return 0;

  // AggregatorV3Interface (Chainlink)

  (, int256 price, , uint256 updatedAt, ) =
  AggregatorV3Interface(gasOracle).latestRoundData();

  // Check freshness (max 1 hour)

  if (block.timestamp - updatedAt > 3600) return 0;

  if (price <= 0) return 0;

  // Convert from Gwei to wei (1 Gwei = 1e9 wei; adjust per feed
  decimals)

  return uint256(price) * 1e10;

  }

  /// @dev Estimates gas units required for a trade.

  /// In production, this should call aggregator.estimateGas().

  /// For testnet, returns a fixed conservative estimate.

  function estimateGasUnits(uint256 amount, bool isSell) internal pure
  returns (uint256) {

  // In a real implementation, this would call:

  // return aggregator.estimateGas(swapData);

  // For testnet, use a fixed estimate.

  return GAS_UNITS_ESTIMATE;

  }

  /// @dev Returns slippage estimate in basis points (bps).

  /// 10 bps = 0.10%, 50 bps = 0.50%

  function getSlippageEstimate(bool isSell) internal view returns
  (uint256) {

  // On testnet, use conservative fixed rates.

  // On mainnet, this could read from an oracle or pool depth (Ch. 12).

  if (isSell) {

  return SLIPPAGE_BPS_SELL;

  } else {

  return SLIPPAGE_BPS_BUY;

  }

  }

  /// @dev Resets daily turnover counter if a new day has started.

  function resetDailyTurnover() internal {

  if (block.timestamp >= lastTurnoverReset + 1 days) {

  emit DailyTurnoverReset(block.timestamp, dailyTurnover); // BP §7.9

  dailyTurnover = 0;

  lastTurnoverReset = block.timestamp;

  }

  }

  /// @dev Absolute value helper for signed integers.

  function abs(int256 x) internal pure returns (uint256) {

  return x > 0 ? uint256(x) : uint256(-x);

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 11.11.3 DEX EXECUTION (carries BP §7.8.3)

  // ============================================================

  /// @dev Executes a sell order for gold (PAXG -> USDC) via DEX
  aggregator.

  /// @param amount Amount of PAXG to sell (in 18 decimals).

  function sellGold(uint256 amount) internal {

  // 1. Get current oracle price (§17 canonical gold price)

  uint256 oraclePrice = getGoldPrice();

  // 2. Compute minimum output (slippage guard, §11.5.1)

  uint256 oracleValue = (amount * oraclePrice) / 1e18;

  uint256 minOutput = oracleValue * (1e18 - slippageTolerance) / 1e18;

  // 3. Get quote from aggregator and validate

  (uint256 execPrice, uint256 output) = getQuote(PAXG, USDC, amount,
  AGGREGATOR);

  // 4. Validate execPrice >= oraclePrice * (1 - slippageTolerance)

  uint256 minValidPrice = oraclePrice * (1e18 - slippageTolerance) /
  1e18;

  require(execPrice >= minValidPrice, "Slippage violation (sell)");

  // 5. Execute swap via aggregator (with deadline)

  bytes memory swapData = encodeSwapData(

  PAXG,

  USDC,

  amount,

  minOutput,

  address(this),

  block.timestamp + 300 // 5-minute deadline

  );

  // 6. Submit via private mempool (Flashbots) if available

  submitTradeWithFlashbots(swapData);

  // 7. Update reserve state

  reserveModule.refresh(); // From §4.4.2 (v1.2)

  emit GoldSold(amount, output, execPrice);

  }

  /// @dev Executes a buy order for gold (USDC -> PAXG) via DEX
  aggregator.

  /// @param amount Amount of USDC to spend.

  function buyGold(uint256 amount) internal {

  // 1. Get current oracle price

  uint256 oraclePrice = getGoldPrice();

  // 2. Compute minimum output (slippage guard, §11.5.1)

  uint256 oracleGold = (amount * 1e18) / oraclePrice;

  uint256 minGold = oracleGold * (1e18 - slippageTolerance) / 1e18;

  // 3. Get quote from aggregator and validate

  (uint256 execPrice, uint256 output) = getQuote(USDC, PAXG, amount,
  AGGREGATOR);

  // 4. Validate execPrice <= oraclePrice * (1 + slippageTolerance)

  uint256 maxValidPrice = oraclePrice * (1e18 + slippageTolerance) /
  1e18;

  require(execPrice <= maxValidPrice, "Slippage violation (buy)");

  // 5. Execute swap via aggregator (with deadline)

  bytes memory swapData = encodeSwapData(

  USDC,

  PAXG,

  amount,

  minGold,

  address(this),

  block.timestamp + 300 // 5-minute deadline

  );

  // 6. Submit via private mempool (Flashbots) if available

  submitTradeWithFlashbots(swapData);

  // 7. Update reserve state

  reserveModule.refresh();

  emit GoldBought(amount, output, execPrice);

  }

  /// @dev Fetches a quote from the DEX aggregator.

  function getQuote(

  address tokenIn,

  address tokenOut,

  uint256 amount,

  address aggregator

  ) internal returns (uint256 execPrice, uint256 output) {

  bytes memory quoteData = abi.encodeWithSelector(

  IAggregator.quote.selector,

  tokenIn,

  tokenOut,

  amount

  );

  (bool success, bytes memory result) =
  aggregator.staticcall(quoteData);

  require(success, "Quote failed");

  (execPrice, output) = abi.decode(result, (uint256, uint256));

  return (execPrice, output);

  }

  /// @dev Submits a trade to Flashbots private mempool (optional).

  /// On testnet, this is a no-op; on mainnet, it routes via Flashbots.

  function submitTradeWithFlashbots(bytes memory swapData) internal {

  // Testnet implementation: execute directly

  // On mainnet: submit to Flashbots relay

  (bool success, ) = AGGREGATOR.call(swapData);

  require(success, "Swap execution failed");

  }

  /// @dev Encodes swap data for the aggregator (simplified).

  /// In production, use the aggregator's specific encoding (Ch. 12).

  function encodeSwapData(

  address tokenIn,

  address tokenOut,

  uint256 amount,

  uint256 minAmountOut,

  address receiver,

  uint256 deadline

  ) internal pure returns (bytes memory) {

  // Placeholder -- replace with the production aggregator encoding

  return abi.encodeWithSelector(

  IAggregator.swap.selector,

  tokenIn,

  tokenOut,

  amount,

  minAmountOut,

  receiver,

  deadline

  );

  }

  // ---- Reserve and price hooks (v1.2 §4.4.2 and §9.4.1 references)
  ----

  function getGoldPrice() internal view returns (uint256) {

  return IGoldPriceOracle(goldOracle).goldPrice(); // §17 canonical
  price

  }

  function getPoolDepth(address pool) internal view returns (uint256) {

  return IPoolDepthOracle(poolDepthOracle).poolDepth(pool); // §11.4

  }

  // ---- Governance functions ----

  /// @dev Sets the gas oracle address (Chainlink or equivalent).

  /// Only callable by Risk Council (4/7) with a 24h timelock.

  function setGasOracle(address _gasOracle) external onlyRiskCouncil {

  require(_gasOracle != address(0), "Invalid address");

  address old = gasOracle;

  gasOracle = _gasOracle;

  emit GasOracleUpdated(old, _gasOracle);

  }

  /// @dev Sets execution venues, assets and module hooks (§11.10
  governance).

  function setExecutionConfig(

  address _paxg,

  address _usdc,

  address _pool,

  address _poolDepthOracle,

  address _goldOracle,

  address _reserveModule,

  address _crisisOracle

  ) external onlyRiskCouncil {

  require(_paxg != address(0) && _usdc != address(0), "Invalid asset");

  require(_pool != address(0), "Invalid pool");

  require(_reserveModule != address(0), "Invalid reserve module");

  PAXG = _paxg;

  USDC = _usdc;

  PAXG_USDC_POOL = _pool;

  poolDepthOracle = _poolDepthOracle;

  goldOracle = _goldOracle;

  reserveModule = IReserveState(_reserveModule);

  crisisOracle = _crisisOracle;

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 11.11.4 MODIFIER PLACEHOLDERS (carries BP §7.8.4)

  // ============================================================

  modifier onlyRiskCouncil() {

  // In production, this checks msg.sender against a 4/7 Multi-Sig.

  // On testnet, it can be bypassed or mocked.

  require(msg.sender == riskCouncil, "Only Risk Council");

  _;

  }

  }

  // ---- Interfaces: registry (Listing 2) and external modules ----

  interface IMASEWeightRegistry {

  function getLiveWeights() external view returns (uint256[7] memory,
  bytes32, bytes32);

  }

  interface ICrisisOracle {

  function score() external view returns (uint256);

  }

  interface AggregatorV3Interface {

  function latestRoundData()

  external

  view

  returns (uint80 roundId, int256 answer, uint256 startedAt, uint256
  updatedAt, uint80 answeredInRound);

  }

  interface IReserveState {

  function refresh() external;

  function nav() external view returns (uint256);

  function reserveRatio() external view returns (uint256);

  function goldValue() external view returns (uint256);

  }

  interface IGoldPriceOracle {

  function goldPrice() external view returns (uint256);

  }

  interface IPoolDepthOracle {

  function poolDepth(address pool) external view returns (uint256);

  }

  interface IAggregator {

  function quote(address tokenIn, address tokenOut, uint256 amount)

  external

  view

  returns (uint256 execPrice, uint256 output);

  function swap(

  address tokenIn,

  address tokenOut,

  uint256 amount,

  uint256 minAmountOut,

  address receiver,

  uint256 deadline

  ) external returns (bool success);

  }

  Fidelity note (adapts BP §7.8). (1) Target source:
  getTargetGoldWeight() from the retired Adaptive Macro Engine (BP §6.7)
  is replaced by a read of the live weight vector from the
  MASEWeightRegistry (Listing 2, Section 7.7); XAU is component index 6,
  and the registry's live (smoothed) state plays the role of W^{Target}
  in the deviation math of Sections 10.4–10.6. (2) No-trade zone: the
  gate of Sections 10.4–10.5 is new relative to BP — executeRebalance()
  returns with RebalanceSkipped("No-trade zone") whenever the deviation
  is inside the dynamic band, which widens to noTradeBandStress when the
  crisis oracle (Section 5.5) exceeds its threshold (Section 11.6); the
  default when the crisis oracle is unset is the conservative (wide)
  band, mirroring Listing 2. (3) Cost-benefit gate: BP's bare benefit >
  costTerm test is tightened to benefit > costTerm + BENEFIT_MARGIN,
  implementing the epsilon margin of Section 10.8 [MS §41, COO-14]; the
  skip reason string records the margin failure. (4) Direction lock:
  retained verbatim, including the mechanical override below RR = 1.05
  (Section 11.5.3). (5) The v1.2 internal references
  updateReserveState(), getNAV(), getReserveRatio() (BP §4.4.2),
  getGoldPrice() (BP §9.4.1) and getPoolDepth() are externalized to
  interfaces implemented by the reserve module (Chapters 13–14) and the
  oracle layer (Chapter 17), with BP call sites and control flow
  preserved. (6) Venue and asset addresses (PAXG, USDC, pool,
  aggregator) resolve through governed configuration rather than
  hard-coded constants, deferring ultimately to the Asset Admission
  Registry (Chapter 15). (7) The placeholder swap encoding
  (IAggregator.swap.selector) and the fixed gas-units estimator must be
  replaced by the production aggregator encoding and estimator before
  mainnet (Chapter 12); BP's own placeholders are flagged in comments.
  (8) Scope: the listing instantiates the gold leg and the fiat bloc —
  the highest-impact execution venue; the per-component generalization
  of Sections 10.4–10.6 runs the identical control flow per registry
  component with component-specific pools, bands and slippage estimates.
  The contract deliberately does not re-solve the J optimization
  on-chain for arbitrary candidate sizes: the on-chain path evaluates
  the gated candidate, and reproducibility of the full decision (Section
  24.1) is achieved by publishing inputs and versions so any auditor can
  re-derive the same trade decision off-chain.

12. Execution Optimization and Slippage Protection

12.1 Purpose

  The Execution and Slippage Protection module is the transaction
  execution layer of the protocol. It translates MARP rebalancing
  decisions into actual on-chain trades through DEX aggregators, and it
  guarantees that every trade the system executes is bounded, quoted,
  sized to the market and shielded from value extraction [BP §10.1].

The module enforces four properties on every protocol trade:

1.  Slippage is bounded on both sides (buy and sell) to prevent
    front-running and oracle manipulation.

2.  Trades are quote-based, not spot-based, to ensure execution at fair
    market prices.

3.  Trade sizes are dynamically capped by liquidity depth to avoid
    excessive market impact.

4.  MEV (Miner Extractable Value) is minimised through private-mempool
    submission and deadline enforcement.

  Never execute a trade based on an oracle spot price alone. Always
  validate against an executable quote.

The modification specification frames execution as an explicit
optimization problem rather than an implementation detail [MS §65]. For
a required rebalance amount q, the engine minimizes total execution
cost:

  ExecutionCost(q) = Spread(q) + Slippage(q) + Impact(q) + Fees(q)

subject to two service constraints:

ExecutionTime ≤ T_(max) MarketImpact ≤ I_(max)

and the engine may split a required rebalance across time and venues
when economically advantageous. Splitting is an optimization decision
inside the cost problem, not an exception path: a large correction
executed as staged chunks across cycles (Section 12.4) is the normal
behavior whenever the single-trade solution would violate the impact
constraint.

Execution optimization is the delivery half of the MARP doctrine. The
decision layer determines whether a weight correction is worth trading
at all — the cost-benefit gate NetBenefit_i > ε of Section 10.8, fed by
the MARP triggers of Chapter 11 — while this chapter defines how the
resulting order is executed so that realized costs stay inside the
estimate the gate used. If execution were unbounded, the gate's
arithmetic would be fiction: a rebalance that clears the gate with an
estimated 40 basis points of cost but executes with 3% slippage is a
loss the decision layer never approved. The execution layer therefore
closes the loop — every realized trade is emitted with its quote, price
and size (Section 12.9), and systematic gaps between estimated and
realized cost are calibration inputs to the validation program (Section
23.11).

12.2 The Quote-Based Execution Model

The protocol does not execute trades directly against spot prices [BP
§10.2]. Every trade follows a three-step sequence:

1.  Fetch a quote from the DEX aggregator (1inch, Paraswap, or an
    equivalent router).

2.  Validate the quote against the oracle-derived reference price.

3.  Execute the trade only if the quote falls within the two-sided
    slippage bounds (Section 12.3).

The reasons for quote-based execution are structural:

-   Spot prices can be manipulated — flash loans and low liquidity make
    a momentary pool price an unreliable reference.

-   Quotes reflect actual market depth and executable prices, not the
    last traded tick.

-   Aggregators route through multiple pools to find the best available
    price for the exact size being traded.

12.2.1 The Quote Lifecycle

1.  requestQuote(tokenIn, tokenOut, amount) returns minOutput for a
    sell, or maxInput for a buy.

2.  Validate the quote: execPrice ≤ oraclePrice × (1 + τ) for buys;
    execPrice ≥ oraclePrice × (1 − τ) for sells.

3.  Execute via swap() with a deadline (Section 12.6).

The validation step is the substance of the model. The executable quote
from the aggregator is compared against the protocol's independent
reference, and only agreement within tolerance authorizes the trade. The
two prices come from disjoint sources — the aggregator observes
liquidity, the oracle observes the validated market consensus (Chapter
17) — so neither side of the comparison can be steered by the same
actor.

  Adaptation note (v2.0): in v1.2 the reference price could be read from
  the protocol's own AMM; under v2.0 the oracle price is the canonical
  multi-source validated price of Chapter 17, never a single pool. This
  closes a manipulation loop in which an attacker who could move the
  reference pool could steer both the index and the execution guard. The
  execution layer additionally refuses to trade when the oracle feed is
  stale or below its confidence quorum (Chapter 17), because a quote
  cannot be validated against a reference that is not live.

12.3 Two-Sided Slippage Bounds

The protocol enforces asymmetric slippage bounds to prevent both
overpayment and under-receipt [BP §10.3]. Buying and selling are guarded
independently because the protocol sits on opposite sides of the trade
in each direction: as a seller it must not receive too little, and as a
buyer it must not pay too much. Each guard compares the executable rate
of a live quote against the oracle rate with a per-asset tolerance τ,
and each guard can only fail the trade — never relax it.

12.3.1 Selling Gold (PAXG → USDC)

When selling gold, the protocol delivers PAXG and receives USDC. The
slippage guard is:

  ExecRate ≥ OracleRate × (1 - τ_(sell))

Meaning: the protocol must receive at least (1 - τ_sell) of the oracle
price per unit of PAXG. If OracleRate = 2,500 USD/PAXG and τ_sell = 0.01
(1%), the protocol requires ExecRate ≥ 2,475 USD/PAXG. If the quote is
worse than 2,475, the transaction reverts — the protocol would rather
leave the deviation uncorrected than correct it at a price that destroys
more value than it repairs.

12.3.2 Buying Gold (USDC → PAXG)

When buying gold, the protocol pays USDC and receives PAXG. The slippage
guard is:

  ExecRate ≤ OracleRate × (1 + τ_(buy))

Meaning: the protocol must pay no more than (1 + τ_buy) of the oracle
price per unit of PAXG. With OracleRate = 2,500 USD/PAXG and τ_buy =
0.01 (1%), the protocol requires ExecRate ≤ 2,525 USD/PAXG; a worse
quote reverts the transaction. Together the two bounds form a two-sided
corridor around the oracle price: any executed trade is provably close
to the independently validated reference, whichever direction the
protocol is trading.

12.3.3 Slippage Tolerance Table

Tolerances are per asset class, reflecting liquidity depth and typical
spread [BP §10.3.3]:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Asset Type        τ_sell            τ_buy             Rationale
  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- -----------------
  Stablecoins       0.005 (0.5%)      0.005 (0.5%)      Highly liquid;
  (USDC, EURC)                                          tight spread.

  Tokenized Gold    0.010 (1.0%)      0.010 (1.0%)      Lower liquidity;
  (PAXG)                                                higher spread.

  Minor FX (JPY,    0.015 (1.5%)      0.015 (1.5%)      Thin liquidity;
  CNY)                                                  wider spread.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

These values are adjustable by the Risk Council (4-of-7) [BP §10.3.3].
In v2.0 they are governed parameters of the execution contract (Listing
5) — changed through the parameter-registry process with publication
(Section 22.3) — rather than compile-time constants, so that
recalibration after validation (Section 23.11) does not require
redeployment. The minor-FX tolerances apply to registry-resolved FX
instruments (Section 15.8), which are executed through the same
quote-based path when the reserve implements FX exposure. In crisis mode
the tolerances themselves do not widen; instead execution sizes shrink
and liquidity requirements rise (Sections 5.5 and 14.4), which tightens
the realized slippage distribution precisely when markets are stressed.

12.4 Trade Sizing and Staged Execution

To prevent the protocol from moving the market against itself, trade
sizes are dynamically capped by available pool depth [BP §10.4]. The cap
is the mechanical expression of the impact constraint MarketImpact ≤
I_max of Section 12.1: rather than estimating impact ex post, the
protocol bounds its own footprint ex ante as a fixed fraction of
observed depth.

12.4.1 The Liquidity Cap

  TradeSize_(max) = min(RequiredTrade, 0.10 × PoolDepth_(24h))

where:

-   RequiredTrade is the amount needed to rebalance, produced by the
    MARP execution engine (Chapter 11) — never a raw index-weight
    difference.

-   PoolDepth_{24h} is the 24-hour rolling average liquidity of the
    primary DEX pool for the pair (for example PAXG/USDC).

-   The 10% cap ensures the protocol never consumes more than 10% of the
    pool's daily volume in a single trade.

The cap is recomputed every cycle from fresh depth observations, so it
contracts automatically when liquidity evaporates: the failure mode of
thin markets is met with smaller orders, not with the same orders at
worse prices. Listing 5 implements the cap as capTradeSize(), applied
before any quote is requested, so that quotes are always solicited for
an admissible size.

12.4.2 Staged Execution for Large Trades

If the required trade exceeds the cap, the engine stages the execution
[BP §10.4.2]:

1.  Execute TradeSize_max immediately.

2.  Wait for the next rebalancing cycle (minimum 1 hour).

3.  Execute another TradeSize_max chunk.

4.  Repeat until the full required trade is completed.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Stage                   Action                  Constraint
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  1                       Execute the first       Size ≤ 10% of
                          TradeSize_max chunk     PoolDepth_24h; quote
                                                  within the two-sided
                                                  slippage bounds.

  2                       Wait for the next       Minimum 1 hour between
                          rebalancing cycle       stages; depth and quote
                                                  re-observed.

  3                       Execute the next chunk  Cap, quote and guard
                                                  revalidated from
                                                  scratch — each stage is
                                                  an independent trade.

  …                       Repeat until complete   Residual is a standing
                                                  intention that must
                                                  re-justify itself each
                                                  cycle (Chapter 11);
                                                  abandoned if the
                                                  trigger lapses or the
                                                  direction lock (Section
                                                  11.5) flips.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

This prevents the protocol from being a forced seller or buyer in thin
markets. Staged execution is also the mechanical realization of the MARP
partial-correction doctrine: a large correction is a sequence of small,
independently justified trades rather than one block transaction, and
the multi-cycle pacing composes with the no-trade zones and the
rebalancing direction lock of Chapters 10 and 11. Splitting across
venues — routing through multiple aggregators and pools — is the spatial
analogue, chosen by the cost optimization of Section 12.1 whenever it
reduces ExecutionCost(q) [MS §65].

12.5 The Minimum Profitability Guard

To prevent the protocol from executing trades that are not economically
beneficial, each trade must meet a minimum profitability threshold [BP
§10.5]:

  NetBenefit = ReductionInDeviation - EstimatedExecutionCost > 0

If NetBenefit ≤ 0, the trade is suppressed — even if the deviation
exceeds the dynamic trigger threshold. This ensures the protocol does
not spend gas and spread on economically insignificant rebalances: a
correction that repairs less deviation than it costs in execution is
value-destructive by its own arithmetic, and the guard refuses it before
any swap calldata is built.

Under the unified v2.0 gate the test is strictly stronger. The MARP
cost-benefit gate of Section 10.8 requires:

NetBenefit_(i) = ReductionInDeviation_(i) - EstimatedCost_(i) > ε ε > 0

and the on-chain implementation enforces ε as the minProfitability
parameter, initialized to $10 (MIN_PROFITABILITY, Section 12.8). A
candidate trade must therefore clear three independent hurdles in
sequence: the MARP trigger logic of Chapters 10–11 (is the deviation
worth correcting at all), the profitability guard (is the correction
worth more than its cost), and the slippage bounds at execution time (is
the live quote within tolerance). Suppression is cheap by design — a
suppressed candidate emits TradeSkipped with its arithmetic (Section
12.9) rather than consuming gas in a reverting transaction.

  Fidelity note: BP §10.5 illustrated the guard with an inline
  require(netBenefit > 0) inside executeRebalance(). That snippet is
  superseded by the cleaned guard functions of Listing 5
  (computeNetBenefit / isTradeProfitable), which add underflow-safe
  arithmetic, the full cost model of Section 12.7 and the ε-threshold of
  Section 10.8.

12.6 MEV Protection (Private Mempool Submission)

To minimise MEV (Miner Extractable Value) attacks, all protocol trades
are protected by three measures [BP §10.6]:

1.  All trades are submitted via a private mempool (for example
    Flashbots Protect or MEV Blocker), so intent is not broadcast to the
    public mempool where searchers can front-run, sandwich or back-run
    it.

2.  Trades include a deadline — maximum 5 minutes from submission — to
    prevent delayed execution of a quote that was valid when signed but
    stale when landed.

3.  Trades are split into smaller chunks when possible to reduce the
    per-transaction front-running incentive; chunking composes with the
    staged execution of Section 12.4.

The private-submission path is submitTradeWithFlashbots() in Listing 5,
and the deadline check is enforced at settlement, so even a bundle that
lands late cannot execute against a moved market: the trade reverts
rather than filling at a stale price. Private submission protects the
quote-validation step itself — a validated quote sitting in the public
mempool is an advertisement of an intended trade at a known bound, which
is exactly the information a sandwich attacker needs.

  Chain adaptation: the relay address is per-chain — Flashbots Protect
  on Ethereum, the equivalent sequencer-level private submission path
  where available on L2 deployments, with testnet addresses listed in
  Appendix D. On chains without a private relay, deadline enforcement
  and quote re-validation at settlement are the binding protections; the
  aggregator address must be immutable or governed by a 4-of-7 Multi-Sig
  with a 48-hour timelock to prevent malicious router upgrades (Section
  12.9).

12.7 Gas Estimation and Cost Control

The protocol estimates execution costs before each trade and adjusts the
NetBenefit calculation accordingly [BP §10.7]. Four cost components are
estimated:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Cost Type                           Estimation Method
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  Gas Price                           gasPrice = oracleGasPrice × 1.2
                                      (20% buffer)

  Gas Units                           gasUnits = estimatedGas × 1.1 (10%
                                      buffer for L2 variance)

  Slippage                            slippage = tradeValue × (τ / 2)
                                      (half of max slippage for
                                      estimation)

  Router Fee                          routerFee = tradeValue × 0.001
                                      (0.1% aggregator fee)
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

EstimatedCost = (gasPrice × gasUnits) + slippage + routerFee

If NetBenefit ≤ EstimatedCost, the trade is suppressed [BP §10.7]. The
buffers are deliberate conservatism: the 20% gas-price buffer absorbs
spikes between estimation and landing, the 10% gas-unit buffer absorbs
L2 variance in execution cost, and slippage is estimated at half its
bound because validated execution normally fills well inside the guard.
Because NetBenefit is defined net of cost in Section 12.5, v2.0 reads
the BP condition as a belt-and-braces double test: a trade must clear
the ε-threshold of Section 10.8 and must not be dominated by its own
cost estimate. Both tests are implemented in the guard functions of
Listing 5.

The gas estimate is refreshed from the live gas price on every
evaluation (oracleGasPrice() in Listing 5), so cost-sensitive
suppression adapts to network conditions automatically: in a gas spike,
marginal trades are skipped and only deviations large enough to justify
the elevated cost are corrected. This is the MARP doctrine expressed in
gas units — calculate daily, trade only on justified urgency (Section
10.1).

12.8 Smart Contract Implementation — Execution Protection

Listing 5 consolidates the execution layer as a single contract: the
constants and state of BP §10.8.1, the core functions of BP §10.8.2
(sellGold, buyGold, getQuote and the private-mempool submission path),
and the minimum profitability guard of BP §10.8.3 (computeNetBenefit,
isTradeProfitable), together with the liquidity cap of Section 12.4 and
the cost model of Section 12.7. The substantive logic — guards, bounds,
sequencing — is carried unchanged from the v1.2 source; the adaptations
are itemized in the fidelity note below the listing.

Listing 5 — Execution and slippage protection (adapts BP §10.8)

  // SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT

  pragma solidity ^0.8.19;

  /// @title ExecutionProtection

  /// @notice Quote-based, slippage-bounded, liquidity-aware execution
  layer for

  /// MARP rebalancing trades (adapts BP §10.8; guards per §12.3–§12.5).

  abstract contract ExecutionProtection {

  // ================================================================

  // 1. CONSTANTS AND STATE (BP §10.8.1)

  // ================================================================

  // ---- Immutable constants ----

  // Trade deadline: constitutional, immutable (BP §10.10).

  uint256 public constant TRADE_DEADLINE = 300; // 5 minutes

  // Typical gas units of the guarded swap path (§12.7 estimation).

  uint256 public constant ESTIMATED_GAS = 350000;

  // Testnet ETH/USD fixing for gas-cost conversion; production reads
  the

  // live ETH/USD feed of the oracle layer (§17; Appendix D).

  uint256 public constant ETH_USD = 2000e18;

  // ---- Governed execution parameters (Risk Council 4/7; §12.3.3,
  §12.9) ----

  // BP §10.8.1 declares these as constants; BP §10.3.3 and §10.10 make
  them

  // Risk-Council-adjustable. Implemented as governed parameters
  initialized

  // to the BP values (1e18 scale).

  uint256 public slippageSellGold = 0.010e18; // tau_sell,gold = 1.0%

  uint256 public slippageBuyGold = 0.010e18; // tau_buy,gold = 1.0%

  uint256 public slippageSellStable = 0.005e18; // tau_sell,stable =
  0.5%

  uint256 public slippageBuyStable = 0.005e18; // tau_buy,stable = 0.5%

  uint256 public maxPoolFraction = 0.10e18; // 10% of 24h pool depth
  (§12.4.1)

  uint256 public minProfitability = 10e18; // $10 minimum net benefit
  (§12.5)

  // ---- Deployment-set immutable addresses (per-chain; Appendix D)
  ----

  address public immutable AGGREGATOR; // 1inch / Paraswap router

  address public immutable FLASHBOTS_RELAY; // private-mempool relay

  address public immutable PAXG; // tokenized gold instrument

  address public immutable USDC; // settlement stablecoin

  address public immutable GOLD_ORACLE; // canonical gold oracle (§17)

  address public immutable RESERVE_MANAGER; // reserve manager contract
  (§14)

  // ---- Governance ----

  address public riskCouncil; // 4/7 Multi-Sig (§22)

  // ================================================================

  // 2. EVENTS (BP §10.9)

  // ================================================================

  event GoldSold(uint256 amount, uint256 output, uint256 execPrice);

  event GoldBought(uint256 amount, uint256 output, uint256 execPrice);

  event TradeExecuted(

  address indexed tokenIn,

  address indexed tokenOut,

  uint256 amountIn,

  uint256 amountOut,

  uint256 execPrice,

  uint256 timestamp

  );

  event TradeSkipped(

  string reason,

  uint256 deviation,

  uint256 estimatedCost,

  uint256 netBenefit

  );

  event SlippageWarning(

  string tradeType,

  uint256 oraclePrice,

  uint256 execPrice,

  uint256 timestamp

  );

  event ExecutionParametersChanged(

  uint256 sellGoldTolerance,

  uint256 buyGoldTolerance,

  uint256 sellStableTolerance,

  uint256 buyStableTolerance,

  uint256 poolFraction,

  uint256 profitabilityThreshold

  );

  modifier onlyRiskCouncil() {

  require(msg.sender == riskCouncil, "Only Risk Council");

  _;

  }

  constructor(

  address aggregator_,

  address flashbotsRelay_,

  address paxg_,

  address usdc_,

  address goldOracle_,

  address reserveManager_,

  address riskCouncil_

  ) {

  AGGREGATOR = aggregator_;

  FLASHBOTS_RELAY = flashbotsRelay_;

  PAXG = paxg_;

  USDC = usdc_;

  GOLD_ORACLE = goldOracle_;

  RESERVE_MANAGER = reserveManager_;

  riskCouncil = riskCouncil_;

  }

  // ================================================================

  // 3. CORE FUNCTIONS (BP §10.8.2)

  // ================================================================

  /// @dev Executes a sell order for gold (PAXG -> USDC).

  /// @param amount Amount of PAXG to sell (18 decimals).

  /// @param minOutput Minimum USDC to receive (computed with slippage
  guard).

  function sellGold(uint256 amount, uint256 minOutput) internal {

  require(amount > 0, "Amount must be positive");

  // 1. Get current oracle price (canonical multi-source gold price,
  §17)

  uint256 oraclePrice = getGoldPrice();

  // 2. Compute minimum output (slippage guard)

  uint256 oracleValue = (amount * oraclePrice) / 1e18;

  uint256 minOutput_ = (oracleValue * (1e18 - slippageSellGold)) / 1e18;

  require(minOutput >= minOutput_, "Slippage too high (sell)");

  // 3. Get quote from aggregator

  (uint256 execPrice, uint256 output) = getQuote(PAXG, USDC, amount,
  AGGREGATOR);

  // 4. Validate execPrice >= oraclePrice * (1 - tau_sell)

  uint256 minValidPrice = (oraclePrice * (1e18 - slippageSellGold)) /
  1e18;

  require(execPrice >= minValidPrice, "Slippage violation (sell)");

  if (execPrice < oraclePrice) {

  emit SlippageWarning("SELL_GOLD", oraclePrice, execPrice,
  block.timestamp);

  }

  // 5. Execute trade via aggregator (with deadline)

  uint256 deadline = block.timestamp + TRADE_DEADLINE;

  bytes memory swapData = encodeSwapData(

  PAXG, USDC, amount, minOutput, address(this), deadline

  );

  // 6. Submit via private mempool (Flashbots)

  submitTradeWithFlashbots(swapData, deadline);

  // 7. Update reserve state (reserve manager, §14)

  updateReserveState();

  emit GoldSold(amount, output, execPrice);

  emit TradeExecuted(PAXG, USDC, amount, output, execPrice,
  block.timestamp);

  }

  /// @dev Executes a buy order for gold (USDC -> PAXG).

  /// @param amount Amount of USDC to spend.

  /// @param minGold Minimum PAXG to receive (computed with slippage
  guard).

  function buyGold(uint256 amount, uint256 minGold) internal {

  require(amount > 0, "Amount must be positive");

  // 1. Get current oracle price (canonical multi-source gold price,
  §17)

  uint256 oraclePrice = getGoldPrice();

  // 2. Compute minimum output (slippage guard)

  uint256 oracleGold = (amount * 1e18) / oraclePrice;

  uint256 minGold_ = (oracleGold * (1e18 - slippageBuyGold)) / 1e18;

  require(minGold >= minGold_, "Slippage too high (buy)");

  // 3. Get quote from aggregator

  (uint256 execPrice, uint256 output) = getQuote(USDC, PAXG, amount,
  AGGREGATOR);

  // 4. Validate execPrice <= oraclePrice * (1 + tau_buy)

  uint256 maxValidPrice = (oraclePrice * (1e18 + slippageBuyGold)) /
  1e18;

  require(execPrice <= maxValidPrice, "Slippage violation (buy)");

  if (execPrice > oraclePrice) {

  emit SlippageWarning("BUY_GOLD", oraclePrice, execPrice,
  block.timestamp);

  }

  // 5. Execute trade via aggregator (with deadline)

  uint256 deadline = block.timestamp + TRADE_DEADLINE;

  bytes memory swapData = encodeSwapData(

  USDC, PAXG, amount, minGold, address(this), deadline

  );

  // 6. Submit via private mempool (Flashbots)

  submitTradeWithFlashbots(swapData, deadline);

  // 7. Update reserve state (reserve manager, §14)

  updateReserveState();

  emit GoldBought(amount, output, execPrice);

  emit TradeExecuted(USDC, PAXG, amount, output, execPrice,
  block.timestamp);

  }

  /// @dev Fetches a quote from the DEX aggregator (§12.2).

  function getQuote(

  address tokenIn,

  address tokenOut,

  uint256 amount,

  address aggregator

  ) internal view returns (uint256 execPrice, uint256 output) {

  (execPrice, output) = IAggregator(aggregator).quote(tokenIn, tokenOut,
  amount);

  }

  /// @notice Liquidity-aware trade cap (§12.4.1):
