  /// TradeSize_max = min(RequiredTrade, maxPoolFraction *
  PoolDepth_24h).

  function capTradeSize(uint256 requiredTrade, uint256 poolDepth24h)

  external view returns (uint256)

  {

  uint256 cap = (maxPoolFraction * poolDepth24h) / 1e18;

  return requiredTrade < cap ? requiredTrade : cap;

  }

  /// @dev Private-mempool submission (§12.6; Flashbots Protect / MEV
  Blocker).

  function submitTradeWithFlashbots(bytes memory swapData, uint256
  deadline) internal {

  // Encode the transaction with a private bundle

  bytes memory bundle = encodeBundle(swapData);

  // Submit to the private relay

  (bool success, ) = FLASHBOTS_RELAY.call(bundle);

  require(success, "Flashbots submission failed");

  require(block.timestamp <= deadline, "Trade deadline exceeded");

  }

  /// @dev Canonical gold price from the multi-source oracle (§17).

  /// Staleness, confidence-quorum and deviation checks are enforced

  /// upstream by the oracle layer; execution does not trade on a

  /// stale or low-confidence feed.

  function getGoldPrice() internal view returns (uint256) {

  return IGoldOracle(GOLD_ORACLE).canonicalPrice();

  }

  /// @dev Reserve accounting refresh after every trade (§14 reserve
  manager).

  function updateReserveState() internal {

  IReserveManager(RESERVE_MANAGER).refresh();

  }

  // ================================================================

  // 4. MINIMUM PROFITABILITY GUARD (BP §10.8.3; §12.5 and §12.7)

  // ================================================================

  /// @dev Computes the net benefit of a trade (USD, 1e18 scale).

  /// A negative net benefit is reported as zero and therefore always

  /// fails the minProfitability check (§12.5).

  function computeNetBenefit(uint256 requiredTrade, uint256 deviation)

  internal view returns (uint256)

  {

  // 1. Reduction in deviation (in USD)

  uint256 reduction = (deviation * requiredTrade) / 1e18;

  // 2. Estimated costs (§12.7 cost model)

  uint256 estimatedCost = estimateExecutionCost(requiredTrade);

  // 3. Net benefit (underflow-safe)

  if (estimatedCost >= reduction) {

  return 0;

  }

  return reduction - estimatedCost;

  }

  /// @dev Checks whether a trade clears the minimum profitability
  threshold.

  function isTradeProfitable(uint256 requiredTrade, uint256 deviation)

  internal returns (bool)

  {

  uint256 estimatedCost = estimateExecutionCost(requiredTrade);

  uint256 netBenefit = computeNetBenefit(requiredTrade, deviation);

  bool profitable = netBenefit > minProfitability;

  if (!profitable) {

  emit TradeSkipped("Not profitable", deviation, estimatedCost,
  netBenefit);

  }

  return profitable;

  }

  /// @notice Estimated execution cost (§12.7): gas + half-slippage +
  router fee.

  function estimateExecutionCost(uint256 tradeValue)

  public view returns (uint256)

  {

  // Gas: price with 20% buffer, units with 10% L2-variance buffer,

  // converted to the USD numeraire at the ETH/USD feed (§12.7).

  uint256 gasPrice = (oracleGasPrice() * 12) / 10; // x 1.2

  uint256 gasUnits = (ESTIMATED_GAS * 11) / 10; // x 1.1

  uint256 gasCost = (gasPrice * gasUnits * ETH_USD) / 1e18;

  // Slippage: half of the applicable tolerance for estimation

  // (direction-appropriate tau is selected by the caller in
  production).

  uint256 slippage = ((tradeValue * slippageSellGold) / 2) / 1e18;

  // Router fee: 0.1% aggregator fee

  uint256 routerFee = tradeValue / 1000;

  return gasCost + slippage + routerFee;

  }

  /// @dev Live gas price with a conservative fallback (§12.7).

  function oracleGasPrice() internal view returns (uint256) {

  return block.basefee > 0 ? block.basefee : 50 gwei;

  }

  // ================================================================

  // 5. RISK-COUNCIL PARAMETER GOVERNANCE (§12.3.3, §12.9)

  // ================================================================

  /// @notice Adjusts slippage tolerances, the liquidity cap and the

  /// profitability threshold (Risk Council 4/7; published per §22.3).

  function setExecutionParameters(

  uint256 newSlippageSellGold,

  uint256 newSlippageBuyGold,

  uint256 newSlippageSellStable,

  uint256 newSlippageBuyStable,

  uint256 newMaxPoolFraction,

  uint256 newMinProfitability

  ) external onlyRiskCouncil {

  slippageSellGold = newSlippageSellGold;

  slippageBuyGold = newSlippageBuyGold;

  slippageSellStable = newSlippageSellStable;

  slippageBuyStable = newSlippageBuyStable;

  maxPoolFraction = newMaxPoolFraction;

  minProfitability = newMinProfitability;

  emit ExecutionParametersChanged(

  newSlippageSellGold,

  newSlippageBuyGold,

  newSlippageSellStable,

  newSlippageBuyStable,

  newMaxPoolFraction,

  newMinProfitability

  );

  }

  // ================================================================

  // 6. AGGREGATOR-SPECIFIC HOOKS (developer action, §12.9)

  // ================================================================

  /// @dev Encodes the aggregator swap calldata. Implemented per
  aggregator

  /// API (1inch / Paraswap) in the deployment repository.

  function encodeSwapData(

  address tokenIn,

  address tokenOut,

  uint256 amount,

  uint256 minOut,

  address recipient,

  uint256 deadline

  ) internal virtual returns (bytes memory);

  /// @dev Wraps swap calldata in a private-mempool bundle.

  function encodeBundle(bytes memory swapData)

  internal virtual returns (bytes memory);

  }

  interface IAggregator {

  function quote(address tokenIn, address tokenOut, uint256 amount)

  external view returns (uint256 execPrice, uint256 output);

  }

  interface IGoldOracle {

  function canonicalPrice() external view returns (uint256);

  }

  interface IReserveManager {

  function refresh() external;

  }

  Fidelity notes (Listing 5 adapts BP §10.8): (i) slippage tolerances,
  the liquidity cap and the minimum profitability threshold are
  compile-time constants in BP §10.8.1 but Risk-Council-adjustable per
  BP §10.3.3 and §10.10 — implemented here as governed parameters
  initialized to the BP values; (ii) the trade deadline remains an
  immutable constant (constitutional per BP §10.10); (iii)
  getGoldPrice() reads the canonical multi-source gold oracle of Chapter
  17, not a single AMM price, and updateReserveState() delegates to the
  Chapter 14 reserve manager; (iv) the aggregator, relay and token
  addresses are constructor-set immutables per deployment (BP listed
  placeholder constants), with testnet values in Appendix D; (v) the
  aggregator quote is typed through the IAggregator interface rather
  than BP's raw staticcall encoding, and call signatures are harmonized
  so the deadline flows through the private-submission path; (vi)
  encodeSwapData() and encodeBundle() remain aggregator-specific
  developer actions (Section 12.9).

12.9 Event Emissions and Constants Summary

Every execution-layer decision is observable. The events of BP §10.9 are
carried in Listing 5 and emitted as follows:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Event                   Emitted When            Key Fields
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  GoldSold / GoldBought   A reserve gold trade    amount, output,
                          settles within its      execPrice
                          bounds                  

  TradeExecuted           Any rebalancing trade   tokenIn, tokenOut,
                          completes               amountIn, amountOut,
                                                  execPrice, timestamp

  TradeSkipped            The profitability guard reason, deviation,
                          suppresses a candidate  estimatedCost,
                          trade                   netBenefit

  SlippageWarning         A validated execution   tradeType, oraclePrice,
                          fills below (sell) or   execPrice, timestamp
                          above (buy) the oracle  
                          reference — inside the  
                          bound but not at par    
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

TradeSkipped and SlippageWarning are the calibration feed: repeated
skips at small deviations confirm that the no-trade zones are placed
correctly, and a rising frequency of warnings indicates a widening
effective spread that should feed the cost model (Section 23.11). All
events are published through the transparency layer (Chapter 24) with
methodology and data versions attached, so any observer can reconcile
published weights, decisions and executions end-to-end.

The developer-facing constants are summarized below [BP §10.10]:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Parameter         Symbol            Value             Governance
  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- -----------------
  Slippage (Sell    τ_{sell,gold}     1.0%              Risk Council
  Gold)                                                 (4/7)

  Slippage (Buy     τ_{buy,gold}      1.0%              Risk Council
  Gold)                                                 (4/7)

  Slippage          τ_{stable}        0.5%              Risk Council
  (Stablecoins)                                         (4/7)

  Liquidity Cap     —                 10% of 24h pool   Risk Council
                                      depth             (4/7)

  Min Profitability —                 $10               Risk Council
                                                        (4/7)

  Trade Deadline    —                 5 minutes         Constitutional
                                                        (immutable)

  Aggregator        —                 1inch / Paraswap  Multi-Sig (4/7)
  Address                                               
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Dependencies and developer actions carry over from the v1.2 insertion
instructions: the module requires Chapter 17 (oracles) for reference
prices and Chapters 10–11 (MARP) for trade decisions; the developer must
implement encodeSwapData() and submitTradeWithFlashbots() against the
specific aggregator API, and set the correct relay address for the
target chain. Security requirement: the aggregator address must be
immutable or governed by a 4-of-7 Multi-Sig with a 48-hour timelock to
prevent malicious upgrades [BP §10.10].

13. Reserve Architecture, Coverage and Liquidity

13.1 The Reserve Architecture Design Principle

  Reserve composition is not required to equal the index weight vector.
  The reserve is sized by obligations, not by index construction [MS
  §46].

  Reserve_(t) = f(OutstandingMTQ_(t), RedemptionRisk_(t), Liquidity_(t),
  CollateralRequirements_(t), GoldRequirements_(t))

The reference basket and the reserve answer different questions. The
basket (Chapters 3–7) defines what one unit of MTQ Σ represents — a
purchasing-power unit over an adaptive set of currencies and gold. The
reserve (this chapter, with valuation in Chapter 14) determines which
eligible assets are actually held to satisfy the obligations that
definition creates. Because the two problems have different objectives
and different constraints, their solutions coincide only by coincidence,
and the architecture never assumes coincidence:

W^(Index) ≠ W^(Reserve) in general (Section 2.4)

  --------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Decision Input             What It Measures        Where It Is Observed
  -------------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  OutstandingMTQ_t           Scale of obligations:   Chapter 19
                             circulating supply      (mint/redeem); Listing
                             valued at the reference 1 (getLiability)
                             price, plus any other   
                             contractual             
                             liabilities.            

  RedemptionRisk_t           Observed and            Section 13.4 (stress
                             stress-modeled          demand); Section 23.11
                             redemption flows        
                             against the reserve.    

  Liquidity_t                Settlement capacity of  Section 13.4 (LCR);
                             eligible instruments —  Chapter 12 (execution
                             how fast holdings can   depth)
                             become redemption-ready 
                             value.                  

  CollateralRequirements_t   Haircut-adjusted        Chapter 14 (haircuts,
                             backing needed to keep  RR tiers)
                             coverage above its      
                             floor.                  

  GoldRequirements_t         Gold-linked obligations Sections 13.3 and 13.5
                             and the strategic gold  
                             decision.               
  --------------------------------------------------------------------------

A replication portfolio — holding exactly W_t^{Index} — would import the
index's risk profile into the backing of obligations. It would, for
example, force the reserve to hold 26% gold whenever the index weight is
26%, regardless of gold's settlement liquidity (0% LCR-liquid, Section
13.4), regardless of custody conditions, and regardless of the
composition of actual redemption demand. The reserve is instead managed
as a liability-matching portfolio: the reserve manager solves a
constrained optimization — minimizing reserve risk, liquidity risk,
transaction cost and concentration risk, subject to CoverageRatio ≥
100%, LiquidityCoverage ≥ LCR_min and custody/concentration constraints
— specified with the reserve manager contract in Chapter 14 [MS §58].

  Lineage [MS §46]: this principle is the reserve-side statement of the
  two-system architecture of Section 2.4. Execution of reserve trades
  flows through the Chapter 12 execution layer; instrument eligibility
  through Chapter 15; valuation, haircuts and reserve-ratio tiers
  through Chapter 14; obligation accounting through Chapter 19.

13.2 Reserve Coverage

Coverage is the solvency constraint: every unit of MTQ Σ obligation must
be backed by at least a full unit of conservatively valued reserve [MS
§47]. The coverage ratio is:

  CoverageRatio_(t) =
  (EligibleReserveNAV_(t))/(OutstandingMTQObligations_(t))

CoverageRatio_(t) ≥ 1 (100%)

with a separately determined operational/stress buffer above the floor
[MS §47]. Both terms of the ratio are defined conservatively rather than
optimistically, which is the whole substance of the measure — the
questions 'how much is enough' and 'what counts' are answered by
liquidation logic, not by book-value optimism.

13.2.1 What Counts: The Eligible Reserve

The numerator counts only the eligible reserve: assets admitted under
the Asset Admission Registry (Chapter 15) and valued after haircuts
(Section 14.1):

EligibleReserveNAV_(t) = V_(net,t) = ∑_(j) Q_(j,t) · P_(j,t) · (1 -
H_(j))

Haircuts H_j are conservative realizable-value discounts — an asset that
would move its market by 10% if sold contributes at most 90 cents per
dollar of face value to coverage. Coverage is therefore a promise
measured in liquidation value, not in face value: it answers the
question of what the reserve would actually realize if obligations had
to be met today. An asset that is valuable but unadmitted, or admitted
but freshly impaired, contributes nothing or less than face — by design.

13.2.2 What Counts: Outstanding Obligations

The denominator is the redemption liability: circulating supply valued
at the MTQ reference price, together with any gold-linked or other
contractual obligations if such instruments exist (Section 13.5). It is
recomputed continuously after every mint, redeem, rebalance and price
update, and it is the same liability measure that drives the reserve
ratio (Section 14.2) and the stress redemption demand (Section 13.4) —
one definition of obligation, consumed by every downstream constraint.

13.2.3 How Much Is Enough

The floor is 100% — the constitutional invariant that MTQ Σ obligations
are never fractionally backed (invariant I2, Section 2.6). Above the
floor, the architecture requires two distinct cushions. The operational
buffer is the managed surplus of normal operation: the reserve-ratio
target of 110% with a stress floor of 105% (Section 14.2, Listing 1).
The stress buffer is the additional coverage needed to keep
CoverageRatio ≥ 1 under stressed haircuts and stressed redemption demand
simultaneously — a quantity that cannot be asserted, only measured,
which is why it is separately determined through the reserve stress
tests of Section 23.11 [MS §47]. Coverage is designed at the stress
point, not at the comfortable point: a reserve that covers obligations
only when markets are calm is under-covered by definition.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Measure           v1.2 Term         v2.0 Term                     Relationship
  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------------------- -----------------
  Backing adequacy  RR_t = NAV_t /    CoverageRatio_t =             CoverageRatio ≤
                    Liability_t       EligibleReserveNAV_t /        RR when haircuts
                    (gross values)    OutstandingMTQObligations_t   are positive; the
                                      (haircut-adjusted)            constitutional
                                                                    requirement binds
                                                                    on the eligible
                                                                    measure.

  Operating target  rrTarget = 110%   rrTarget = 110% (Listing 1)   Unchanged.

  Stress floor      rrStressFloor =   rrStressFloor = 105% (Section Unchanged;
                    105%              14.2)                         DEFENSIVE
                                                                    trigger.

  Solvency floor    RR_HARD_FLOOR =   CoverageRatio ≥ 1.00          Same rule, stated
                    100%              (invariant I2)                on the
                                                                    conservative
                                                                    measure.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------------------

  The COO review endorsed the coverage ratio as sound — 'Good.' — but
  flagged the missing companion requirement, liquidity coverage, as 'one
  of the most important omissions' from the pasted version [COO-19].
  Section 13.4 closes that omission. Solvency without liquidity is a
  solvency that cannot be delivered on time.

13.3 Gold Reserve and Gold Index Allocation Are Separate

  W_(Gold)^(Index) ≠ W_(Gold)^(Reserve)

This separation is mandatory [MS §49]. A 26% gold reference allocation
does not mean the reserve must hold 26% gold — and no communication,
parameter or contract state may assume otherwise. The rule is the
reserve-side half of constitutional invariant I12 (Section 2.6): the
gold reference allocation and the gold reserve allocation are separate,
and no reserve asset may be used for secondary-market price support (the
second half is Section 13.6).

The two gold quantities are produced by different engines for different
purposes. W_{Gold}^{Index} is an output of MASE: gold's adaptive
allocation is computed from its stability, diversification,
purchasing-power and crisis characteristics (Section 3.3), bounded by
the 20–32% validation envelope (Section 8.1), and it exists to define
the unit. W_{Gold}^{Reserve} is an output of the reserve engine: the
gold holding is sized by redemption risk, settlement liquidity, custody
risk and collateral requirements (Sections 13.1 and 13.5), and it exists
to back obligations. Neither is an input to the other — the gold index
weight is not a reserve target, and the reserve holding is not evidence
about the index.

Three consequences follow. First, communication discipline: quoting the
index gold weight as the reserve gold holding is a category error,
exactly as quoting any fixed percentage as the composition of MTQ Σ is a
category error (Section 3.1). Second, movement independence: the index
gold weight may traverse its envelope without automatically triggering
any reserve trade — a reserve trade must independently justify itself
through the reserve engine and the MARP execution gates (Chapters
10–12), so index-driven drift never becomes forced reserve turnover.
Third, stress asymmetry: in a redemption surge the reserve shifts toward
immediately liquid assets — potentially away from gold, whose LCR
liquidity class is 0% (Section 13.4) — at exactly the time the index may
be increasing gold's weight for crisis diversification (Section 5.5).
Both movements are correct, because they answer different questions.

  Pinned section — referenced from invariant I12 (Section 2.6).
  Instrument eligibility for whatever gold the reserve does hold is
  governed by Chapter 15 (Section 15.8); the gold price reference for
  the index is governed by Chapter 17; the sizing methodology is
  specified in Section 13.5.

13.4 Reserve Liquidity Coverage

  Solvency does not guarantee liquidity. A reserve can be fully covered
  on a haircut-adjusted basis and still be unable to meet a sudden surge
  of redemptions, because its assets cannot be settled quickly enough
  [BP §4.3]. The liquidity coverage requirement is the second
  constitutional pillar of the reserve architecture.

The COO review of the modification specification identified exactly this
gap: the pasted version carried the coverage ratio but not the liquidity
requirement, calling it 'one of the most important omissions' [COO-19].
The requirement is therefore stated as a first-class constitutional
constraint [MS §48, COO-19]:

  LiquidityCoverage_(t) =
  (ImmediatelyLiquidEligibleAssets_(t))/(NearTermObligations_(t)) ≥
  LCR_(min)

where LCR_{min} is determined through stress testing [MS §48]. The
requirement answers a time dimension that CoverageRatio cannot see:
coverage asks whether obligations are backed by value; liquidity
coverage asks whether that value can arrive before the obligations do.

13.4.1 What Counts as Immediately Liquid

Immediately liquid eligible assets are the portion of the reserve that
can be settled within 24 hours without incurring excessive slippage
(more than 0.5%) [BP §4.3.1]:

LiquidAssets_(t) = ∑_(j ∈ Stablecoins) Q_(j,t) × P_(j,t) × (1 - H_(j))

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Asset Class             LCR Liquidity Class     Rationale
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Stablecoins (USDC,      100% liquid             Deep 24-hour
  EURC)                                           settlement; tight
                                                  spreads (τ = 0.5%).

  Tokenized Gold (PAXG)   0% liquid               Gold markets can be
                                                  closed on weekends; DEX
                                                  liquidity can
                                                  evaporate.

  BUIDL / Tokenized       0% liquid unless        Settlement windows
  T-Bills                 approved                exceed 24 hours;
                                                  specific instruments
                                                  may be approved by the
                                                  Risk Council.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The classification is deliberately unforgiving: gold is counted at zero
despite being a deep, globally traded asset class, because the LCR
measures worst-case convertibility on a 24-hour clock, and weekend
closures and DEX liquidity evaporation are exactly the conditions under
which the ratio is needed. In v2.0 the class assignment is an admission
attribute of the Asset Admission Registry (Chapter 15) rather than a
hard-coded list: an instrument that demonstrates 24-hour settlement with
bounded slippage under stress can be admitted to the liquid class by
governance — the tokenized-T-bill approval path of BP §4.3.1 is the same
rule stated as an exception rather than as a general mechanism.

13.4.2 Near-Term Obligations and Stress Redemption Demand

Near-term obligations NTO_t are the obligations that could come due
within the stress horizon, and they are defined by stress testing rather
than by the current redemption rate [MS §48]. The operational definition
from the v1.2 architecture is the stress redemption demand: the
estimated total USD value that could be redeemed within a 30-day stress
period [BP §4.3.2]:

  StressRedemptionDemand_(t) = S_(circ,t) × P_(MTQ,t) × 0.25

The 25% assumption holds that under extreme market conditions up to a
quarter of the circulating supply could be redeemed within 30 days — a
conservative figure based on historical stablecoin depeg scenarios [BP
§4.3.2]. The pair (25%, 30 days) is the initial parameterization: the
stress tests of Section 23.11 recalibrate both the fraction and the
horizon against observed redemption behavior, and NTO_t additionally
includes near-term operational obligations — fees, execution budgets and
known costs — where they are material. The stress horizon and the
24-hour settlement clock of Section 13.4.1 are deliberately different
clocks: the liquid buffer must cover the front of the redemption wave,
and the reserve engine's re-routing capacity (Section 13.4.3) is what
bridges the remainder of the horizon.

13.4.3 The Constitutional Floor and Breach Behavior

LCR_(t) ≥ 1.00

If LCR_t < 1.00, three responses engage automatically [BP §4.3.3]:

1.  The protocol enters DEFENSIVE mode — the risk-state machine of
    Chapter 21 re-rates protocol posture and publishes the state change.

2.  Rebalancing is re-routed to increase the liquid asset allocation —
    sell gold for stablecoins — until LCR returns above 1.00.

3.  Minting is paused until LCR recovers; redemption remains available
    (Chapter 19).

The repair trades are executed through the Chapter 12 execution layer:
they remain quote-based, slippage-bounded and liquidity-capped, because
a liquidity repair executed at unbounded cost would repair the liquidity
ratio by damaging the coverage ratio. Defensive rebalancing is
obligation-driven, not price-driven — it is categorically distinct from
the market-price support prohibited in Section 13.6.

The on-chain measure is the getLCR() function of Listing 1, which
computes reserveState.liquidValue (the net stablecoin value) over
exactly the BP stress demand — circulating supply times price times 25%
— and is therefore a direct implementation of LiquidityCoverage with
LIQ_t equal to the stablecoin NAV after haircuts and NTO_t equal to
StressRedemptionDemand_t. The generalized definitions of this section
govern interpretation: the getter is the canonical on-chain measure, the
constitutional floor is 1.00, and the operational target (lcrTarget in
Listing 1, a Risk-Council parameter initialized at 1.00) may be set
above the floor following stress-test results. Widening the numerator
beyond the stablecoin class is a governance act through Chapter 15
admission, not a contract-level reinterpretation.

  Worked example (full arithmetic in Appendix C): with circulating
  supply 10,000,000 MTQ Σ at a reference price of $1.05, obligations are
  $10.5M and NTO_t = 0.25 × $10.5M = $2.625M. If the reserve holds
  $2.80M of stablecoins net of haircuts, LiquidityCoverage = 2.80 /
  2.625 ≈ 1.067 ≥ 1.00 — normal operation. If stablecoin holdings fall
  to $2.50M (a redemption wave without re-routing), LiquidityCoverage ≈
  0.952 < 1.00 — DEFENSIVE mode, minting paused, and rebalancing
  re-routed toward liquid assets until the ratio recovers.

13.5 Strategic Gold Reserve Sizing

The strategic gold reserve is sized by decision inputs, not by a fixed
percentage [MS §50]:

  R_(G) = f(MTQ obligations, GoldLinked obligations, Liquidity, Stress,
  Custody, Redemption)

— not simply R_G = 26%. The v1.2 figure of 26% (more precisely, the
26.25% total that its core-plus-buffer structure produced in the BASE
state, Section 13.5.2) was the output of one parameterization under one
set of conditions, and quoting it as the reserve gold allocation would
repeat the fixed-weighting error of Section 3.1 on the reserve side. The
reserve gold holding is recomputed by the reserve engine against the
constraints of Sections 13.2 and 13.4.

13.5.1 The Decision Inputs

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Input                               How It Pushes the Gold Decision
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  MTQ obligations                     Sets the scale: the total backing
                                      requirement that the gold holding
                                      shares with every other reserve
                                      asset.

  Gold-linked obligations             If a gold-redeemable instrument
                                      exists (the historical SGTX-G
                                      concept, usable as design input
                                      only — Section 4.8), its
                                      obligations create a hard floor on
                                      gold holdings.

  Liquidity                           Gold's 0% LCR liquidity class
                                      (Section 13.4) means every
                                      additional unit of gold dilutes
                                      liquidity coverage; the LCR floor
                                      bounds gold from above.

  Stress                              Gold's diversification behavior
                                      under stress argues for holding
                                      more; redemption-surge behavior
                                      argues for holding less. The stress
                                      tests of Section 23.11 price this
                                      trade-off.

  Custody                             Issuer, counterparty and storage
                                      risk of the specific gold
                                      instruments — tokenized versus
                                      allocated physical (Section 13.5.3)
                                      — scale the haircut and the
                                      admissible share.

  Redemption                          The composition of observed
                                      redemption demand: redemptions
                                      settled in liquid assets do not
                                      require gold settlement capacity,
                                      so redemption behavior directly
                                      reduces the gold the reserve needs.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Two constraints deserve emphasis. First, gold is bounded from above by
the liquidity coverage requirement — a reserve cannot solve its solvency
problem with gold at the cost of failing its liquidity problem. Second,
strategic gold is not an everyday trading wallet: ordinary rebalancing
and execution costs are paid from execution budgets, ordinary reserve
operations and natural cash flows, and gold liquidation requires a
defined exceptional condition — the reserve operations rules of Chapter
14 [MS §59].

13.5.2 Historical Input — the v1.2 Core-plus-Buffer Structure

The v1.2 architecture decomposed the reserve into a core and an actively
managed buffer [BP §8]. The core reserve provides the 100% liability
coverage and is never touched for profit; the buffer is an additional
10% of excess collateral, managed as a defense layer whose gold ratio
moves with the reserve ratio. The core held 20% gold (CORE_GOLD_WEIGHT),
and the buffer's gold ratio B_{gold}(RR_t) took three state-dependent
values:

V_(net,t) = V_(core,t) + V_(buffer,t) V_(core,t) = S_(circ,t) ×
P_(MTQ,t) V_(buffer,t) = 0.10 × S_(circ,t) × P_(MTQ,t)

V_(buffer,gold,t) = V_(buffer,t) × B_(gold)(RR_(t)) V_(buffer,fiat,t) =
V_(buffer,t) × (1 - B_(gold)(RR_(t)))

W_(gold)^(total) = 0.20 + 0.10 × B_(gold)(RR_(t))

  -------------------------------------------------------------------------
  State       RR Range    B_gold      Buffer Fiat Total       Description
                                                  Reserve     
                                                  Gold        
  ----------- ----------- ----------- ----------- ----------- -------------
  BASE        RR ≥ 1.10   62.5%       37.5%       26.25%      Normal
  (Normal)                                                    operations;
                                                              split derived
                                                              from
                                                              simulations
                                                              (Pareto
                                                              frontier).

  STRESS      1.05 ≤ RR < 85%         15%         28.5%       Defensive
              1.10                                            posture;
                                                              minting
                                                              throttled to
                                                              50% capacity.

  EMERGENCY   RR < 1.05   100%        0%          30.0%       Maximum
                                                              defense;
                                                              minting
                                                              paused;
                                                              emergency
                                                              rebalancing
                                                              triggered;
                                                              redemption
                                                              allowed with
                                                              increased
                                                              fee.
  -------------------------------------------------------------------------

Transitions between BASE and STRESS ramp linearly over 24 hours to avoid
market impact; the transition into EMERGENCY jumps immediately. The
structure caps total reserve gold at 30% — the buffer never makes the
reserve 100% gold — and orders loss absorption as a first-loss
waterfall: operational surplus first, then buffer fiat, buffer gold,
core fiat, and core gold last, with core-gold consumption protected by
an Exhaustion Certificate requiring a 4/7 Multi-Sig. In v2.0 this
structure is retained as historical input and candidate policy: it
demonstrates the correct shape — a stress-conditional gold policy with
RR-triggered layers, capped exposure and a protected core — but its
specific numbers are validation-stage values, to be re-derived under the
Section 13.5.1 inputs and the Section 23.11 stress tests rather than
quoted as the reserve's definition. The operational reserve-ratio tiers
and the reserve manager contract that executes this policy live in
Chapter 14.

13.5.3 Instrument Eligibility — Tokenized and Physical Gold

Tokenized gold may be an eligible implementation instrument for reserve
gold exposure [MS §51]. Eligibility is scored per instrument j:

E_(j) = f(UnderlyingGoldCoverage, Custody, Redemption, IssuerRisk,
Liquidity, Auditability, LegalRights)

E_(j) ≥ E_(min)

Only instruments meeting the threshold can enter the reserve. The full
admission machinery — scoring weights, thresholds and registry state —
is the Asset Admission framework of Chapter 15 (Section 15.8); the
reserve consumes its outputs. Physical gold is the comparison case:
where legally and operationally appropriate, allocated physical bullion
provides a different risk profile from an issuer-dependent token, and
the reserve engine must be able to compare PhysicalGold against
TokenizedPhysicalGold using the same reserve-risk framework — the same
inputs (custody, liquidity, auditability, legal rights), the same
haircuts and the same LCR class [MS §52].

  Tokenized gold is an implementation and collateral instrument, not the
  definition of gold [MS §51].

That sentence closes the loop with the reference basket. The index's
gold exposure is to the gold price reference unit (Section 17.4),
sourced from the canonical multi-source oracle (Chapter 17), not to any
token; the reserve's gold exposure is to whatever eligible instrument
best carries it under the Section 13.5.1 decision inputs. Either side
may change instruments or weights without renegotiating the other — the
same separation stated three times: Section 2.4 in general, Section 13.3
for gold specifically, and here at the instrument level.

13.6 No Market-Price Support

  MTQ Σ must not use reserve assets to guarantee a secondary-market
  price, to engineer appreciation, to conduct discretionary buybacks, or
  to create an artificial floor. The reserve protects system
  obligations, not speculative market pricing [MS §61].

The prohibition is absolute and explicit. The reserve must never be used
to:

-   Guarantee a secondary-market price of MTQ Σ.

-   Engineer appreciation of the MTQ Σ market price.

-   Conduct discretionary buybacks of MTQ Σ.

-   Create an artificial floor under the MTQ Σ market price.

Three arguments ground the rule. First, coverage erosion: every unit of
reserve committed to defending a market price is a unit not backing an
obligation, so price support consumes the CoverageRatio and
LiquidityCoverage budgets of Sections 13.2 and 13.4 without improving
either — the reserve would be weakest exactly while it is being spent.
Second, implicit-promise hazard: a defended price converts a
methodology-defined unit into a discretionary liability. The value of
MTQ Σ derives from the index (Chapter 9) and from mint/redeem
equivalence at index value (Chapter 19), not from a defended market
quote — the denomination is a normalization choice, not a price promise
(Section 3.5). Third, incompatibility with adaptive weights: the
basket's weights float by design (Chapters 3–8); there is no fixed price
level to defend, and any attempted floor would silently re-encode the
fixed-quantity assumption the constitution has abandoned.

The rule draws a sharp line between system-integrity controls and market
intervention. The crisis framework — the crisis score (Section 5.5),
crisis mode (Section 14.4) and the DEFENSIVE/CRISIS risk states of
Chapter 21 — exists to protect CoverageRatio, LiquidityCoverage and
settlement capacity: redemption fees, mint throttles and pauses, LCR
repair rebalancing (Section 13.4.3) and execution-size limits are all
obligation-driven, and none of them trades MTQ Σ in the secondary
market. Market-price discipline is provided by the permissionless
primary market instead: because minting and redemption occur at index
value (Chapter 19), any participant can arbitrage a secondary-market
deviation — a mechanism, not an intervention, and one that costs the
reserve nothing. The previous architecture's prohibition on buybacks and
price support remains appropriate and is retained unchanged [MS §61].

  Pinned section — the second clause of invariant I12 (Section 2.6,
  alongside Section 13.3). Cross-references: crisis mode (Section 14.4),
  risk states (Chapter 21), the primary market (Chapter 19), and
  publication of every defensive action with reason codes (Chapter 24).

14. Reserve Valuation and Risk Management

14.1 Net Asset Value — Prudential Valuation

  The protocol does not use the gross market value of its reserve to
  determine solvency. Every reserve instrument is carried at a
  prudential value — its oracle-consensus price less an asset-specific
  haircut — so that the balance sheet already anticipates the frictions
  of an actual liquidation before they are discovered [BP §4.1].

A haircut H_j is a fixed safety discount applied to asset j that
accounts for settlement risk, custody risk, and potential depeg or
slippage during liquidation. Gross valuations systematically overstate
what a reserve could actually deliver under stress, because the price at
which an asset marks is rarely the price at which it clears when many
sellers are transacting at once. Prudential valuation internalizes that
gap at measurement time rather than at trade time. The net asset value
of the reserve at time t is:

  V_(net,t) = ∑_(j=1)^(n) Q_(j,t) × P_(j,t) × (1 - H_(j))

where Q_{j,t} is the quantity of reserve asset j (for example 10,000
USDC, or 1.5 PAXG), P_{j,t} is the current USD price of asset j from the
multi-source oracle consensus (Chapter 17), and H_j is the fixed haircut
for asset j. This definition is constitutional: invariant I4 (Section
2.6) requires that V_{net,t} is always calculated with asset-specific
haircuts, and the core variable table of Section 2.5 carries V_{net,t}
with exactly this expression. Any figure published as the reserve value
that omits the haircut term is, by definition, not the protocol's NAV.

For gold instruments the valuation is deliberately conservative. The
protocol uses the more conservative of the two gold prices for NAV
calculation — the reference price (LBMA spot consensus) versus the
executable price (PAXG market price) [BP §4.1]:

P_(gold,t) = min(P_(gold,reference), P_(gold,executable))

This min-of-two rule avoids overvaluing gold during thin liquidity, when
the executable price can fall below the reference spot price, and it
composes with the canonical gold-price aggregation of Section 17.4. The
same conservatism appears again in the liquidity framework: tokenized
gold is treated as 0% liquid for LCR purposes (Section 13.4), so gold is
marked carefully and counted cautiously at every layer of the balance
sheet.

14.1.1 The Constitutional Haircut Table

The haircut schedule below is the v1.2 constitutional table, retained as
the default calibration [BP §4.1.1]. The haircuts are immutable by
default but can be adjusted by the Risk Council (4-of-7 Multi-Sig,
24-hour timelock) if the risk profile of an asset changes materially —
for example, if an issuer's redemption window lengthens or an audit
finds custody weaknesses. Haircut calibration is itself a stress-test
subject: the validation program (Section 23.11) checks that each H_j
covers realized liquidation costs in historical depeg and fire-sale
scenarios.

  -------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Asset Class       Asset Example       Haircut (H_j)     Rationale
  ----------------- ------------------- ----------------- -----------------
  USD Stablecoin    USDC, USDP          0.5%              Extremely liquid;
                                                          regulated;
                                                          minimal depeg
                                                          risk.

  EUR Stablecoin    EURC                0.7%              Highly liquid;
                                                          slightly lower
                                                          market depth than
                                                          USDC.

  GBP Stablecoin    Registry-resolved   1.0%              Lower on-chain
                                                          liquidity; higher
                                                          conversion
                                                          friction.

  JPY Stablecoin    Registry-resolved   1.0%              Lower on-chain
                                                          liquidity; higher
                                                          conversion
                                                          friction.

  CNY Stablecoin    Registry-resolved   1.5%              Limited
                    (offshore CNH)                        liquidity;
                                                          offshore market
                                                          constraints.

  Tokenized Gold    PAXG, XAUT          1.0%              Custody spread
                                                          (0.5%) + DEX
                                                          slippage (0.5%)
                                                          on sale.

  Tokenized T-Bills BUIDL (if approved) 2.0%              Interest-rate
                                                          risk; redemption
                                                          delay risk.
  -------------------------------------------------------------------------

Two implementation rules attach to this table. First, when a new asset
is added via the Asset Admission Registry (Chapter 15), its haircut must
be approved simultaneously by the Risk Council — an asset can never
enter the reserve unpriced, and a haircut can never be attached after
the fact to a holding that was admitted under different assumptions.
Second, the haircut feeds both solvency and liquidity measurement: the
same H_j that reduces NAV also reduces the liquid-asset computation of
Section 13.4, so a deteriorating instrument is automatically
double-counted conservatively rather than being discovered once and
ignored twice.

14.2 The Reserve Ratio and Constitutional Tiers

The Reserve Ratio (RR) is the primary solvency metric. It ensures that
the total USD value of the vault — net of haircuts — is sufficient to
cover the total USD liability [BP §4.2]:

  RR_(t) = (V_(net,t))/(L_(t))

where V_{net,t} is the Net Asset Value from Section 14.1 and L_t is the
total liability, L_t = S_{circ,t} × P_{MTQ,t}, defined in Section 2.5
and computed by the core contract (Listing 1). Because the numerator is
prudential (haircut) and the denominator is marked to the adaptive index
price, RR_t is a conservative solvency measure: it can only be inflated
by overstating prices, which the oracle layer (Chapter 17) and the
min-of-two gold rule are designed to prevent. Invariant I2 (Section 2.6)
makes the hard floor constitutional — RR_t ≥ 1.00 at all times, enforced
by the reserve manager and the governance layer (Chapter 22).

14.2.1 The Three Floors

The protocol operates with three distinct thresholds that trigger
progressively stricter actions [BP §4.2.1]. The three-floor design
separates the ordinary operating buffer from the defensive posture and
the never-breach boundary, so that deterioration is met with
proportionate, pre-committed responses rather than improvisation.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Tier              Symbol            Value             Operational
                                                        Meaning
  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- -----------------
  TARGET            RR_target         1.10 (110%)       Normal operating
                                                        buffer. Protocol
                                                        aims to stay at
                                                        or above this.

  STRESS FLOOR      RR_stress         1.05 (105%)       Defensive
                                                        posture: minting
                                                        throttled;
                                                        rebalancing
                                                        prioritized.

  HARD SOLVENCY     RR_hard           1.00 (100%)       Must never
  FLOOR                                                 breach. If
                                                        breached,
                                                        redemptions are
                                                        paused, and the
                                                        protocol enters
                                                        Emergency
                                                        Recovery.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

14.2.2 Status Determination

The protocol computes the current status from the RR_t and LCR_t values
(Section 13.4) to drive the state machine (Chapter 18). Status is a
function of both solvency and liquidity: a fully solvent but illiquid
vault is already in a defensive condition, because a redemption surge
cannot be met even though the assets exist. The determination logic is
[BP §4.2.2]:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Status            RR Range          LCR Requirement   Actions
  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- -----------------
  NORMAL            RR ≥ 1.10         LCR ≥ 1.00        Standard
                                                        operations; full
                                                        mint/redeem.

  CAUTION           1.05 ≤ RR < 1.10  LCR ≥ 0.90        Minting throttled
                                                        to 50%;
                                                        rebalancing
                                                        urgency
                                                        increased.

  DEFENSIVE         1.00 ≤ RR < 1.05  Any               Minting paused;
                                                        redemption fee
                                                        increased to
                                                        0.5%; emergency
                                                        rebalancing
                                                        triggered.

  EMERGENCY         RR < 1.00         Any               Redemptions
                                                        paused; circuit
                                                        breakers engaged;
                                                        governance
                                                        notified
                                                        immediately.

  RECOVERY          RR ≥ 1.05         LCR ≥ 0.95        Gradual
                    (rising)                            restoration of
                                                        minting and
                                                        standard fees.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The RECOVERY row is directional: it requires the ratio to be rising and
sustained, not merely to have crossed back above the stress floor, which
prevents status oscillation at the boundary — the same anti-thrashing
philosophy as the constituency hysteresis of Section 4.7. Redemption
fees follow the parameter registry of Section 2.9 (redeemFeeStress =
0.50%, redeemFeeDefensive = 1.00%), so the 0.5% figure in the DEFENSIVE
row is the stress-tier fee; the full defensive tier carries the higher
rate. The on-chain implementation of this table is the status getter of
Listing 6, and the complete state machine — including how each status
throttles minting, redemption and rebalancing — is specified in Chapter
18, with the risk-state model of Chapter 21 consuming the same inputs.

14.3 Tokenized and Physical Gold Instruments

Gold exposure in the reserve is implemented through instruments, and
instruments are admitted by evidence. Tokenized gold — PAXG and XAUT
being the canonical current examples — may be an eligible implementation
instrument, subject to an instrument-level eligibility score [MS §51]:

  E_(j) = f(UnderlyingGoldCoverage, Custody, Redemption, IssuerRisk,
  Liquidity, Auditability, LegalRights)

E_(j) ≥ E_(min)

Only instruments whose composite score E_j meets or exceeds the
threshold E_{min} can enter the reserve. The score is dominated in
practice by issuer risk: a tokenized-gold instrument is a claim on an
issuer's custody and redemption machinery, not on gold itself, so the
difference between a strong and weak instrument is the difference
between an audited, segregated, redeemable claim and an unaudited
promise. The eight-criteria eligibility standard of Section 15.2
operationalizes exactly these dimensions (redemption right, custody
structure, audit, liquidity, issuer authorization), and the
tokenized-gold haircut of 1.0% (Section 14.1.1) prices the residual
custody spread and DEX slippage even for admitted instruments.

  Tokenized gold is an implementation/collateral instrument, not the
  definition of gold [MS §51].

This sentence is the load-bearing distinction of the whole gold
architecture. The reference basket's gold component is defined by the
gold price reference unit (Section 17.4) and its adaptive allocation
W_{G,t} (Section 3.3); the reserve's gold holdings are chosen among
eligible instruments under Chapter 15. Invariant I12 (Section 2.6)
states the separation constitutionally: gold reference allocation and
gold reserve allocation are separate quantities, governed by separate
mechanisms. Because the earlier SGTX-G token design is not inherited
automatically (Section 4.8), a future MTQ-native physical-gold token
would have to earn admission through this same framework rather than
arriving by legacy.

Where legally and operationally appropriate, allocated physical bullion
provides a different risk profile from an issuer-dependent token [MS
§52]. Physical gold held as allocated bullion with a qualified custodian
removes token-issuer redemption risk but introduces custody, insurance,
audit and transport costs, and its liquidation is slower. The reserve
engine must therefore be able to compare:

PhysicalGold vs. TokenizedPhysicalGold

using the same reserve-risk framework — the eligibility score E_j, the
haircut H_j, the liquidity classification for LCR (Section 13.4), and
the concentration caps of Section 15.6 — rather than treating physical
and tokenized gold as categorically different asset classes. In practice
this means the choice between PAXG, XAUT and allocated bullion is a
governed implementation decision on a single exposure axis: the
framework scores each implementation's custody, redemption and liquidity
profile, applies the matching haircut, and lets the reserve optimization
(Section 14.4) allocate across them. The canonical gold price remains
source-independent (Chapter 17), so no implementation choice
contaminates the pricing layer.

14.4 Reserve Rebalancing and the Gold-Cost Rule

The reserve manager maintains the reserve against its coverage,
liquidity and concentration obligations by solving a constrained risk
minimization [MS §58]:

  min_(R) [ ReserveRisk + LiquidityRisk + TransactionCost +
  ConcentrationRisk ]

subject to CoverageRatio_(t) ≥ 100%, LiquidityCoverage_(t) ≥ LCR_(min)

and custody/concentration constraints. The coverage constraint is the
reserve-side expression of the hard solvency floor (Section 13.2;
invariant I2), and the liquidity constraint binds the solution to the
LCR definition of Section 13.4 — tokenized gold counts as illiquid, so a
solution that over-weights gold must compensate with a deeper stablecoin
liquid layer. Execution of the solution is never a block trade: every
adjustment passes through the MARP gates of Chapters 10–11, which test
whether each incremental correction is economically justified after
costs, and through the issuer concentration limits of Section 15.6,
which cap how much of the solution may concentrate in any single issuer.
The turnover penalty of Section 7.3 and the execution budgets of Chapter
11 price the TransactionCost term, so the optimizer and the executor see
the same economics.

14.4.1 The Gold-Cost Rule

Strategic gold is not an everyday trading wallet [MS §59]. Ordinary
operating costs — rebalancing spreads, gas, execution fees — must never
be funded by liquidating the gold allocation, because a reserve that
pays its grocery bill from its strategic asset converts a temporary cost
into a permanent reduction of the diversification core. Normal costs
come from:

-   execution budgets (Chapter 11), which are provisioned for exactly
    this purpose;

-   ordinary reserve operations and their fee flows;

-   transparent fees where permitted (Section 2.9);

-   natural cash flows (mint/redemption fee accrual, the operational
    surplus of Section 16.4).

Gold liquidation requires a defined exceptional condition [MS §59].
Formally:

GoldSold_(t) > 0 ⇒ ExceptionalCondition_(t) = true

The exceptional conditions are enumerated, and each is a structural
event rather than a discretionary judgment:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Exceptional Condition   Mechanism               Cross-Reference
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Methodology-driven      MASE target gold weight §7.5; Chapters 10–11
  allocation change       change executed through 
                          MARP gates.             

  Buffer state transition Defensive gold tilt of  Chapter 16
                          the dynamic buffer.     

  LCR floor breach        Liquidity-restoration   §13.4
                          rebalancing (sell gold  
                          for stablecoins) until  
                          LCR ≥ 1.00.             

  First-loss waterfall    Loss absorption         §16.4
  consumption             descending to buffer    
                          gold, and ultimately    
                          core gold.              

  Instrument rotation     Swap between eligible   Chapter 15
                          gold implementations at 
                          equal exposure (e.g.,   
                          PAXG to allocated       
                          bullion).               
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Every gold reserve change therefore requires structural justification in
the sense of this table: a methodology version, a buffer-state event, a
breached floor, a waterfall step, or a governance-admitted instrument
rotation. Instrument rotation deserves emphasis because it changes the
gold implementation without changing the gold exposure — moving from
PAXG to physical bullion is not gold liquidation and is not gated by
this rule, only by the registry and concentration framework. The rule
composes with invariant I12: the gold reserve allocation may move for
structural reasons, and never for secondary-market price purposes
(Section 13.6).

14.5 Crisis Mode

When the crisis-risk score crosses its threshold, the reserve and
execution layers shift into a deliberately more conservative posture [MS
§60]:

  CrisisScore_(t) > C_(threshold)

The CrisisScore_t is the aggregation of stress evidence defined in
Section 5.5 (FX volatility, cross-asset correlation, liquidity stress,
inflation shock, credit stress, market drawdown and geopolitical
indicators), and its threshold C_{threshold} is a Risk-Council
parameter. When it is breached, five coordinated changes take effect:

-   no-trade thresholds may widen — deviations must be larger before any
    correction trade is triggered (Section 10.8);

-   execution size may be reduced — each MARP slice shrinks, stretching
    large adjustments over more, smaller trades;

-   liquidity requirements may increase — only deeper venues and tighter
    quotes qualify for execution (Chapter 11);

-   reserve preservation becomes more important — the reserve manager
    biases toward holding rather than trading, and status-tiered
    restrictions (Section 14.2.2) bind earlier;

-   oracle confirmation requirements increase — price updates need
    stronger quorum and fresher sources before they may move valuation
    state (Chapter 17).

  --------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Execution-Layer         Normal Mode             Crisis Mode
  Parameter                                       
  ----------------------- ----------------------- --------------------------
  No-trade threshold      Standard width          Widened — fewer,
  (deviation band)                                better-justified trades

  Maximum execution size  Standard cap            Reduced — smaller market
  per slice                                       footprint

  Venue liquidity         Standard depth          Increased — only deepest
  requirement                                     venues qualify

  Oracle confirmation     Standard quorum         Additional independent
                                                  confirmations required

  Reserve posture         Target-following        Preservation-prioritized
  --------------------------------------------------------------------------

Reserve preservation as a priority means the ranking of objectives
inverts: in normal mode the reserve follows the methodology's targets
efficiently; in crisis mode it minimizes the cost of being wrong,
accepting tracking error and wider spreads as the price of not being
forced to liquidate into a dislocated market. This is a risk-control
function, not a token-price-support function [MS §60]. Crisis mode
widens safety margins; it never authorizes buybacks, price engineering
or artificial floors — the prohibition on market-price support (Section
13.6, invariant I12) applies with full force during crises, precisely
because a stressed reserve is when the temptation to support prices
would be greatest. The crisis posture is entered and exited through the
risk-state model of Chapter 21, its observable consequences are executed
through the state machine of Chapter 18, and its stress behavior is a
named scenario family in the validation program (Section 23.11).

14.6 Smart Contract Implementation — Reserve Manager

The reserve manager is the on-chain embodiment of Sections 14.1 and
14.2: it holds the haircut constants, recomputes the prudential reserve
state from balances and oracle prices, and exposes NAV, RR, LCR and the
protocol status to every other contract. The listing below adapts BP
§4.4 substantially complete: it carries the immutable haircut constants
(BP §4.4.1), the core reserve state and its update routine (BP §4.4.2),
the getter functions (BP §4.4.3) and the safety modifiers (BP §4.4.4),
plus the event emissions of BP §4.5. The LCR getter is reconciled with
the Section 13.4 definition: the numerator is the net stablecoin layer
only (tokenized gold counts 0% liquid), and the denominator uses the
25%-of-liability-in-30-days stress-demand assumption
(STRESS_REDEMPTION_RATE).

14.6.1 The Contract

Listing 6 — Reserve manager: NAV, haircuts, RR and LCR state (adapts BP
§4.4)

  // SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT

  pragma solidity ^0.8.19;

  /// @title ReserveManager — prudential NAV, reserve ratio and LCR
  state.

  /// @dev Adapts BP §4.4. Prices come from the multi-source oracle
  layer

  /// (Chapter 17); instruments from the Asset Admission Registry (Ch
  15).

  contract ReserveManager {

  // ============================================================

  // 1. IMMUTABLE HAIRCUT AND FLOOR CONSTANTS (§14.1, §14.2)

  // ============================================================

  // Haircuts scaled to 1e18 (e.g., 0.5% = 0.005e18)

  uint256 public constant HAIRCUT_USDC = 0.005e18; // USD stablecoin
  class

  uint256 public constant HAIRCUT_EURC = 0.007e18; // EUR stablecoin
  class

  uint256 public constant HAIRCUT_GBP = 0.010e18; // GBP
  registry-resolved class

  uint256 public constant HAIRCUT_JPY = 0.010e18; // JPY
  registry-resolved class

  uint256 public constant HAIRCUT_CNY = 0.015e18; // CNY offshore (CNH)
  class

  uint256 public constant HAIRCUT_PAXG = 0.010e18; // tokenized gold
  (PAXG, XAUT)

  // Constitutional floor constants (§14.2.1)

  uint256 public constant RR_TARGET = 1.10e18; // 110% target buffer

  uint256 public constant RR_STRESS = 1.05e18; // 105% stress floor

  uint256 public constant RR_HARD = 1.00e18; // 100% hard solvency floor

  uint256 public constant LCR_TARGET = 1.00e18; // liquidity coverage
  floor

  uint256 public constant STRESS_REDEMPTION_RATE = 0.25e18; // 25% of
  liability (§13.4)

  uint256 public constant RECOVERY_CONFIRMATION_PERIOD = 48 hours;

  // ============================================================

  // 2. CORE STATE (BP §4.4.2)

  // ============================================================

  struct ReserveState {

  uint256 fiatValue; // net USD value of all stablecoins (post-haircut)

  uint256 goldValue; // net USD value of tokenized gold (post-haircut)

  uint256 liquidValue; // net stablecoin value — the LCR numerator
  (§13.4)

  uint256 grossFiat; // gross stablecoin value (monitoring only)

  uint256 grossGold; // gross gold value (monitoring only)

  }

  ReserveState public reserveState;

  bool public recoveryMode; // set on EMERGENCY entry (§14.2.2)

  uint256 public recoveryStartedAt;

  // Genesis instruments; the registry (Chapter 15) is the canonical

  // source of admitted instruments in production.

  address public usdcToken;

  address public eurcToken;

  address public paxgToken;

  ICoreToken public core; // MTQ core contract (Listing 1, §2.7)

  IPriceOracle public oracle; // multi-source price consensus (Chapter
  17)

  constructor(

  address _core,

  address _oracle,

  address _usdc,

  address _eurc,

  address _paxg

  ) {

  core = ICoreToken(_core);

  oracle = IPriceOracle(_oracle);

  usdcToken = _usdc;

  eurcToken = _eurc;

  paxgToken = _paxg;

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 3. STATE UPDATE (BP §4.4.2)

  // ============================================================

  /// @dev Recomputes reserve values from balances and oracle prices.

  /// Called after every mint, redeem, rebalance or price update.

  function updateReserveState() internal {

  // Fetch asset balances and oracle consensus prices

  uint256 usdcBalance = IERC20(usdcToken).balanceOf(address(this));

  uint256 eurcBalance = IERC20(eurcToken).balanceOf(address(this));

  uint256 paxgBalance = IERC20(paxgToken).balanceOf(address(this));

  uint256 usdcPrice = oracle.getPrice(usdcToken);

  uint256 eurcPrice = oracle.getPrice(eurcToken);

  uint256 paxgPrice = getMinGoldPrice(); // min(reference, executable)

  uint256 grossFiat = usdcBalance * usdcPrice / 1e18

  + eurcBalance * eurcPrice / 1e18;

  uint256 grossGold = paxgBalance * paxgPrice / 1e18;

  // Apply haircuts per asset (invariant I4). Production iterates over

  // all registry-admitted instruments (Chapter 15); the genesis set

  // is written out explicitly here.

  uint256 netUsdc = usdcBalance * usdcPrice / 1e18 * (1e18 -
  HAIRCUT_USDC) / 1e18;

  uint256 netEurc = eurcBalance * eurcPrice / 1e18 * (1e18 -
  HAIRCUT_EURC) / 1e18;

  uint256 netGold = grossGold * (1e18 - HAIRCUT_PAXG) / 1e18;

  uint256 netFiat = netUsdc + netEurc;

  reserveState.grossFiat = grossFiat;

  reserveState.grossGold = grossGold;

  reserveState.fiatValue = netFiat;

  reserveState.goldValue = netGold;

  reserveState.liquidValue = netFiat; // stablecoins are the liquid
  layer

  _publishMetrics();

  }

  /// @dev Conservative gold price for NAV (§14.1): the lower of the

  /// reference (LBMA spot consensus) and executable (PAXG market).

  function getMinGoldPrice() internal view returns (uint256) {

  uint256 pReference = oracle.getGoldReferencePrice();

  uint256 pExecutable = oracle.getGoldExecutablePrice();

  return pReference < pExecutable ? pReference : pExecutable;

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 4. GETTER FUNCTIONS (BP §4.4.3)

  // ============================================================

  /// @dev Returns the Net Asset Value (NAV) in USD, 18 decimals —

  /// net of asset-specific haircuts (invariant I4).

  function getNAV() public view returns (uint256) {

  return reserveState.fiatValue + reserveState.goldValue;

  }

  /// @dev Returns the Reserve Ratio (RR) scaled to 1e18: V_net,t / L_t.

  function getReserveRatio() public view returns (uint256) {

  uint256 nav = getNAV();

  uint256 liability = core.getLiability(); // L_t = S_circ,t × P_MTQ,t

  return (nav * 1e18) / liability;

  }

  /// @dev Returns the Liquidity Coverage Ratio (LCR) scaled to 1e18.

  /// Implements the §13.4 definition: liquid assets are the net

  /// stablecoin layer (tokenized gold counts 0% liquid), and the

  /// stress demand assumes 25% of the USD liability redeemable

  /// within a 30-day stress period.

  function getLCR() public view returns (uint256) {

  uint256 liquidAssets = reserveState.liquidValue;

  uint256 price = core.getMTQPrice();

  uint256 circSupply = core.getCirculatingSupply();

  uint256 stressDemand = (circSupply * price / 1e18) *
  STRESS_REDEMPTION_RATE / 1e18;

  return (liquidAssets * 1e18) / stressDemand;

  }

  /// @dev Returns the current protocol status (§14.2.2). The full state

  /// machine, including hysteresis and recovery exits, is Chapter 18.

  function getProtocolStatus() public view returns (string memory) {

  uint256 rr = getReserveRatio();

  uint256 lcr = getLCR();

  if (rr < RR_HARD) return "EMERGENCY";

  if (recoveryMode) {

  // RECOVERY: RR >= 1.05 (rising), LCR >= 0.95, sustained for the

  // confirmation period; governance then clears the flag.

  if (rr >= RR_STRESS && lcr >= 0.95e18

  && block.timestamp >= recoveryStartedAt +
  RECOVERY_CONFIRMATION_PERIOD) {

  return "RECOVERY";

  }

  return "DEFENSIVE";

  }

  if (rr >= RR_TARGET && lcr >= LCR_TARGET) return "NORMAL";

  if (rr >= RR_STRESS && lcr >= 0.90e18) return "CAUTION";

  return "DEFENSIVE";

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 5. SAFETY MODIFIERS (BP §4.4.4)

  // ============================================================

  modifier onlyIfSolvent() {

  require(getReserveRatio() >= RR_HARD, "MTQ: Insolvent - operations
  paused");

  _;

  }

  modifier onlyIfLiquid() {

  require(getLCR() >= LCR_TARGET, "MTQ: Illiquid - minting paused");

  _;

  }

  // Used for minting (mint/redeem economics are specified in Chapter
  19)

  function mint(uint256 amount) external onlyIfSolvent onlyIfLiquid {

  // Minting logic

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 6. EVENT EMISSIONS (BP §4.5)

  // ============================================================

  event ReserveMetricsUpdated(

  uint256 indexed nav,

  uint256 indexed reserveRatio,

  uint256 indexed liquidityRatio,

  string status

  );

  function _publishMetrics() internal {

  uint256 nav = getNAV();

  uint256 rr = getReserveRatio();

  uint256 lcr = getLCR();

  emit ReserveMetricsUpdated(nav, rr, lcr, getProtocolStatus());

  }

  }

  interface ICoreToken {

  function getLiability() external view returns (uint256);

  function getMTQPrice() external view returns (uint256);

  function getCirculatingSupply() external view returns (uint256);

  }

  interface IPriceOracle {

  function getPrice(address token) external view returns (uint256);

  function getGoldReferencePrice() external view returns (uint256);

  function getGoldExecutablePrice() external view returns (uint256);

  }

  interface IERC20 {

  function balanceOf(address account) external view returns (uint256);

  }

  Fidelity note (BP §4.4 → Listing 6): (1) BP applied a blended
  stablecoin haircut 'for simplicity' and stored gross values, which its
  own illustrative getNAV then returned; this listing applies per-asset
  haircuts and stores net values so invariant I4 (Section 2.6) holds by
  construction — gross values are retained only as monitoring fields.
  (2) getLCR() is reconciled with the Section 13.4 LiquidityCoverage
  definition: the numerator is the net stablecoin layer and the
  denominator embeds the 25%-of-liability, 30-day stress-demand
  assumption via STRESS_REDEMPTION_RATE. (3) BP's illustrative
  getProtocolStatus could never return RECOVERY (the branch was
  unreachable); the recoveryMode flag plus RECOVERY_CONFIRMATION_PERIOD
  (48 hours, Listing 1) restore the Section 14.2.2 row, with the full
  machine in Chapter 18. (4) The genesis instruments are written
  explicitly; production iterates over registry-admitted instruments,
  including XAUT-class instruments, which carry the tokenized-gold
  haircut of 1.0%.

14.6.2 Event Emissions

The reserve manager emits a single consolidated health event,
ReserveMetricsUpdated(nav, reserveRatio, liquidityRatio, status),
published from _publishMetrics() at the end of every reserve-state
recomputation [BP §4.5]. Because updateReserveState() runs after every
mint, redeem, rebalance and price update, the event fires exactly when
any input to RR or LCR could have changed — there is no path by which
the metrics move silently. The three numeric fields are indexed so that
off-chain indexers and the transparency layer (Chapter 24) can filter
the full history of reserve health cheaply, and the status string makes
each emission self-describing for the risk-state model of Chapter 21 and
the monitoring dashboards of Chapter 25. A missing or stale event
sequence is itself an alarm condition: metric liveness is checked by the
validation gates (Section 23.11) and the failure-mode analysis of
Chapter 21.

14.6.3 Developer-Facing Constants

The developer-facing constant set of the reserve manager is carried from
BP §4.6. Values are 1e18-scaled; governance scopes follow the layered
hierarchy of Chapter 22 (constitutional changes require the 7/7
Multi-Sig with the 90-day timelock; Risk-Council changes require 4/7
with the 24-hour timelock).

  ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Constant          Symbol                   Value             Scope
  ----------------- ------------------------ ----------------- -----------------
  USDC Haircut      HAIRCUT_USDC             0.005e18 (0.5%)   Immutable (Risk
                                                               Council)

  EURC Haircut      HAIRCUT_EURC             0.007e18 (0.7%)   Immutable (Risk
                                                               Council)

  GBP/JPY/CNY       HAIRCUT_OTHER            0.010e18 /        Immutable (Risk
  Haircut                                    0.015e18          Council)

  Gold Haircut      HAIRCUT_PAXG             0.010e18 (1.0%)   Immutable (Risk
                                                               Council)

  RR Target         RR_TARGET                1.10e18 (110%)    Constitutional
                                                               (7/7 Multi-Sig)

  RR Stress Floor   RR_STRESS                1.05e18 (105%)    Risk Council
                                                               (4/7)

  RR Hard Floor     RR_HARD                  1.00e18 (100%)    Constitutional
                                                               (immutable)

  Stress Redemption STRESS_REDEMPTION_RATE   0.25e18 (25%)     Risk Council
  Rate                                                         (4/7)
  ------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Two constants complete the set from the core registry of Section 2.9:
LCR_TARGET = 1.00 (Risk Council, 4/7) and RECOVERY_CONFIRMATION_PERIOD =
48 hours (DAO vote). The haircut constants are immutable in the contract
but remain Risk-Council-adjustable at the parameter-registry level
(Section 22.3) under the material-change standard of Section 14.1.1 —
meaning an adjustment is a governed redeployment or parameter
transaction with a 24-hour timelock and public disclosure, never a
silent edit. This closing loop matters: Sections 14.1 and 14.2 are
referenced by invariants I4 and I2 respectively, so every constant that
participates in those computations must itself be under
constitutional-grade change control.

15. The Asset Admission Registry

15.1 Purpose and Scope

  The Asset Admission Registry is the canonical source of truth for
  which on-chain instruments are eligible to be held in the Reserve
  Vault [BP §5.1]. It decouples the reference index — which is defined
  in currencies — from the implementation assets (stablecoins, gold
  tokens, tokenized T-bills) that actually back it, allowing the
  protocol to adapt to changes in the stablecoin and gold-token
  ecosystem without rebuilding the core monetary logic.

The scope boundary must be stated precisely, because it is the most
easily confused distinction in the reserve architecture. The registry
governs instrument eligibility; the Constituency Engine (Chapter 4)
governs currency eligibility. These are different questions answered by
different mechanisms:

Constituency Engine (Chapter 4): which currencies are in the index ≠
Asset Admission Registry (Chapter 15): which instruments may back the
reserve

A currency can be a reference-basket member with no admitted instrument
yet (GBP and JPY at genesis, until a suitable regulated instrument
emerges), and an instrument can be admitted for a currency that later
exits the basket — in which case the reserve runs the instrument down
through rebalancing rather than by registry decree. The index references
currency exposure and resolves the implementing token through the
registry; the reserve holds only registry-admitted instruments; neither
layer can silently redefine the other. This is the reserve-side
complement of the two-system separation of Section 2.4 (W^{Index} ≠
W^{Reserve}).

The registry is consumed by three systems [BP §5.1]: the Minting Engine,
to know which assets to buy when a user deposits (Chapter 19); the
Rebalancing Engine, to know which assets are eligible for trading
(Chapters 10–11); and the Risk State Machine, to determine if an asset
should be suspended or ejected (Chapters 18 and 21). Each consumer reads
the registry at decision time rather than caching instrument lists, so a
state change (Section 15.3) takes effect at the next decision, not at
the next redeployment.

15.2 The Eligibility Standard

An asset must satisfy all eight criteria to be admitted as an eligible
reserve asset; there is no scoring trade-off at admission, only
pass/fail on each criterion [BP §5.2]. The criteria are enforced by the
Risk Council (4-of-7 Multi-Sig) before an asset is added, and each has a
named verification method so that admission is an evidence process, not
an opinion:

  ------------------------------------------------------------------------
  #                 Criterion         Description       Verification
                                                        Method
  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------
  1                 Issuer            The issuer is a   Regulatory
                    Authorization     regulated entity  registration
                                      or subject to an  number; legal
                                      enforceable legal opinion on file.
                                      framework.        

  2                 Redemption Right  The holder has a  Public
                                      verifiable right  attestations;

                                      stablecoins) or   redeem function.
                                      for physical      
                                      gold.

  5                 Smart Contract    The token         Audit report; no
