  ------------------------------------------------------------------------

The registry does not automatically admit assets [BP §5.2]. Each
addition requires a governance proposal and a 48-hour timelock (4-of-7
Risk Council approval) to ensure thorough review, so an instrument that
passes all eight checks on paper still waits two days of public scrutiny
before it can touch the vault. For gold instruments, the eligibility
score E_j of Section 14.3 is the quantitative expression of criteria 2,
3, 5 and 7 — a tokenized-gold instrument that fails the redemption-right
or custody criteria fails the score, and an admitted instrument's
residual risks are priced through its haircut (Section 14.1.1) rather
than ignored.

The eight criteria are deliberately instrument-level, not issuer-level
compliments. An issuer may operate several tokens of differing quality
(for example, a well-audited USD stablecoin and a thinner-liquidity EUR
instrument), and each token is judged on its own evidence. Conversely,
criterion 8 ensures that even individually excellent instruments cannot
collectively create dependence on one issuer — the concentration
analysis of Section 15.6 aggregates across instruments by issuerId after
admission.

15.3 Asset States

Each registered asset exists in one of four states, forming an
escalation machine from fully eligible to fully exited [BP §5.3]. The
states are ordered: an asset moves down the ladder as evidence
deteriorates and up only through governance review, and every transition
is an emitted event (Section 15.7) so the reserve's instrument history
is fully reconstructible.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  State                   Action                  Trigger
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  ACTIVE                  The asset is eligible   Default state after
                          for purchase,           admission.
                          rebalancing, and        
                          collateral.             

  WATCH                   No new purchases;       Triggered when a
                          existing holdings are   criterion is about to
                          maintained.             fail (e.g., liquidity
                                                  drops below $1M for 3
                                                  days).

  RESTRICTED              The asset cannot be     Triggered when a
                          used for new            criterion is violated
                          collateral; the         (e.g., issuer is
                          protocol will sell it   sanctioned).
                          during the next         
                          rebalancing cycle.      

  EJECTED                 The protocol sells 100% Triggered when the
                          of the asset via the    asset is confirmed
                          staged liquidation      de-pegged or issuer
                          ladder (Chapter 11).    redemption is frozen.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The WATCH state is the machine's early-warning layer: it freezes new
accumulation without forcing disposal, which prevents panic-selling an
instrument whose criterion dip may be transient (a three-day liquidity
slump is not a depeg). RESTRICTED converts concern into scheduled exit —
the instrument stops being collateral immediately but is sold through
the ordinary rebalancing cycle rather than in a fire sale, and MARP's
execution gates still apply to the disposal itself. EJECTED is the
terminal state: confirmed failure (depeg or frozen redemption) removes
the instrument from all uses, and disposal proceeds through the staged
liquidation ladder of Chapter 11, which sizes and paces the exit to
market depth.

  Fidelity note: BP §5.3's four-state machine (ACTIVE / WATCH /
  RESTRICTED / EJECTED) is retained verbatim. The intermediate WATCH and
  RESTRICTED states perform the suspend function and EJECTED the delist
  function, with an explicit quarantine step that a coarser
  ACTIVE/SUSPENDED/DELISTED machine would lack — the quarantine is what
  stops a three-day liquidity dip from becoming either an ignored
  warning or a forced liquidation.

15.4 Registry Data Structures

The registry stores one record per admitted instrument and one mapping
per currency code. The record is the unit of governance (admission,
state changes, parameter updates); the mapping is the unit of resolution
(which token currently implements a currency exposure) [BP §5.4].

15.4.1 Asset Record

Each instrument is described by a fixed field set [BP §5.4.1]:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Field                   Type                    Meaning
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  currencyCode            bytes3                  The currency the
                                                  instrument implements,
                                                  e.g., USD, EUR, GBP,
                                                  JPY, CNY, XAU (for
                                                  gold).

  tokenAddress            address                 The on-chain token
                                                  address.

  name                    string                  Human-readable name
                                                  (e.g., USDC, PAXG).

  haircut                 uint256                 Asset-specific haircut
                                                  (1e18 scale), approved
                                                  at admission (§14.1.1).

  liquidityThreshold      uint256                 Minimum 24h volume in
                                                  USD (1e18 scale) — the
                                                  WATCH trigger level.

  admissionDate           uint256                 Timestamp of when the
                                                  asset was added.

  state                   AssetState              Current state (ACTIVE /
                                                  WATCH / RESTRICTED /
                                                  EJECTED).

  isStablecoin            bool                    True if fiat-pegged;
                                                  false for gold and
                                                  commodities — drives
                                                  the LCR liquid
                                                  classification (§13.4).

  issuerId                bytes32                 Hash of the issuer
                                                  identifier, for
                                                  concentration tracking
                                                  (§15.6).
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Two fields deserve comment. The haircut lives in the record, not only in
the reserve manager's constants, because the registry is the point of
admission where the Risk Council approves instrument and price together
(Section 14.1.1); the reserve manager consumes the admitted value. The
issuerId is deliberately a hash rather than a string so that
concentration accounting can aggregate by issuer on-chain without
maintaining a parallel issuer directory, and so that a renamed or
rebranded issuer cannot shed its concentration history.

15.4.2 Currency-to-Asset Mapping

To support the index, the registry maintains a mapping from currency
code to the active token address. This allows the index and the engines
to call getAssetForCurrency("EUR") and always obtain the current
canonical asset [BP §5.4.2]:

currencyToAsset: bytes3 ↦ address

The genesis mapping is set at deployment [BP §5.4.2], extended to the
seven-component basket of Section 3.4:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Currency Code           Initial Asset           Why
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  USD                     USDC (Circle)           Largest regulated USD
                                                  stablecoin.

  EUR                     EURC (Circle)           Largest regulated EUR
                                                  stablecoin.

  GBP                     TBD                     Will be set once a
                          (governance-approved)   suitable regulated GBP
                                                  stablecoin emerges.

  JPY                     TBD                     Will be set once a
                          (governance-approved)   suitable regulated JPY
                                                  stablecoin emerges.

  CNY                     TBD                     Will be set once a
                          (governance-approved)   suitable regulated CNY
                                                  stablecoin emerges
                                                  (offshore CNH).

  CHF                     TBD                     Registry-resolved at
                          (governance-approved)   genesis per Section
                                                  3.4; set upon admission
                                                  of a qualified CHF
                                                  instrument.

  XAU                     PAXG / XAUT             Tokenized gold
                          (registry-resolved)     implementation,
                                                  admitted under the
                                                  Section 14.3 score;
                                                  physical bullion
                                                  compared under the same
                                                  framework.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

  Fidelity note: BP's genesis mapping table predates the seven-component
  v2.0 basket and listed only USD, EUR, GBP, JPY and CNY; the CHF and
  XAU rows are added to match the genesis snapshot of Section 3.4. The
  mapping stores the canonical instrument per currency; a successor
  instrument replaces the mapping through governance (delist and
  re-add), and issuer-level diversification across multiple gold
  instruments is governed by the concentration limits of Section 15.6
  rather than by parallel mappings.

15.5 Core Registry Functions

The registry exposes a small, deliberately boring function set: two
governance-gated mutators and three public queries [BP §5.5]. All
mutation flows through the Risk Council modifier; the 48-hour timelock
of Section 15.2 is enforced by the timelock controller (Chapter 22)
around these entry points, not inside them.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Function                      Access                  Purpose
  ----------------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  addAsset(...)                 Risk Council (4/7 + 48h Admits a new
                                timelock)               instrument: sets code,
                                                        address, name, haircut,
                                                        liquidity threshold,
                                                        stablecoin flag and
                                                        issuer hash.

  updateAssetState(...)         Risk Council (4/7)      Transitions the
                                                        instrument's state
                                                        (ACTIVE → WATCH →
                                                        RESTRICTED → EJECTED).

  getAssetForCurrency(bytes3)   public view             Resolves the canonical
                                                        token address
                                                        implementing a currency
                                                        exposure.

  getAssetRecord(bytes3)        public view             Returns the full asset
                                                        record for audit and
                                                        risk engines.

  getActiveAssets()             public view             Lists all ACTIVE
                                                        instruments — the
                                                        eligible set for
                                                        minting and
                                                        rebalancing.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

addAsset validates before it writes: the currency slot must be free (no
instrument already mapped), the token address must be non-zero, the
haircut must lie within the admissible range of 0.5%–10%, and the
liquidity threshold must meet the $1,000,000 minimum of criterion 6.
These bounds make the registry self-defending against governance error —
a proposal that attempts to admit an instrument with a 0.2% haircut or a
token at the zero address fails at the contract, not merely at review.
The full Solidity implementation of all five functions, with the
modifiers and events, is Listing 7 in Section 15.8.

updateAssetState is intentionally narrow: it moves an instrument along
the state machine of Section 15.3 and does nothing else. Haircut and
liquidity-threshold revisions are separate parameter transactions
(carried in Listing 7 as updateAssetParameters) so that a state demotion
can never smuggle in a valuation change, and a valuation change can
never silently rescue an instrument from RESTRICTED. Every mutator emits
the corresponding event of Section 15.7, and the record's admissionDate
is immutable after admission — the audit trail of who was admitted when
is never editable.

15.6 Concentration Limits and Chain Diversification

Concentration is the failure mode the registry exists to prevent: a
reserve of individually excellent instruments can still be fragile if
they share an issuer, a custodian, a chain or a venue. This section
carries the issuer and asset caps of the blueprint, merged with the
reserve- and currency-concentration rules of the modification
specification [MS §62–63].

15.6.1 Issuer Concentration

To prevent over-reliance on a single issuer (for example, Circle), the
protocol enforces [BP §5.6]:

  (Value of assets issued by issuer_(i))/(Total Reserve Value) ≤ 30%

Enforcement happens before execution, not after: the Rebalancing Engine
checks this constraint before executing any trade that would increase
exposure to an issuer that already exceeds 25% of the reserve, and any
trade that would breach 30% is blocked outright [BP §5.6]. The 25% soft
checkpoint and 30% hard block create a warning band in which
accumulation is possible only in the direction of reduction. A 4-of-7
Risk Council vote can temporarily raise the limit to 35% during a
liquidity crisis, but the override requires a 24-hour timelock and
public disclosure — a crisis exception that is visible, bounded and
reversible, not a discretion.

15.6.2 Reserve Concentration by Asset

For any reserve asset j, its share of the total reserve value is [MS
§62]:

C_(j) = (ReserveValue_(j))/(TotalReserveValue)

C_(j) ≤ C_(max)

The same principle is applied beyond instruments, to every dependency of
the reserve architecture [MS §62]:

-   stablecoin issuers — capped by the 30% issuer limit of Section
    15.6.1;

-   custodians — no single custodian may hold a dominant share of gold
    or fiat holdings;

-   gold issuers — tokenized-gold exposure is capped per issuer, with
    rotation to physical bullion as the release valve (Section 14.3);

-   liquidity venues — execution is diversified across venues so no
    single DEX or CEX failure can trap the exit path (Chapter 11);

-   oracle providers — price feeds are diversified so that no single
    provider failure can corrupt valuation (Chapter 17, invariant I9).

The concentration stack is thus uniform in principle but layered in
enforcement: issuer caps are checked at trade time by the rebalancing
engine, custodian and venue caps are governance-monitored risk limits,
and oracle diversity is a structural property of the multi-source
architecture rather than a tunable number. What is common is the
accounting: every layer computes a share of a total and compares it to a
published maximum, so concentration risk is always a measured quantity,
never a narrative one.

15.6.3 Currency Concentration

On the currency axis, the modification specification requires [MS §63]:

w_(i) ≤ w_(i,max)

but the maximum should be a constitutional envelope rather than a fixed
live allocation [MS §63]. This is the same principle as the reference
basket's admissibility bounds (Section 8.1): the envelope caps what any
single currency's weight may reach (for example, the USD upper bound of
32%), while the live weight within the envelope is an adaptive output of
the MASE pipeline. The distinction matters in both directions — the
envelope prevents the optimizer from concentrating the basket or the
reserve in one currency no matter how good its current score, and it
equally prevents anyone from quoting the current weight as a structural
entitlement. On the index side the envelopes are enforced by the weight
verification contract (Listing 2, Section 7.7); on the reserve side,
currency exposure is bounded by the same envelopes through the
rebalancing engine's constraint set (Section 14.4).

15.6.4 Chain Diversification Policy

To minimize blockchain-specific risk (L2 downtime, bridge exploits), the
protocol follows four rules [BP §5.7]:

-   Primary chain: the Reserve Vault holds assets only on the mainnet of
    the deployed chain (e.g., Arbitrum, Ethereum).

-   No wrapped assets: the protocol does not hold wrapped versions of
    assets that are native to another chain (e.g., wormhole USDC); only
    the native token is allowed.

-   Canonical token preference: for each asset, the registry must
    specify the canonical token address for the deployed chain;
    alternative bridges are not accepted.

-   Chain-specific risk: if the Risk Council determines that the
    deployed chain poses elevated risk (e.g., repeated sequencer
    failures), it may pause minting and rebalancing until the issue is
    resolved.

The registry enforces canonicality structurally: new assets must be the
canonical version for the chain (the IS_CANONICAL flag in Listing 7),
and the admission review confirms the token's provenance. This policy
accepts a real cost — reserve mobility is confined to one chain's
liquidity — in exchange for eliminating an entire class of bridge risk
from the backing of the unit, which is the correct trade for a reserve
whose purpose is obligation coverage rather than capital efficiency.

15.7 Integration and Events

The index and the engines use currency exposure, not token addresses [BP
§5.8]. The registry provides the mapping so that every consumer resolves
instruments at decision time. Two flows illustrate the pattern:

Minting flow (simplified) [BP §5.8]:

1.  User deposits USDC.

2.  The contract calls getAssetForCurrency("EUR") to get the current
    EURC address.

3.  The atomic swap uses that address to buy EURC.

Rebalancing flow [BP §5.8]:

1.  The engine determines the target weights (e.g., 27.8% EUR exposure).

2.  It calls getAssetForCurrency("EUR") to know which token to buy or
    sell.

In both flows the monetary decision is made in currency space (weights,
exposures) and only the execution resolves into instrument space. If the
registry replaces the EUR instrument — a governance event with a 48-hour
timelock — every subsequent decision automatically uses the successor
token, and the retirement of the old instrument proceeds through the
RESTRICTED state's scheduled disposal rather than through any
special-case code path. The minting economics themselves are specified
in Chapter 19; the execution mechanics in Chapters 10–11.

The registry emits four events [BP §5.9]: AssetAdded(currencyCode,
tokenAddress, name) on admission; AssetStateChanged(currencyCode,
newState) on every state transition; AssetRemoved(currencyCode) on
delisting; and AssetUpdated(currencyCode, field, newValue) on parameter
revisions. Together these events form the complete instrument history of
the reserve — the transparency layer (Chapter 24) reconstructs the
eligible set at any past block from them, and the risk-state model
(Chapter 21) consumes state transitions as inputs, since a WATCH or
RESTRICTED event is itself risk evidence about the reserve's
composition.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Parameter               Value                   Governance
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Max Issuer              30%                     Constitutional (Risk
  Concentration                                   Council)

  Min 24h Volume          $1,000,000              Risk Council (4/7)

  Haircut Range           0.5% – 10%              Risk Council (4/7)

  Timelock for Asset      48 hours                Constitutional (7/7)
  Changes                                         
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

15.8 Smart Contract Implementation — Asset Admission Registry

The listing below assembles the registry contract from BP §5.5: the
state machine, the asset record, the currency-to-asset mapping, the two
governance-gated mutators, the three public queries and the
parameter-revision function, with the events of BP §5.9 and the
canonical-token flag of BP §5.7. Access control is the onlyRiskCouncil
modifier, which in production must be backed by the 4/7 Multi-Sig (for
example, a Gnosis Safe) with the 48-hour timelock enforced by the
governance layer of Chapter 22.

Listing 7 — Asset admission registry (adapts BP §5.5)

  // SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT

  pragma solidity ^0.8.19;

  /// @title AssetAdmissionRegistry — canonical source of truth for
  which

  /// on-chain instruments may back the reserve (§15.1).

  contract AssetAdmissionRegistry {

  // ---- Governance ----

  address public riskCouncil; // 4/7 Multi-Sig (e.g., Gnosis Safe)

  // ---- Developer-facing bounds (§15.7) ----

  uint256 public constant MAX_ISSUER_CONCENTRATION = 0.30e18; // 30%

  uint256 public constant MIN_LIQUIDITY_THRESHOLD = 1000000e18; // $1M,
  1e18 USD scale

  uint256 public constant HAIRCUT_FLOOR = 0.005e18; // 0.5%

  uint256 public constant HAIRCUT_CEILING = 0.10e18; // 10%

  // A flag to indicate if the asset is the canonical version for this
  chain

  bool public constant IS_CANONICAL = true; // Hardcoded; new assets
  must be canonical

  // ---- Asset states (§15.3) ----

  enum AssetState { ACTIVE, WATCH, RESTRICTED, EJECTED }

  // ---- Asset record (§15.4.1) ----

  struct AssetRecord {

  bytes3 currencyCode; // e.g., "USD", "EUR", "GBP", "JPY", "CNY", "XAU"

  address tokenAddress; // the on-chain token address

  string name; // human-readable name (e.g., "USDC", "PAXG")

  uint256 haircut; // asset-specific haircut (1e18 scale)

  uint256 liquidityThreshold; // minimum 24h volume in USD (1e18 scale)

  uint256 admissionDate; // timestamp of when the asset was added

  AssetState state; // current state (enum)

  bool isStablecoin; // true if fiat-pegged, false for gold/commodities

  bytes32 issuerId; // hash of issuer identifier (concentration
  tracking)

  }

  AssetRecord[] public assets;

  mapping(bytes3 => address) public currencyToAsset; // §15.4.2

  modifier onlyRiskCouncil() {

  require(msg.sender == riskCouncil, "Only Risk Council");

  _;

  }

  // ---- Events (§15.7, BP §5.9) ----

  event AssetAdded(bytes3 indexed currencyCode, address indexed
  tokenAddress, string name);

  event AssetStateChanged(bytes3 indexed currencyCode, AssetState
  newState);

  event AssetRemoved(bytes3 indexed currencyCode);

  event AssetUpdated(bytes3 indexed currencyCode, string field, uint256
  newValue);

  constructor(address _riskCouncil) {

  riskCouncil = _riskCouncil;

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 1. ADD ASSET — governance only (BP §5.5.1)

  // ============================================================

  function addAsset(

  bytes3 currencyCode,

  address tokenAddress,

  string memory name,

  uint256 haircut,

  uint256 liquidityThreshold,

  bool isStablecoin,

  bytes32 issuerId

  ) external onlyRiskCouncil {

  require(currencyToAsset[currencyCode] == address(0), "Asset already
  exists");

  require(tokenAddress != address(0), "Invalid address");

  require(haircut >= HAIRCUT_FLOOR && haircut <= HAIRCUT_CEILING,
  "Haircut out of bounds");

  require(liquidityThreshold >= MIN_LIQUIDITY_THRESHOLD, "Liquidity
  threshold too low");

  require(IS_CANONICAL, "Only canonical tokens admitted");

  AssetRecord memory newAsset = AssetRecord({

  currencyCode: currencyCode,

  tokenAddress: tokenAddress,

  name: name,

  haircut: haircut,

  liquidityThreshold: liquidityThreshold,

  admissionDate: block.timestamp,

  state: AssetState.ACTIVE,

  isStablecoin: isStablecoin,

  issuerId: issuerId

  });

  assets.push(newAsset);

  currencyToAsset[currencyCode] = tokenAddress;

  emit AssetAdded(currencyCode, tokenAddress, name);

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 2. UPDATE ASSET STATE — Risk Council (BP §5.5.2)

  // ============================================================

  function updateAssetState(bytes3 currencyCode, AssetState newState)

  external onlyRiskCouncil

  {

  uint256 index = getAssetIndex(currencyCode);

  assets[index].state = newState;

  emit AssetStateChanged(currencyCode, newState);

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 3. QUERIES (BP §5.5.3 – §5.5.5)

  // ============================================================

  function getAssetForCurrency(bytes3 currencyCode) public view returns
  (address) {

  address token = currencyToAsset[currencyCode];

  require(token != address(0), "Currency not supported");

  return token;

  }

  function getAssetRecord(bytes3 currencyCode) public view returns
  (AssetRecord memory) {

  uint256 index = getAssetIndex(currencyCode);

  return assets[index];

  }

  function getActiveAssets() public view returns (AssetRecord[] memory)
  {

  uint256 count = 0;

  for (uint256 i = 0; i < assets.length; i++) {

  if (assets[i].state == AssetState.ACTIVE) count++;

  }

  AssetRecord[] memory active = new AssetRecord[](count);

  uint256 idx = 0;

  for (uint256 i = 0; i < assets.length; i++) {

  if (assets[i].state == AssetState.ACTIVE) {

  active[idx] = assets[i];

  idx++;

  }

  }

  return active;

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 4. REGISTRY MAINTENANCE (supports §14.1.1 haircut rule)

  // ============================================================

  /// @notice Revise an admitted instrument's haircut and liquidity

  /// threshold. Haircut changes are parameter transactions,

  /// strictly separated from state transitions.

  function updateAssetParameters(

  bytes3 currencyCode,

  uint256 haircut,

  uint256 liquidityThreshold

  ) external onlyRiskCouncil {

  uint256 index = getAssetIndex(currencyCode);

  require(haircut >= HAIRCUT_FLOOR && haircut <= HAIRCUT_CEILING,
  "Haircut out of bounds");

  assets[index].haircut = haircut;

  assets[index].liquidityThreshold = liquidityThreshold;

  emit AssetUpdated(currencyCode, "haircut", haircut);

  }

  /// @notice Delist an instrument: set EJECTED and free the currency
  slot

  /// so a successor can be mapped. The record is retained for audit.

  function removeAsset(bytes3 currencyCode) external onlyRiskCouncil {

  uint256 index = getAssetIndex(currencyCode);

  assets[index].state = AssetState.EJECTED;

  currencyToAsset[currencyCode] = address(0);

  emit AssetRemoved(currencyCode);

  }

  // ---- Internal index lookup (linear scan; registry is small) ----

  function getAssetIndex(bytes3 currencyCode) internal view returns
  (uint256) {

  address token = currencyToAsset[currencyCode];

  require(token != address(0), "Currency not supported");

  for (uint256 i = 0; i < assets.length; i++) {

  if (assets[i].tokenAddress == token) return i;

  }

  revert("Asset record not found");

  }

  }

  Fidelity note (BP §5.5 → Listing 7): (1) BP carried the registry
  functions as separate fragments; they are assembled here into one
  contract with the BP §5.9 events and the BP §5.7 canonical-token flag.
  (2) The liquidity-threshold floor is expressed as
  MIN_LIQUIDITY_THRESHOLD = 1000000e18, the $1M minimum of eligibility
  criterion 6; BP's illustrative literal (0.5e18) was inconsistent with
  the 1e18 USD scale. (3) AssetRemoved, declared but never implemented
  in BP, is realized as delisting — the record is marked EJECTED and the
  currency slot is freed for a successor, while the record itself is
  retained for audit. (4) The one-instrument-per-currency mapping is the
  BP design; multiple implementations of one exposure (e.g., PAXG and
  XAUT) are handled by governance succession plus issuer-level
  concentration limits (§15.6), not by parallel mappings. (5) The
  48-hour timelock for asset changes is enforced by the governance layer
  (Chapter 22) around these entry points.

16. The Dynamic Buffer

16.1 Purpose

  The Dynamic Buffer is the multi-layer safety cushion that sits on top
  of the 100% core reserve. It absorbs market volatility, provides a
  first line of defence against devaluation, and adjusts its composition
  based on the current health of the protocol [BP §8.1].

The buffer is not a separate vault — it is an accounting construct that
distinguishes two layers of the same reserve [BP §8.1]:

-   Core Reserve: the 100% liability coverage — sacred, never touched
    for profit.

-   Buffer Reserve: the additional 10% excess collateral that is
    actively managed to protect solvency.

The purpose of the separation is to make the target tier of Section
14.2.1 operational rather than decorative. A reserve ratio of 1.10 means
the vault holds 10% more than its liabilities; the buffer is that 10%,
identified and managed as a distinct defensive layer. When losses arrive
— a stablecoin depeg, a gold correction, an oracle-true repricing — the
buffer absorbs them before the core is impaired, which is precisely what
keeps invariant I2 (RR_t ≥ 1.00) from being tested by ordinary
volatility. The consumption mechanics are the first-loss waterfall of
Section 16.4; the state-dependent composition is Sections 16.2 and 16.3.

Because the buffer is an accounting construct, every buffer figure in
this chapter is computed from the same measured quantities as the
reserve manager's state (Listing 6): haircut-adjusted instrument values,
oracle-consensus prices, and the liability L_t. Nothing about the buffer
changes how assets are custodyed or where they live; it changes how
losses are attributed, and therefore which actions the architecture
takes as health deteriorates.

16.2 The Three Buffer States

The buffer has three distinct layers, each with a different gold/fiat
split, triggered by the Reserve Ratio [BP §8.2]:

  --------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  State          RR Range       Buffer Gold    Buffer Fiat    Description
                                Ratio (B_gold) Ratio          
  -------------- -------------- -------------- -------------- --------------------
  BASE (Normal)  RR ≥ 1.10      62.5%          37.5%          Normal operations.
                                                              Buffer is 62.5%
                                                              gold, 37.5% fiat.
                                                              This is the optimal
                                                              split derived from
                                                              simulations
                                                              (Pareto-frontier).

  STRESS         1.05 ≤ RR <    85%            15%            Defensive posture.
                 1.10                                         Buffer shifts
                                                              heavily to gold to
                                                              protect against
                                                              further devaluation.
                                                              Minting is throttled
                                                              (50% capacity).

  EMERGENCY      RR < 1.05      100%           0%             Maximum defence.
                                                              Buffer is 100% gold.
                                                              Minting is paused.
                                                              Emergency
                                                              rebalancing is
                                                              triggered.
                                                              Redemption is still
                                                              allowed (with
                                                              increased fee).
  --------------------------------------------------------------------------------

A key clarification avoids the most common misreading of this table [BP
§8.2]: "100% gold" means the entire 10% buffer is held in gold — never
that the whole reserve becomes gold. The total reserve gold weight is:

  W_(gold,total) = W_(gold,core) + (0.10 × B_(gold))

With the v1.2 baseline core gold weight of 20%, the three states
produce:

-   in BASE (62.5% buffer gold): W_{gold,total} = 0.20 + (0.10 × 0.625)
    = 26.25%;

-   in STRESS (85% buffer gold): W_{gold,total} = 0.20 + (0.10 × 0.85) =
    28.5%;

-   in EMERGENCY (100% buffer gold): W_{gold,total} = 0.20 + (0.10 ×
    1.00) = 30.0%.

  This is the maximum gold exposure (30%). The buffer never makes the
  reserve 100% gold [BP §8.2].

  Fidelity note (v1.2 → v2.0): the fixed core gold weight of 20% (and
  hence the 26.25%/28.5%/30.0% totals) is a v1.2 constant tied to the
  fixed gold allocation; under v2.0 the core reserve's gold share is an
  output of the reserve optimization (Section 14.4) bounded by the
  liquidity classification of Section 13.4, and the gold reference
  allocation is separate (invariant I12). The buffer-layer mechanics —
  the three states, the defensive tilts toward gold as RR deteriorates,
  the ramps and the waterfall — are retained unchanged, with the
  v1.2-simulated 62.5% BASE split retained as a validation-stage
  initialization value to be re-derived under the stress program of
  Section 23.11. The general form W_{gold,total} = W_{gold,core,t} +
  (0.10 × B_{gold}) holds with the adaptive core share.

16.3 Buffer Allocation and State Transitions

The total reserve is decomposed as [BP §8.3]:

V_(net,t) = V_(core,t) + V_(buffer,t)

where V_{core,t} = S_{circ,t} × P_{MTQ,t} is the 100% liability coverage
— always exactly equal to liabilities — and V_{buffer,t} = 0.10 ×
S_{circ,t} × P_{MTQ,t} is the 10% excess collateral. The buffer's
internal split follows the state table of Section 16.2:

V_(buffer,gold,t) = V_(buffer,t) × B_(gold)(RR_(t))

V_(buffer,fiat,t) = V_(buffer,t) × (1 - B_(gold)(RR_(t)))

and the total gold holding is the sum of the core and buffer gold
layers:

V_(gold,total,t) = V_(core,gold,t) + V_(buffer,gold,t)

The buffer state is re-evaluated every time the Reserve Ratio is updated
— after every mint, redeem, rebalance or price update [BP §8.4] — so the
buffer is always consistent with the freshest solvency measurement
rather than with a stale snapshot. State transitions follow fixed rules
with hysteresis on the way back up:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  From → To               Trigger                 Action
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  BASE → STRESS           RR < 1.10               Buffer gold ratio
                                                  increases from 62.5% to
                                                  85% over 24 hours
                                                  (linear ramp to avoid
                                                  sudden market impact).

  STRESS → BASE           RR ≥ 1.10 for 24        Buffer gold ratio
                          consecutive hours       decreases from 85% to
                                                  62.5% over 24 hours.

  STRESS → EMERGENCY      RR < 1.05               Buffer gold ratio jumps
                                                  to 100% immediately.
                                                  Minting paused.
                                                  Emergency rebalance
                                                  triggered.

  EMERGENCY → STRESS      RR ≥ 1.05 for 48        Buffer gold ratio
                          consecutive hours       decreases from 100% to
                                                  85%. Minting resumes at
                                                  50% capacity.

  EMERGENCY → BASE        RR ≥ 1.10 for 48        Buffer gold ratio
                          consecutive hours       decreases from 100% to
                                                  62.5%. Minting resumes
                                                  at 100% capacity.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The asymmetry is deliberate: deterioration acts immediately or on short
ramps, while recovery requires sustained evidence (24 or 48 consecutive
hours above the threshold) before the buffer relaxes [BP §8.4]. This is
the same anti-oscillation philosophy as the constituency hysteresis of
Section 4.7 and the recovery directionality of Section 14.2.2 — a buffer
that relaxed on the first uptick would oscillate between states and
trade the market impact it was designed to avoid. The minting throttles
and pauses referenced in the table are executed by the state machine of
Chapter 18, which consumes the buffer state as an input.

To avoid market impact, transitions between BASE and STRESS are ramped
linearly over 24 hours, allowing the rebalancing engine to execute small
trades gradually rather than a single large one [BP §8.4]. For a BASE →
STRESS transition:

-   at t = 0: B_{gold} = 0.625;

-   at t = 24h: B_{gold} = 0.85;

-   the target is updated every hour along the linear path:

B_(gold,t) = 0.625 + (0.85 - 0.625) × t/(24)

Only the STRESS → EMERGENCY edge is unramped: when the hard region is
approached, defensive composition is assumed at once and the (bounded)
market impact of the shift is accepted as the smaller risk. Every hourly
step of a ramp, and every immediate transition, is itself executed
through the MARP gates of Chapters 10–11 — the buffer sets the target;
it does not bypass the executor.

16.4 Buffer Consumption — The First-Loss Waterfall

The buffer exists to be consumed before the core reserve in a crisis.
The protocol defines a first-loss waterfall for absorbing losses [BP
§8.5]:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Layer                   Asset                   Purpose
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  1. Operational Surplus  Fee revenue             Absorbs small,
                                                  temporary deviations.

  2. Buffer Fiat          37.5% / 15% of buffer   Absorbs medium-sized
                                                  losses (stablecoin
                                                  depegs).

  3. Buffer Gold          62.5% / 85% / 100% of   Absorbs larger losses
                          buffer                  (gold market
                                                  corrections).

  4. Core Fiat            80% of core             Absorbs severe losses
                                                  (last line before
                                                  gold).

  5. Core Gold            20% of core             Absolute last resort.
                                                  Protected by Exhaustion
                                                  Certificate (requires
                                                  4/7 Multi-Sig).
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

When a loss occurs (for example, a stablecoin depegs by 1%) [BP §8.5]:

1.  The loss is first deducted from the Operational Surplus (fee
    revenue).

2.  If surplus is insufficient, the loss is deducted from the Buffer
    Fiat (stablecoins).

3.  If buffer fiat is insufficient, the loss is deducted from the Buffer
    Gold.

4.  If buffer gold is insufficient, the loss is deducted from the Core
    Fiat.

5.  If core fiat is insufficient, the loss is deducted from the Core
    Gold (requires governance approval).

  This waterfall ensures the core reserve is protected for as long as
  possible [BP §8.5].

Two properties of the waterfall deserve emphasis. First, it is loss
accounting, not a trading program: attributing a depeg loss to the
buffer fiat layer does not by itself sell anything — it reclassifies
which layer the loss has consumed, and the rebalancing engine then
restores the layer boundaries through the ordinary, gated process.
Second, the ordering is risk-logical rather than value-logical: fiat
layers absorb before gold layers at the same seniority because fiat is
the liquid, redemption-facing side of the reserve (Section 13.4), and
gold — the strategic, illiquid-when-stressed asset — is spent last at
every level. The final layer, core gold, is protected by the Exhaustion
Certificate: a 4/7 Multi-Sig approval required before any consumption,
which makes core-gold use a visible, accountable governance event. This
is the sharpest expression of the gold-cost rule of Section 14.4 —
core-gold consumption is the exceptional condition par excellence, and
it composes with invariant I12 (no reserve asset used for
secondary-market price support).

16.4.1 Integration with the Rebalancing Engine

The Rebalancing Engine uses the effective total weights computed from
the buffer state [BP §8.6]. In the v1.2 parameterization:

W^(target)_(gold,total,t) = 0.20 + (0.10 × B_(gold)(RR_(t)))

W^(target)_(fiat,total,t) = 0.80 + (0.10 × (1 - B_(gold)(RR_(t))))

Under v2.0 the same composition rule holds with the adaptive core split
— W^{target}_{gold,total,t} = W^{core}_{gold,t} + 0.10 × B_{gold}(RR_t)
and its complement for fiat — per the fidelity note of Section 16.2.
These values are the gold and fiat targets handed to the rebalancing
engine. The flow is tightly coupled by construction [BP §8.6]:

1.  The Reserve Ratio is updated (Listing 6).

2.  The buffer state is evaluated (BASE / STRESS / EMERGENCY).

3.  The total target gold weight is computed.

4.  The Rebalancing Engine compares observed versus target and executes
    trades if needed.

This coupling means the buffer is automatically rebalanced as the
protocol's health changes — there is no separate buffer-keeper or
discretionary rebalance. Every waterfall consumption or state transition
changes the observed-versus-target gap, and the engine's response flows
through the same MARP gates, cost tests and concentration checks
(Chapters 10–11) as any other adjustment, so buffer defence never
becomes an ungated flow of trades. The execution side additionally
honors the crisis-mode restrictions of Section 14.5 whenever
CrisisScore_t is elevated, since buffer transitions and crisis posture
are most likely to co-occur.

16.5 Smart Contract Implementation — Dynamic Buffer

The listing below adapts BP §8.7 substantially complete: the constants
(BP §8.7.1), the state-machine and ramp logic (BP §8.7.2), the
first-loss waterfall (BP §8.7.3) and the events (BP §8.7.4). The buffer
contract reads the Reserve Ratio from the reserve manager (Listing 6),
drives minting pauses and emergency rebalances through the state machine
(Chapter 18), and tracks the five waterfall layers as explicit
accounting state. Core-gold consumption is gated by the Exhaustion
Certificate — a pre-approved amount issued by the 4/7 Emergency Council
(Section 16.4).

16.5.1 The Contract

Listing 8 — Dynamic buffer: states, transitions and first-loss waterfall
(adapts BP §8.7)

  // SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT

  pragma solidity ^0.8.19;

  /// @title DynamicBuffer — three defensive layers over the 100% core

  /// reserve, with ramped state transitions and the first-loss

  /// waterfall (§16.1–§16.4).

  contract DynamicBuffer {

  // ---- Governance ----

  address public emergencyCouncil; // 4/7 Multi-Sig

  // ============================================================

  // 1. CONSTANTS (BP §8.7.1)

  // ============================================================

  // Buffer constants

  uint256 public constant BUFFER_SIZE = 0.10e18; // 10% of liability

  uint256 public constant CORE_GOLD_WEIGHT = 0.20e18; // 20% of core is
  gold (v1.2 baseline)

  // Buffer state thresholds

  uint256 public constant RR_BASE_THRESHOLD = 1.10e18;

  uint256 public constant RR_STRESS_THRESHOLD = 1.05e18;

  // Buffer gold ratios for each state

  uint256 public constant BUFFER_GOLD_BASE = 0.625e18; // 62.5%

  uint256 public constant BUFFER_GOLD_STRESS = 0.85e18; // 85%

  uint256 public constant BUFFER_GOLD_EMERGENCY = 1.00e18; // 100%

  // Ramp duration (seconds)

  uint256 public constant RAMP_DURATION = 24 hours;

  // ---- State tracking ----

  enum BufferState { BASE, STRESS, EMERGENCY }

  BufferState public currentBufferState;

  // ---- Ramp tracking ----

  uint256 public rampStartTime;

  uint256 public rampStartGoldRatio;

  uint256 public rampTargetGoldRatio;

  // ---- Waterfall layer accounting (USD values, 1e18 scale) ----

  // Written by the reserve-accounting integration after every mint,

  // redeem, rebalance or price update (§16.3).

  uint256 public operationalSurplus; // layer 1 — fee revenue retained

  uint256 public bufferFiatValue; // layer 2 — buffer fiat

  uint256 public bufferGoldValue; // layer 3 — buffer gold

  uint256 public coreFiatValue; // layer 4 — core fiat

  uint256 public coreGoldValue; // layer 5 — core gold

  // ---- Core-gold Exhaustion Certificate (§16.4) ----

  uint256 public coreGoldExhaustionCertificate; // pre-approved amount

  // ---- External integrations ----

  IReserveManager public reserveManager; // Listing 6

  IStateMachine public stateMachine; // Chapter 18

  constructor(address _emergencyCouncil, address _reserveManager,
  address _stateMachine) {

  emergencyCouncil = _emergencyCouncil;

  reserveManager = IReserveManager(_reserveManager);

  stateMachine = IStateMachine(_stateMachine);

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 2. CORE LOGIC (BP §8.7.2)

  // ============================================================

  /// @dev Updates the buffer state based on the current Reserve Ratio.

  /// Called after every mint, redeem, rebalance, or price update.

  function updateBufferState() internal {

  uint256 rr = reserveManager.getReserveRatio();

  BufferState newState;

  if (rr >= RR_BASE_THRESHOLD) {

  newState = BufferState.BASE;

  } else if (rr >= RR_STRESS_THRESHOLD) {

  newState = BufferState.STRESS;

  } else {

  newState = BufferState.EMERGENCY;

  }

  // Handle state transitions

  if (newState != currentBufferState) {

  if (newState == BufferState.EMERGENCY) {

  // Immediate transition to EMERGENCY

  currentBufferState = newState;

  rampStartTime = 0; // no ramp for emergency

  emit BufferStateChanged(newState, "EMERGENCY", block.timestamp);

  // Pause minting; force the emergency gold target (§16.4.1)

  pauseMinting();

  triggerEmergencyRebalance();

  } else {

  // Ramp between BASE and STRESS

  rampStartTime = block.timestamp;

  rampStartGoldRatio = getCurrentBufferGoldRatio();

  rampTargetGoldRatio = (newState == BufferState.BASE)

  ? BUFFER_GOLD_BASE

  : BUFFER_GOLD_STRESS;

  currentBufferState = newState;

  emit BufferStateChanged(newState, "RAMPING", block.timestamp);

  }

  }

  }

  /// @dev Returns the current buffer gold ratio (considering ramps).

  function getCurrentBufferGoldRatio() public view returns (uint256) {

  if (currentBufferState == BufferState.EMERGENCY) {

  return BUFFER_GOLD_EMERGENCY;

  }

  if (rampStartTime == 0) {

  return (currentBufferState == BufferState.BASE)

  ? BUFFER_GOLD_BASE

  : BUFFER_GOLD_STRESS;

  }

  // Check if ramp is complete

  if (block.timestamp >= rampStartTime + RAMP_DURATION) {

  return rampTargetGoldRatio;

  }

  // Compute progress (0 to 1)

  uint256 elapsed = block.timestamp - rampStartTime;

  uint256 progress = (elapsed * 1e18) / RAMP_DURATION;

  // Interpolate

  uint256 diff = (rampTargetGoldRatio > rampStartGoldRatio)

  ? rampTargetGoldRatio - rampStartGoldRatio

  : rampStartGoldRatio - rampTargetGoldRatio;

  uint256 adjustment = (diff * progress) / 1e18;

  return (rampTargetGoldRatio > rampStartGoldRatio)

  ? rampStartGoldRatio + adjustment

  : rampStartGoldRatio - adjustment;

  }

  /// @dev Returns the total target gold weight (core + buffer).

  /// This is the value used by the Rebalancing Engine (Ch 10-11).

  function getTotalTargetGoldWeight() public view returns (uint256) {

  uint256 bufferGoldRatio = getCurrentBufferGoldRatio();

  return CORE_GOLD_WEIGHT + (BUFFER_SIZE * bufferGoldRatio / 1e18);

  }

  /// @dev Returns the total target fiat weight.

  function getTotalTargetFiatWeight() public view returns (uint256) {

  return 1e18 - getTotalTargetGoldWeight();

  }

  /// @dev Emergency function to force the buffer to 100% gold

  /// (Emergency State). Only callable by the 4/7 Emergency Council.

  function forceEmergencyBuffer() external onlyEmergencyCouncil {

  currentBufferState = BufferState.EMERGENCY;

  rampStartTime = 0;

  pauseMinting();

  triggerEmergencyRebalance();

  emit BufferStateChanged(BufferState.EMERGENCY, "FORCED",
  block.timestamp);

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 3. BUFFER CONSUMPTION (BP §8.7.3)

  // ============================================================

  /// @dev Applies a loss using the first-loss waterfall (§16.4).

  /// Called when a loss is detected (e.g., stablecoin depeg,

  /// oracle correction).

  function applyLoss(uint256 lossAmount) internal {

  uint256 remainingLoss = lossAmount;

  // 1. Consume operational surplus (fee revenue)

  if (operationalSurplus > 0) {

  uint256 consumed = (operationalSurplus >= remainingLoss)

  ? remainingLoss

  : operationalSurplus;

  remainingLoss -= consumed;

  consumeSurplus(consumed);

  emit BufferConsumed("operationalSurplus", consumed, remainingLoss);

  if (remainingLoss == 0) return;

  }

  // 2. Consume buffer fiat

  if (bufferFiatValue > 0) {

  uint256 consumed = (bufferFiatValue >= remainingLoss)

  ? remainingLoss

  : bufferFiatValue;

  remainingLoss -= consumed;

  consumeBufferFiat(consumed);

  emit BufferConsumed("bufferFiat", consumed, remainingLoss);

  if (remainingLoss == 0) return;

  }

  // 3. Consume buffer gold

  if (bufferGoldValue > 0) {

  uint256 consumed = (bufferGoldValue >= remainingLoss)

  ? remainingLoss

  : bufferGoldValue;

  remainingLoss -= consumed;

  consumeBufferGold(consumed);

  emit BufferConsumed("bufferGold", consumed, remainingLoss);

  if (remainingLoss == 0) return;

  }

  // 4. Consume core fiat (emergency)

  if (coreFiatValue > 0) {

  uint256 consumed = (coreFiatValue >= remainingLoss)

  ? remainingLoss

  : coreFiatValue;

  remainingLoss -= consumed;

  consumeCoreFiat(consumed);

  emit BufferConsumed("coreFiat", consumed, remainingLoss);

  if (remainingLoss == 0) return;

  }

  // 5. Consume core gold (requires governance approval —

  // the Exhaustion Certificate, §16.4)

  if (remainingLoss > 0) {

  requireGovernanceApprovalForCoreGoldConsumption(remainingLoss);

  consumeCoreGold(remainingLoss);

  emit BufferConsumed("coreGold", remainingLoss, 0);

  }

  }

  // ---- Layer consumption helpers ----

  function consumeSurplus(uint256 amount) internal {

  operationalSurplus -= amount;

  }

  function consumeBufferFiat(uint256 amount) internal {

  bufferFiatValue -= amount;

  }

  function consumeBufferGold(uint256 amount) internal {

  bufferGoldValue -= amount;

  }

  function consumeCoreFiat(uint256 amount) internal {

  coreFiatValue -= amount;

  }

  function consumeCoreGold(uint256 amount) internal {

  coreGoldValue -= amount;

  }

  /// @dev Core-gold consumption requires the Exhaustion Certificate: a

  /// pre-registered 4/7 Emergency-Council approval (§16.4).

  function requireGovernanceApprovalForCoreGoldConsumption(uint256
  amount)

  internal view

  {

  require(

  coreGoldExhaustionCertificate >= amount,

  "Core gold: no exhaustion certificate"

  );

  }

  /// @notice Issue the core-gold Exhaustion Certificate (4/7).

  function issueCoreGoldExhaustionCertificate(uint256 amount)

  external onlyEmergencyCouncil

  {

  coreGoldExhaustionCertificate = amount;

  }

  // ---- State machine hooks (Chapter 18) ----

  function pauseMinting() internal {

  stateMachine.pauseMinting();

  }

  function triggerEmergencyRebalance() internal {

  stateMachine.triggerEmergencyRebalance();

  }

  modifier onlyEmergencyCouncil() {

  require(msg.sender == emergencyCouncil, "Only Emergency Council");

  _;

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 4. EVENT EMISSIONS (BP §8.7.4)

  // ============================================================

  event BufferStateChanged(

  BufferState indexed newState,

  string action,

  uint256 timestamp

  );

  uint256 indexed startRatio,

  uint256 indexed targetRatio,

  uint256 progress

  );

  event BufferConsumed(

  string layer,

  uint256 amount,

  uint256 remainingLoss

  );

  }

  interface IReserveManager {

  function getReserveRatio() external view returns (uint256);

  }

  interface IStateMachine {

  function pauseMinting() external;

  function triggerEmergencyRebalance() external;

  }

  Fidelity note (BP §8.7 → Listing 8): (1) BP §8.7.3 referenced
  undefined accounting hooks (getBufferFiatValue, consumeSurplus, and so
  on); this listing realizes them as explicit layer state with decrement
  helpers, and emits BufferConsumed at each waterfall step — BP declared
  the event but never showed its emission. (2) The core-gold governance
  approval is implemented as the Exhaustion Certificate: a pre-approved
  amount issued by the 4/7 Emergency Council, matching the §16.4
  protection of the core-gold layer. (3) pauseMinting and
  triggerEmergencyRebalance are routed through the Chapter 18 state
  machine interface rather than left dangling. (4) CORE_GOLD_WEIGHT =
  0.20e18 and the 62.5%/85%/100% ratios are v1.2 constants retained as
  validation-stage initialization values; under v2.0 the core gold share
  is adaptive per the Section 16.2 fidelity note, and the buffer
  constants remain Risk-Council parameters under the scopes of the table
  below. (5) BufferRampUpdated is emitted by the hourly keeper that
  advances ramps (§16.3); the ramp interpolation itself is pure view
  logic carried verbatim from BP §8.7.2.

16.5.2 Developer-Facing Constants

The buffer's developer-facing constants are carried from BP §8.8. The
constitutional items — buffer size, core gold weight and the two RR
thresholds — bind the buffer's geometry and can change only through the
7/7 process; the defensive gold ratios and the ramp duration are
Risk-Council parameters within that geometry, so the council can tune
how defensively the buffer behaves but not whether it exists or how
large it is.

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Parameter         Symbol                  Value             Governance
  ----------------- ----------------------- ----------------- -----------------
  Buffer Size       BUFFER_SIZE             10% of liability  Constitutional
                                                              (immutable)

  Core Gold Weight  CORE_GOLD_WEIGHT        20%               Constitutional
                                                              (immutable)

  Buffer Gold       BUFFER_GOLD_BASE        62.5%             Risk Council
  (BASE)                                                      (4/7)

  Buffer Gold       BUFFER_GOLD_STRESS      85%               Risk Council
  (STRESS)                                                    (4/7)

  (EMERGENCY)                                                 (4/7)

                                                              (4/7)

Deployment wiring: set emergencyCouncil to the 4/7 Multi-Sig,
reserveManager to the Listing 6 deployment, and stateMachine to the
Chapter 18 machine; initialize the layer values from the first
reserve-state snapshot; and leave the Exhaustion Certificate at zero so
that core-gold consumption is impossible until the Emergency Council
affirmatively issues one. The buffer's thresholds deliberately mirror
the RR tiers of Section 14.2.1 — the same 1.10 and 1.05 values appear as
the constitutional constants of both contracts — so the solvency ladder
and the buffer ladder are two readings of one measurement, and the
stress program of Section 23.11 validates them jointly rather than
per-contract.

17. Oracle Architecture and the Canonical Gold Price

17.1 Purpose and Design Principles

  The Oracle Architecture is the data ingestion layer of the protocol
  [BP §9.1]. It provides reliable, tamper-resistant price data for every
  asset in the reference basket, the reference index, and the macro
  signals (VIX, DXY) — and, in v2.0, for the canonical gold price that
  defines the gold component's reference-unit value. Everything upstream
  in this document is only as trustworthy as this layer: the index of
  Chapter 9, the MARP execution gates of Chapter 11, the reserve
  valuation of Chapters 13–14, the instrument eligibility of Chapter 15
  and the mint/redemption pricing of Chapter 19 all consume oracle
  outputs directly.

The architecture is designed to be defense-in-depth: no single oracle
failure can corrupt the system [BP §9.1]. It uses multiple independent
sources, validates each data point against strict criteria, and
implements fallback logic so that the protocol remains operational
during partial outages. The governing principle is carried verbatim from
the v1.2 blueprint:

  Trust, but verify. And never trust a single source.

One rule from the Modification Specification sharpens this principle for
gold. MTQ Σ must not use a single MTQ-native AMM as the canonical
gold-price source [MS §53]. The earlier architecture relied heavily on
its own SGTX-G market to generate the gold price — a circular
arrangement in which the protocol's own token market defined the value
of the gold it was supposed to be measuring, so that thin trading or
manipulation in one pool could move the unit definition itself. MTQ Σ
instead aggregates independent sources: external benchmarks, derivatives
venues and tokenized-gold feeds whose errors are, at best, weakly
correlated with the protocol's own activity.

This chapter is the enforcement point of Invariant I9 (Section 2.6): all
oracle prices must pass timestamp, confidence and deviation checks, and
the canonical gold price requires an independent-source quorum. Sections
17.3 through 17.7 — validation criteria, the canonical gold price,
aggregation logic, confidence mathematics and degradation modes — are
pinned sections that the constitution references directly. The chapter
closes with the staleness/heartbeat regime (Section 17.8), correlation
detection (Section 17.9), the address and constant inventories (Sections
17.10–17.11) and the reference implementation (Listing 9, Section
17.12).

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Consumer                Data Consumed           Consequence of Oracle
                                                  Failure
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Reference index I_t     FX pairs and the        An index error
  (Chapter 9)             canonical gold price    propagates directly
                                                  into P_{MTQ,t} and
                                                  every downstream price.

  MASE and MARP (Chapters Validated price         Corrupted inputs would
  6–11)                   histories for           distort weights and
                          covariance, regime and  justify uneconomic
                          execution signals       trades.

  Reserve valuation       Asset prices,           NAV, RR_t and haircuts
  (Chapters 13–14)        conservative gold price misstate solvency.

  Minting and redemption  Guarded reference price Mispriced mints and
  (Chapter 19)                                    redemptions transfer
                                                  value between users and
                                                  the reserve.

  Risk states (Chapter    Oracle health,          Degradation must drive
  21)                     confidence scores,      WATCH/ALERT escalation,
                          warnings                not silent operation.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

17.2 The Three-Source Consensus Model

For every price feed, the protocol queries three independent oracle
providers [BP §9.2]. The v1.2 deployment standard — retained in v2.0 as
the reference configuration — is the Chainlink / Pyth / Chronicle triad:

  ------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Provider          Type              Update Mechanism  Key Strength
  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------
  Chainlink         Push-based        Aggregator        Industry standard;
                                      contract, updated proven
                                      on deviation or   reliability; wide
                                      heartbeat         asset coverage.

  Pyth Network      Pull-based        On-demand price   High frequency;
                                      updates with      sub-second
                                      confidence        updates;
                                      intervals         confidence scores.

  Chronicle         Verifiable        Signed price      Oracle-agnostic;
  Protocol                            statements from   verifiable via
                                      independent       Schnorr
                                      reporters         signatures; MCD
                                                        compatible.
  ------------------------------------------------------------------------

The three providers are deliberately heterogeneous in delivery model.
Chainlink pushes updates through aggregator contracts, Pyth serves
on-demand pull updates carrying confidence intervals, and Chronicle
publishes signed statements from independent reporters that anyone can
verify. Heterogeneity is the point: push, pull and signed-statement
architectures fail in different ways, so a compromise or outage in one
family rarely propagates to the others. Provider substitution (for
example, a Redstone-style verifiable-data source in place of Chronicle)
is permitted by governance without changing the model, provided the
source-family independence test of Section 17.3.4 still passes.

The consensus rule for combining the available feeds is [BP §9.2]:

-   If all three are available: use the median price.

-   If only two are available: use the average of the two, with a sanity
    check.

-   If fewer than two are available: pause minting and rebalancing until
    feeds are restored.

Why median over average with three sources: the median is robust to a
single outlier (for example, one oracle manipulated), whereas an average
can be skewed by an extreme value even when two sources agree. The
median sacrifices a little statistical efficiency — it ignores some
information the average uses — in exchange for a bounded worst case: one
fully compromised feed can move the median by at most the gap between
the two honest feeds. That asymmetry is exactly the trade a monetary
protocol should make.

This rule defines the oracle quorum, the term used throughout this
document for the minimum number of valid, independent feeds required
before any price may be published or consumed:

N_(valid) ≥ 2 and |source families| ≥ 2

The quorum threshold — at least two feeds, drawn from at least two
different source families — is a constitutional constant (Section 17.10)
changeable only by the 7/7 Constitutional Multi-Sig with the 90-day
timelock. The quorum is deliberately expressed independently of the
number of configured sources: if a future methodology version configures
five providers, the quorum rule is re-derived by governance and
re-published, never silently recomputed.

17.3 Validation Criteria

Each price point must pass four independent checks before it is
considered valid [BP §9.3]. The four checks — timestamp freshness
(17.3.1), confidence interval (17.3.2), deviation circuit breaker
(17.3.3) and source independence (17.3.4) — are jointly the timestamp,
confidence and deviation checks of Invariant I9 (Section 2.6). A feed
that fails any applicable check is discarded for the round; it is not
averaged in at reduced weight, because reduced weight is reserved for
the correlation regime of Section 17.9. The four checks are independent
in the sense that each catches a distinct failure mode: stale data,
uncertain data, manipulated data and correlated data.

17.3.1 Timestamp Freshness

The price must be updated within a predefined staleness threshold [BP
§9.3.1]. Staleness is measured against the provider's own update
timestamp, not the time of the on-chain read, so that a contract cannot
launder an old price into apparent freshness by querying it late:

t_(now) - τ_(i) ≤ θ_(stale)(feed)

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Feed Type               Maximum Age             Action if Stale
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Major FX (EUR/USD,      60 seconds              Discard feed
  GBP/USD)                                        

  Minor FX (JPY/USD,      120 seconds             Discard feed
  CNY/USD)                                        

  Gold (PAXG/XAU)         60 seconds              Discard feed

  VIX, DXY                120 seconds             Discard feed
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The bounds differ by feed class because the underlying markets update at
different cadences: deep major-FX pairs and gold reprice continuously,
while minor pairs and index-like signals move on slightly coarser
schedules. The 60-second bound for gold matches the major-FX class
because the canonical gold price must never be older than the currencies
it is aggregated with in the index (Chapter 9). Both bounds are
constitutional constants (Section 17.10); the implementation is the
require-style guard carried in Listing 9.

17.3.2 Confidence Interval (Pyth-Specific)

Pyth provides a confidence interval around each price. The contract
discards Pyth prices where the interval is too wide, because a wide
interval indicates low liquidity or high volatility in the underlying
aggregation — the price is formally present but materially uncertain [BP
§9.3.2]:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Asset                               Max Confidence Width (as % of
                                      price)
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  Major FX (EUR/USD)                  0.5%

  Minor FX (JPY/USD, CNY/USD)         1.0%

  Gold (PAXG)                         1.0%

  VIX, DXY                            2.0%
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Providers that do not publish a confidence interval are not exempt from
uncertainty; they simply express it differently. Chainlink feeds carry
deviation thresholds and heartbeats that bound how stale or how moved a
price can be before the aggregator itself updates, and Chronicle's
signed statements carry the reporter's timestamp, which feeds the
freshness check of 17.3.1. The Pyth check is retained as an explicit,
quantitative gate because the interval is the most direct on-chain
expression of price uncertainty available. The width caps are
Risk-Council parameters (4/7) with the initial values above.

17.3.3 Deviation Circuit Breaker

If any feed deviates by more than 2.5% from the median of the available
feeds, it is discarded as anomalous [BP §9.3.3]:

|P_(i) - P̃| ≤ 0.025 P̃

Worked example from the v1.2 blueprint: Feed 1 = 1.00, Feed 2 = 1.02,
Feed 3 = 1.25. The median of the available feeds is 1.01, so the maximum
tolerated deviation is 0.0253; Feed 3 deviates by 0.24 and is discarded,
and the protocol proceeds on Feeds 1 and 2. The breaker runs after
per-feed validation, so a price must be fresh, confident and
non-anomalous simultaneously to count. The 2.5% threshold is a
Risk-Council parameter (4/7): wide enough that ordinary FX and gold
noise never fires it, narrow enough that a manipulation attempt large
enough to matter cannot survive it.

17.3.4 Source Independence Check

To prevent correlated failure — for example, Chainlink and Pyth both
sourcing from the same underlying liquidity pool — the protocol verifies
that at least two feeds come from different source families [BP §9.3.4].
If all feeds are derived from the same primary source, the protocol
flags SOURCE_CORRELATION_WARNING and uses a weighted average based on
historical reliability. The family registry, which maps each configured
provider to a source family, is maintained by governance so that a new
provider cannot silently join a family that is already over-represented.

For the canonical gold price the independence requirement is stricter,
because gold sources cluster more than FX sources. The quorum of Section
17.4 requires at least two distinct source classes — LBMA-basis
benchmarks, derivatives venues, tokenized-gold markets — among the valid
feeds. A quorum drawn only from tokenized-gold pools fails the
independence test even if three separate feeds agree, because all three
measure the same market microstructure and can fail together.

17.4 The Gold Price Reference Unit and Canonical Gold Price

The gold component of the reference basket is defined on the gold price
reference unit: the USD price of one reference unit of gold — an
investment-grade, deliverable troy-ounce-equivalent quantity struck on
the LBMA spot basis [MS §54, BP §9.8]. The reference unit is a price
concept, not a token: no specific token's market price defines the unit,
and gold exposure in the reference basket is denominated in the
reference unit rather than in PAXG, XAUT or any future MTQ-native
instrument. This is the decoupling specified in Section 4.8 — SGTX-G is
not inherited — and it is what allows the canonical gold price to be
source-independent.

The canonical gold price is constructed by robust aggregation over
independent sources P_1, P_2, ..., P_n [MS §54]:

P_(G)^(raw) = Median(P₁, …, P_(n))

or a robust weighted median, with weights reflecting per-source
reliability. Both forms are admissible under a methodology version; the
plain median is the default because it requires no reliability weights
to be trusted, and the weighted median is the perturbation alternative
(Section 23.6). The raw aggregate then passes through the canonical
filter before it may be consumed:

  P_(G)^(canonical) = Filter(P_(G)^(raw), Staleness, Liquidity, Outlier)

The Filter applies, in order: the staleness criteria of Section 17.3.1
(stale sources are dropped before aggregation), the liquidity-quality
screen, the outlier deviation breaker of Section 17.3.3, and finally the
confidence gate of Section 17.6 — a filtered price whose confidence
falls below C_min is not published as canonical at all. The three
independent source classes feeding the aggregation are: (i) LBMA-basis
spot benchmarks and their distributor feeds (the reference class); (ii)
derivatives venues — COMEX-style futures rolled to spot and OTC
benchmarks; and (iii) tokenized-gold market prices (PAXG/XAUT spot). The
independent-source quorum requires at least two distinct classes among
the valid feeds, per Section 17.3.4.

Gold also receives special handling because tokenized gold trades
continuously while issuer redemption does not [BP §9.8]. PAXG may trade
24/7 on secondary markets, but direct issuer redemption is only
available during London gold market hours. The protocol therefore
distinguishes the reference gold price (LBMA spot basis, used for index
valuation — Chapter 9) from the executable gold price (tokenized-gold
market price, used for rebalancing execution — Chapter 11). For reserve
valuation the protocol uses the more conservative of the two:

P_(gold,t) = min(P_(reference,t), P_(executable,t))

The conservative minimum prices the reserve at what it could actually
realize at the valuation instant, not at the benchmark it would prefer
to believe; Chapters 13–14 apply their haircuts on top of this floor,
and MARP execution trades against the executable price (Chapter 11). The
min rule is deliberately asymmetric — it can only reduce NAV, never
inflate it — so it is safe to apply in exactly the states (feed
degradation, market stress) where the two price classes are most likely
to diverge. The canonical gold price then feeds the gold fixing of the
index (Chapter 9) and stands in the same relation to the genesis base
fixing BASE_GOLD_USD (Section 2.7) that the FX base fixings stand to
their pairs.

17.5 Price Aggregation Logic

The aggregation pipeline of Listing 9 implements three functions —
getPrice(), validatePrice() and getMedian() — specified in BP §9.4 and
described here in prose [BP §9.4]. The getPrice() path runs in six
steps:

1.  Fetch: query all three providers for the feed identifier (Chainlink,
    Pyth, Chronicle).

2.  Validate: run validatePrice() (or validatePricePyth()) on each feed
    — see below.

3.  Quorum: require at least two valid feeds (constitutional, Invariant
    I9).

4.  Deviation breaker: compute the median of the valid prices and
    discard any feed more than 2.5% away from it.

5.  Cache: store the last-known-good price, append the aggregate to
    price history and emit PriceUpdated.

6.  Aggregate: return the average of the validated, non-outlier prices.

A feed is valid when it satisfies the three per-feed conditions of
validatePrice() — positivity, freshness and plausible range [BP §9.4.2]:

valid_(i) = (P_(i) > 0) wedge (t_(now) - τ_(i) ≤ θ_(stale,i)) wedge
(P_(min,i) ≤ P_(i) ≤ P_(max,i))

The plausible range [P_min, P_max] is a coarse sanity band set far wider
than the deviation breaker; it exists to catch gross errors — wrong
decimal scaling, a compromised feed returning garbage — before they can
even participate in a median. For Pyth feeds the confidence-interval
check of Section 17.3.2 is added on top. The deviation anchor is
computed by getMedian():

P̃ = Median({P_(i) : valid_(i)}) P^(agg) = 1/(|S|) ∑_(i ∈ S) P_(i)

where S is the set of feeds that survived both validation and the
breaker. Two conventions should be noted. First, the returned aggregate
is the outlier-filtered mean, and the median is the validity anchor: the
breaker measures deviations from the median even when the published
number is the filtered mean, so the median governs what may count while
the mean extracts the information from what does count. When all three
feeds survive and cluster tightly, the two statistics coincide to within
tolerance, which is why the consensus rule of Section 17.2 (median of
three) and the implementation agree in the normal case. Second,
getMedian() takes the upper middle element for even-length arrays — the
BP convention, carried deliberately in Listing 9.

The full Solidity implementation, including the gold special handling of
Section 17.4 and the confidence hook of Section 17.6, is Listing 9 in
Section 17.12.

17.6 Gold Oracle Confidence Mathematics

The v1.2 blueprint supplied the oracle architecture but not the
confidence calculation; COO Review item 20 closes that gap [COO-20]. The
gold oracle publishes not just a price but a confidence score — a number
in [0, 1] stating how much the protocol should trust the canonical gold
price at this instant. The review's key point is that a confidence
function is much better than relying on a simple source count: three
agreeing sources can still be stale, illiquid or mutually correlated,
and a single high-quality source can be more trustworthy than two
degraded ones. The count is an input; the function is the judgment.

  Confidence_(G) = f(SourceAgreement, Freshness, Liquidity, Deviation,
  SourceCoverage)

Confidence_(G) < C_(min) ⇒ OracleDegraded

Each input factor is itself a normalized quantity in [0, 1], computed
from the current round state of the gold feeds:

SourceAgreement. The dispersion of the currently valid source prices
around their median. High agreement means the sources cluster tightly,
so the canonical price is well identified; a wide spread means the true
price is ambiguous and every downstream calculation inherits that
ambiguity. Agreement is the earliest symptom of one bad or manipulated
feed, appearing before any single check formally fails.

Freshness. The age of the newest valid source updates, decayed linearly
to zero at the staleness thresholds of Section 17.3.1. A price that is
technically valid but drifting toward staleness deserves less trust than
one just published, and Freshness encodes that gradient continuously
instead of as a cliff. In Listing 9 the factor decays with the age of
the last aggregation round relative to the feed's staleness threshold.

Liquidity. The depth and quality of the underlying markets the sources
measure — LBMA session state for the reference class, pool depth and
spread quality for tokenized gold. A price quoted on thin markets is
easier to manipulate and harder to realize, so liquidity enters the
trust calculation directly rather than being treated as an
execution-only concern. The input is posted by the approved keeper and
is Risk-Council bounded.

Deviation. The magnitude of the latest aggregate move relative to the
recent band. A canonical price that has just jumped several percent in
one update is either genuinely important news or a feed problem, and
until the sources confirm each other, confidence is reduced. This factor
makes the score dynamic in fast markets, not only in outages — exactly
when the protocol most needs to know whether it can trust its gold
price.

SourceCoverage. The fraction of configured independent sources — and,
more importantly, independent source families — currently valid. This is
the old source count, demoted from a binary gate to one weighted input
among five: three agreeing sources from one family contribute less than
two from distinct families, because family diversity is what makes
errors independent. Coverage therefore internalizes the independence
check of Section 17.3.4 into the score itself.
