Rows with constitutional authority are immutable in the reference
implementation: the liquidation ladder's cumulative percentages, the
reintegration staging, the hard floor RR = 1.00 and the three timelocks
cannot be changed by any on-chain path — they require redeployment
through the constitutional process. Rows marked Monetary and Risk are
live-updatable through the proposal paths of Listing 14. Parameters
introduced by later chapters — validation outputs of Chapter 23,
publication intervals of Chapter 24 — are registered at deployment under
the same rules; the matrix is closed under the registry, and any
parameter not listed here has no update path at all. The Emergency layer
deliberately registers actions rather than parameters: the emergency
action set itself is constitutional, so the fastest authority in the
system cannot create new knobs to turn.

  The buffer-gold rows are carried from BP §14.4.2 for the reserve-side
  gold overlay. Under v2.0 the reserve gold allocation is sized
  independently of the reference-basket weight (Invariant I12, §13.3),
  so these parameters govern reserve posture only, never index
  composition.

22.5 Emergency Actions

The Emergency layer holds the protocol's fastest powers, and every one
of them is a verb — pause, force, eject, resume — rather than a setting
[BP §14.5]. Emergency authority can never change a parameter, never set
a weight and never amend the constitution; it can only halt or force the
operations the state machine would otherwise run:

  --------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Action               Description       Trigger           Authority
  -------------------- ----------------- ----------------- -----------------
  pauseMinting()       Stops all new     RR < 1.05         Automatic +
                       minting           (automatic) OR    Council
                                         Council decision  

  pauseRedeeming()     Stops all         EMERGENCY state + Council (4/7)
                       redemptions       Council vote      
                       (extreme cases                      
                       only)                               

  forceRebalance()     Executes an       EMERGENCY state   Council (4/7)
                       immediate                           
                       rebalance                           

  emergencyEject()     Immediately       Issuer freeze     Council (4/7)
                       ejects a          confirmed         
                       de-pegged asset                     

  resumeOperations()   Restores normal   Recovery          Council (4/7)
                       operations        confirmed         
  --------------------------------------------------------------------------

The trigger column shows the two-speed design. The first pause — minting
at RR < 1.05 — is automatic: the state machine applies it the moment
STRESS is entered (Section 21.4), so the protocol's first defensive move
does not wait for humans. The remaining actions are council-triggered,
because they trade user-facing continuity against risk in ways that
require judgment. Pausing redemptions is the most drastic user-facing
action in the system and requires the EMERGENCY state plus a council
vote. A forced rebalance is a discretionary override of the MARP
doctrine's justification gates and corresponds to Level 5 of the
execution hierarchy (Section 10.3). The emergency eject bypasses the
staged liquidation ladder and executes through emergencyLiquidate
(Section 21.6) when an issuer freeze is confirmed. And resumeOperations
is deliberately blocked while the state machine is still in EMERGENCY:
recovery, not decree, must end an existential state.

Checks and balances. Every emergency action requires the 4/7 Emergency
Council Multi-Sig with hardware wallet requirements, emits an on-chain
event, and is reported through the transparency layer (Chapter 24) and
the honest-status declaration (Section 25). The oracle layer's circuit
breakers (Chapter 17) engage automatically in EMERGENCY, so price-trust
failures and solvency failures brake together. The state machine
continues to run underneath the council's actions: an emergency pause
does not freeze RR/LCR reporting, state transitions or the recovery
clock, so the council cannot use a pause to obscure the condition it is
pausing about. Emergency power, in short, is instant but narrow,
recorded, and reversible only by recovery — the exact trade the
four-layer hierarchy of Section 22.3 is designed to force.

22.6 Smart Contract Implementation — Governance and Emergency Functions

The governance registry is the authorization plane of the protocol [BP
§14.6.4–14.6.5]. It owns the parameter registry and the proposal flow:
the DAO proposes and executes monetary changes across a 48-hour
timelock, the Risk Council owns the 24-hour risk path and holds a veto
over monetary proposals inside their window, the Constitutional Council
registers parameters and their envelopes, and the Emergency Council
dispatches the pause, force and resume actions of Section 22.5. Every
executed update is forwarded to the operations contract of Listing 13
through the IOperations interface, and the operations contract
re-verifies its caller before applying the value — authorization and
execution each check the other. The emergency eject is the single
exception to the dispatch pattern: emergencyLiquidate is signed directly
by the Emergency Council on the operations contract (Section 21.6),
exactly as in the source module. The onlyDAO modifier is implemented by
a DAO contract with 51% quorum, and the emergency modifier by a 4/7
Multi-Sig with hardware wallet requirements [BP §14].

Listing 14 — Governance parameter registry and emergency functions
(adapts BP §14.6.4-14.6.5)

  // SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT

  pragma solidity ^0.8.19;

  /// @notice Governance parameter registry and emergency functions.

  /// @dev Authorization plane: role checks, timelocks and the parameter

  /// registry. The operations contract (Listing 13) is the execution

  /// plane and re-checks caller identity on arrival.

  contract GovernanceParameterRegistry {

  // ============================================================

  // 1. GOVERNANCE LAYERS (BP §14.4.1)

  // ============================================================

  enum ParameterLayer { CONSTITUTIONAL, MONETARY, RISK, EMERGENCY }

  struct ParameterRecord {

  ParameterLayer layer; // governance class (I7/I8, §2.6)

  uint256 value; // current live value

  uint256 minValue; // admissible envelope, lower bound

  uint256 maxValue; // admissible envelope, upper bound

  bool registered;

  }

  struct Proposal {

  uint256 value;

  address proposer;

  uint256 timestamp;

  bool executed;

  }

  // ---- Timelocks (constitutional, immutable; BP §14.8) ----

  uint256 public constant TIMELOCK_MONETARY = 48 hours;

  uint256 public constant TIMELOCK_RISK = 24 hours;

  uint256 public constant TIMELOCK_CONSTITUTIONAL = 90 days; // enforced
  by the 7/7 process

  // ---- Governance addresses (set at deployment) ----

  address public dao; // DAO with 51% quorum

  address public riskCouncil; // 4/7 Multi-Sig

  address public emergencyCouncil; // 4/7 Multi-Sig, hardware wallets

  address public constitutionalCouncil; // 7/7 Multi-Sig

  // ---- Registry and proposals ----

  mapping(bytes32 => ParameterRecord) public parameterRegistry;

  mapping(bytes32 => Proposal) public proposals;

  // ---- Execution plane (risk state machine, Listing 13) ----

  address public ops;

  // ============================================================

  // 2. EVENTS (BP §14.7)

  // ============================================================

  event ParameterRegistered(bytes32 indexed parameterId, ParameterLayer
  layer, uint256 value, uint256 minValue, uint256 maxValue, uint256
  timestamp);

  event ParameterProposed(bytes32 indexed parameterId, uint256 newValue,
  uint256 timestamp);

  event ParameterExecuted(bytes32 indexed parameterId, uint256 newValue,
  uint256 timestamp);

  event ParameterVetoed(bytes32 indexed parameterId, uint256 timestamp);

  event MintingPaused(uint256 timestamp);

  event MintingResumed(uint256 timestamp);

  event RedeemingPaused(uint256 timestamp);

  event RedeemingResumed(uint256 timestamp);

  event RebalancingForced(uint256 timestamp);

  event OperationsResumed(uint256 timestamp);

  modifier onlyDAO() { require(msg.sender == dao, "Only DAO"); _; }

  modifier onlyRiskCouncil() { require(msg.sender == riskCouncil, "Only
  Risk Council"); _; }

  modifier onlyEmergencyCouncil() { require(msg.sender ==
  emergencyCouncil, "Only Emergency Council"); _; }

  modifier onlyConstitutionalCouncil() { require(msg.sender ==
  constitutionalCouncil, "Only Constitutional Council"); _; }

  constructor(address dao_, address riskCouncil_, address
  emergencyCouncil_, address constitutionalCouncil_, address ops_) {

  dao = dao_;

  riskCouncil = riskCouncil_;

  emergencyCouncil = emergencyCouncil_;

  constitutionalCouncil = constitutionalCouncil_;

  ops = ops_;

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 3. PARAMETER REGISTRATION (Constitutional; §22.4)

  // ============================================================

  /// @dev Registers a tunable parameter with its governance class and

  /// admissible envelope. Unregistered parameters cannot be updated

  /// (fail-closed). Envelope changes are constitutional actions with

  /// the 90-day timelock enforced by the multi-sig process.

  function registerParameter(

  bytes32 parameterId,

  ParameterLayer layer,

  uint256 value,

  uint256 minValue,

  uint256 maxValue

  ) external onlyConstitutionalCouncil {

  require(minValue <= value && value <= maxValue, "Value outside
  envelope");

  parameterRegistry[parameterId] = ParameterRecord({

  layer: layer,

  value: value,

  minValue: minValue,

  maxValue: maxValue,

  registered: true

  });

  emit ParameterRegistered(parameterId, layer, value, minValue,
  maxValue, block.timestamp);

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 4. MONETARY PARAMETER PATH (DAO 51% + 48h; BP §14.6.4)

  // ============================================================

  /// @dev Proposes a monetary parameter change (DAO).

  function proposeMonetaryParameter(bytes32 parameterId, uint256
  newValue) external onlyDAO {

  require(parameterRegistry[parameterId].registered, "Unknown
  parameter");

  require(parameterRegistry[parameterId].layer ==
  ParameterLayer.MONETARY, "Not a monetary parameter");

  require(newValue >= parameterRegistry[parameterId].minValue, "Below
  envelope");

  require(newValue <= parameterRegistry[parameterId].maxValue, "Above
  envelope");

  proposals[parameterId] = Proposal({

  value: newValue,

  proposer: msg.sender,

  timestamp: block.timestamp,

  executed: false

  });

  emit ParameterProposed(parameterId, newValue, block.timestamp);

  }

  /// @dev Executes a monetary parameter change after the 48h timelock.

  function executeMonetaryParameter(bytes32 parameterId) external
  onlyDAO {

  Proposal memory proposal = proposals[parameterId];

  require(proposal.timestamp > 0, "No proposal");

  require(block.timestamp >= proposal.timestamp + TIMELOCK_MONETARY,
  "Timelock not met");

  require(!proposal.executed, "Already executed");

  // Update the parameter

  updateParameter(parameterId, proposal.value);

  proposals[parameterId].executed = true;

  emit ParameterExecuted(parameterId, proposal.value, block.timestamp);

  }

  /// @dev Vetoes a monetary parameter change (Risk Council, within the

  /// timelock window).

  function vetoMonetaryParameter(bytes32 parameterId) external
  onlyRiskCouncil {

  require(proposals[parameterId].timestamp > 0, "No proposal");

  require(block.timestamp < proposals[parameterId].timestamp +
  TIMELOCK_MONETARY, "Timelock expired");

  delete proposals[parameterId];

  emit ParameterVetoed(parameterId, block.timestamp);

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 5. RISK PARAMETER PATH (Risk Council 4/7 + 24h; extends BP §14.6.4)

  // ============================================================

  /// @dev Proposes a risk parameter change (Risk Council).

  function proposeRiskParameter(bytes32 parameterId, uint256 newValue)
  external onlyRiskCouncil {

  require(parameterRegistry[parameterId].registered, "Unknown
  parameter");

  require(parameterRegistry[parameterId].layer == ParameterLayer.RISK,
  "Not a risk parameter");

  require(newValue >= parameterRegistry[parameterId].minValue, "Below
  envelope");

  require(newValue <= parameterRegistry[parameterId].maxValue, "Above
  envelope");

  proposals[parameterId] = Proposal({

  value: newValue,

  proposer: msg.sender,

  timestamp: block.timestamp,

  executed: false

  });

  emit ParameterProposed(parameterId, newValue, block.timestamp);

  }

  /// @dev Executes a risk parameter change after the 24h timelock.

  function executeRiskParameter(bytes32 parameterId) external
  onlyRiskCouncil {

  Proposal memory proposal = proposals[parameterId];

  require(proposal.timestamp > 0, "No proposal");

  require(block.timestamp >= proposal.timestamp + TIMELOCK_RISK,
  "Timelock not met");

  require(!proposal.executed, "Already executed");

  updateParameter(parameterId, proposal.value);

  proposals[parameterId].executed = true;

  emit ParameterExecuted(parameterId, proposal.value, block.timestamp);

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 6. PARAMETER DISPATCH

  // ============================================================

  /// @dev Writes the accepted value to the registry and forwards it to
  the

  /// operations contract (Listing 13). The envelope is re-checked on

  /// arrival; unregistered parameters fail closed.

  function updateParameter(bytes32 parameterId, uint256 newValue)
  internal {

  ParameterRecord storage record = parameterRegistry[parameterId];

  require(record.registered, "Unknown parameter");

  require(newValue >= record.minValue && newValue <= record.maxValue,
  "Outside envelope");

  record.value = newValue;

  IOperations(ops).applyParameter(parameterId, newValue);

  }

  // ============================================================

  // 7. EMERGENCY FUNCTIONS (Emergency Council 4/7, instant; BP §14.6.5)

  // ============================================================

  /// @dev Pauses minting (Emergency Council).

  function pauseMinting() external onlyEmergencyCouncil {

  IOperations(ops).pauseMinting();

  emit MintingPaused(block.timestamp);

  }

  /// @dev Pauses redemptions (Emergency Council; extreme cases only).

  function pauseRedeeming() external onlyEmergencyCouncil {

  IOperations(ops).pauseRedeeming();

  emit RedeemingPaused(block.timestamp);

  }

  /// @dev Executes an immediate forced rebalance (Emergency Council).

  function forceRebalance() external onlyEmergencyCouncil {

  IOperations(ops).forceRebalance();

  emit RebalancingForced(block.timestamp);

  }

  /// @dev Resumes operations (Emergency Council; blocked while the
  state

  /// machine is in EMERGENCY, see Listing 13).

  function resumeOperations() external onlyEmergencyCouncil {

  IOperations(ops).resumeOperations();

  emit OperationsResumed(block.timestamp);

  }

  }

  interface IOperations {

  function applyParameter(bytes32 parameterId, uint256 value) external;

  function pauseMinting() external;

  function pauseRedeeming() external;

  function forceRebalance() external;

  function resumeOperations() external;

  }

  Fidelity note: Listing 14 adapts BP §14.6.4–§14.6.5. The v1.2 listing
  referenced a parameterRegistry mapping, a ParameterLayer enum,
  proposals and an updateParameter dispatcher without declaring them;
  they are declared here as part of the Section 22.4 registry design.
  Additions beyond the source: parameter registration with envelope
  bounds (constitutional), the Risk-Council parameter path with its
  24-hour timelock, and the pauseRedeeming and forceRebalance entry
  points from the §14.5 emergency-action table. The monetary path —
  propose, execute after the 48-hour timelock, Risk-Council veto within
  the window — is carried verbatim modulo the added envelope checks.
  Because the governance functions and the state machine are presented
  as separate contracts across Chapters 21 and 22, the emergency
  functions dispatch through the IOperations interface to Listing 13,
  which re-checks caller identity on arrival; emergencyLiquidate (the
  emergency eject) remains directly authorized on the operations
  contract by the Emergency Council, as in BP §11.5.2. All numeric
  values — 48 hours, 24 hours, 90 days, the quorums and the layer
  assignments — are unchanged from BP §14.4.1 and §14.8.

23. The Validation and Research Program (Production Precondition)

23.1 The Validation Methodology

  The validation program is not an appendix to the MTQ Σ design — it is
  a production precondition. No component of the adaptive architecture
  may govern a live monetary reference until the full program of this
  chapter has been executed, documented, independently reproduced and
  passed [MS §79].

The starting point of the methodology is a negative rule. Historical
performance figures produced under the old architecture — approximately
5.21% annualized volatility and a maximum drawdown of −4.1% — must not
be presented as evidence for MTQ Σ, because they were calculated under
the prior architecture: a fixed-quantity basket with a fixed gold
percentage and a single-AMM gold price. That engine shared none of the
live machinery of v2.0 — no constituency engine, no MASE multi-model
ensemble, no multi-source oracle consensus, no MARP execution gates — so
its statistics describe a different system answering a different
question:

Perf(old architecture) not⇒ Perf(MTQ Σ)

The program that replaces those figures has a fixed structure.
Historical evidence comes from the backtest of Section 23.2 and the
walk-forward validation of Section 23.3, both executed under the
leakage-control discipline of Section 23.4. Synthetic evidence comes
from the Monte Carlo testing of Section 23.5. Robustness evidence comes
from the parameter perturbation study of Section 23.6. Model and
methodology selection is governed by the criterion of Section 23.7, and
adversarial evidence comes from the stress suites of Sections 23.8
through 23.11, all measured through the reserve stress equation. Section
23.12 consolidates the gate sequence; the gate table itself is
maintained in Section 25.5.

A communication rule follows from the precondition status of the
program. Until the gates of Section 23.12 are passed, any performance
figure quoted for MTQ Σ must be labeled as a prior or an illustrative
projection, never as a measured property of the system. The discipline
is the same as the no-fixed-weighting principle of Section 3.1:
composition is an output of transparent mathematics, and evidence about
that composition is an output of transparent validation — neither may be
asserted in advance of the machinery that produces it.

23.2 The Historical Backtest

The backtest uses the longest reliable common data history available,
ideally beginning no later than 2010 and extending through the most
recent complete observation period [MS §80]:

T_(backtest) ⊆ [2010, T_(last)]

The 2010 start is chosen for two reasons. First, it places the sample in
the post-2008 monetary regime — floating reserve-currency volatility,
active central-bank gold accumulation and a continuously quoted offshore
CNY (CNH) market — so that every component of the initial universe has a
reliable common price history. Second, it forces the sample to contain
the episodes that matter for a stability mandate: the 2011 European
sovereign-debt crisis and gold peak, the 2013 taper tantrum, the
2014–2015 dollar bull market and oil collapse, the January 2015 Swiss
franc depeg, the 2020 COVID liquidity shock, and the 2022–2023 inflation
surge with its concurrent yen weakening. A stability engine validated
only on calm data has not been validated.

The backtest must be multi-horizon. Component volatilities are measured
on the 30/90/252/756-day blend of Section 5.1; drawdown,
purchasing-power error, turnover and coverage statistics are reported
per horizon as well as aggregate, because a system can look stable at
one horizon and unstable at another. All results carry the requirements
of the live system, not a paper simplification: the full pipeline —
constituency scoring, ensemble, constraint projection, smoothing and
execution-cost modeling (Chapters 10–11) — runs end-to-end, so the
reported statistics include the costs the live system would actually
pay.

  Data requirement: every series entering the backtest is snapshot,
  versioned and published with its DataVersion (Section 24.2). COFER
  observations arrive quarterly, the BIS Triennial Survey every three
  years with governed interpolation (Section 4.3), and price series come
  from the same validated multi-source construction used by the oracle
  layer (Chapter 17). A backtest that cannot state its data versions
  cannot be reproduced, and therefore does not count as evidence.

23.3 Walk-Forward Validation

Historical averages are not enough: the engine must be tested in the
mode in which it will live, re-estimating on the past and being scored
on the future it has never seen. Walk-forward validation implements
exactly that loop [MS §81]:

  Train_(t) → Optimize → OutOfSample_(t+1) then roll forward

For each period t, the system is calibrated — constituency scores,
covariance shrinkage, candidate models, ensemble coefficients, smoothing
state — using only data available up to t; the optimizer produces the
complete pipeline output; and the result is then evaluated on the
out-of-sample window t+1. The window then rolls forward and the
procedure repeats, concatenating the out-of-sample segments into a
single continuous simulated live record. No information from the future
test window may enter the training process [MS §81] — this is the hard
wall that Section 23.4 fortifies with purging and embargo.

Both window schemes are run. The expanding window (train on all history
up to t) measures how the system behaves as evidence accumulates; the
rolling window (train on the most recent K years only) measures how it
behaves when the regime is allowed to age out of the estimate. Cadence
is also varied — quarterly re-estimation on the strategic review cycle
(Section 10.10), and a faster cadence matching the tactical layer —
because re-estimation frequency is itself a validated parameter, not an
operational convenience. The concatenated out-of-sample record is the
only performance record eligible to feed the model-selection criterion
of Section 23.7 and the adaptive ensemble scores of Section 7.6.

23.4 Purged and Leakage-Controlled Validation

Walk-forward alone is not airtight when observations overlap. MTQ Σ
estimates 756-day volatilities, long-window correlations, regime labels
and ensemble performance scores — statistics whose estimation windows
can extend into or across the test period, quietly leaking future
information into training. The modification review elevated leakage
protection from good practice to a binding rule [MS §82, COO-24]:

  All model calibration, feature construction, optimizer parameter
  selection and ensemble weighting shall be performed without access to
  future test-period information. For overlapping data, leakage controls
  and purging shall be applied where required [COO-24].

This section is pinned because it is referenced from Section 7.6: the
adaptive ensemble's Score_m uses out-of-sample statistics only, and
those statistics are credible only if the out-of-sample label is honest.
The mechanism has two parts. Purging removes from the training set any
observation whose label window overlaps the test window — a 756-day
volatility estimate whose window reaches into the test period is
dropped, not truncated:

D_(train)^((purged)) = { τ : [τ, τ+h_(label)) ∩ [t, t+h_(test)) = ∅ }

Embargo then deletes a further block of training observations
immediately following the test window, because serial correlation lets
information cross the boundary even without literal overlap:

Embargo: τ ∈ (t+h_(test), t+h_(test)+h_(embargo)) excluded from training

The embargo length h_{embargo} is set at least as long as the longest
autocorrelation in the return and signal series — by default the 30-day
horizon — and is itself a published methodology parameter. Four
estimation targets receive the treatment explicitly: volatility,
correlations, regimes and model ensemble weights [MS §82], because these
are exactly the quantities whose long windows make them the natural
leakage channels.

  Why this matters enormously: MTQ Σ is intended to govern a monetary
  reference methodology [COO-24]. A leaked validation record does not
  merely flatter a trading strategy — it silently changes what is
  claimed about a unit of account. Every out-of-sample number in this
  document's evidence base inherits its credibility from this section,
  and any methodology version that cannot demonstrate purge and embargo
  discipline in its validation logs is inadmissible at the gates of
  Section 23.12.

23.5 Monte Carlo Testing

Historical data contains one realized path; a monetary reference must
survive the paths that did not happen. Monte Carlo testing therefore
generates N simulated paths under multiple distributions and regimes,
and tests the stability of the live weight trajectory W_t under path
variations [MS §83]:

  { W_(t)^((k)) }_(k=1)^(N) generated under multiple distributions and
  regimes

The scenario families are constructed so that each attacks a different
assumption of the engine, and each family's generator is versioned with
the methodology so results are reproducible (Section 24.1):

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Family                  Generator               Failure assumption
                                                  attacked
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Historical block        Resampled blocks of     That the future
  bootstrap               joint currency/gold     resembles reordered
                          returns, preserving     history.
                          short-horizon           
                          dependence              

  Parametric Gaussian     Multivariate normal     Behavior under
                          with the shrunk         idealized, thin-tailed
                          covariance of Section   dynamics.
                          6.2                     

  Fat-tailed              Multivariate Student-t  That tails are
                          with heavy degrees of   Gaussian-sized.
                          freedom                 

  Regime-switching        Markov switching among  That regimes arrive
                          the six regimes of      gently and rarely.
                          Section 5.4 with stress 
                          transitions             

  Jump / crisis           Jump-diffusion paths    That moves are
                          with synchronized FX    continuous.
                          and gold jumps          

  Correlation collapse    Paths with ρ_{ij} arrow That diversification
                          1                       persists when needed
                                                  (Section 23.9).

  Purchasing-power drift  Paths with persistent   That purchasing-power
                          divergence between      tracking is easy.
                          currency returns and    
                          the GPP index           
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

For each family the program reports the dispersion of the resulting
weight trajectories, the distribution of turnover and execution cost,
the drawdown distribution against the constitutional MDD envelope, and
the reserve coverage distribution (Section 23.11). The stability
requirement is not that W_t be constant — the whole architecture exists
to let weights move — but that the distribution of outcomes remain
inside the envelopes, the velocity limits and the cost budget across the
path population. A model family whose behavior bifurcates under modest
path variation is treated as non-robust by the selection criterion of
Section 23.7 regardless of its mean performance.

23.6 Parameter Perturbation and Robustness Testing

A methodology with dozens of calibrated parameters can always be tuned
to look good once. The defense is perturbation: for every important
parameter θ, the validation program runs the system at θ+δ and at θ−δ,
and measures how much performance moves [MS §84, COO-25]. This section
is pinned because it is the referenced discipline of Sections 4.5
(score-construction weights) and 6.2 (shrinkage intensity):

  Δ Performance(θ,δ) = | Perf(θ+δ) - Perf(θ-δ) |

Perf(θ ± δ) ≈ Perf(θ) rather than collapsing under tiny perturbations
[MS §84]

A robust model should not collapse under modest parameter changes
[COO-25]. The perturbation set δ is scaled to each parameter's natural
variation — a few percent of the parameter's range for bounded
parameters, one estimation standard error for statistically calibrated
ones — and every run re-executes the full pipeline of Section 23.3, not
the optimizer in isolation, because a parameter that is harmless in the
optimizer can be destructive after smoothing and execution. The
parameters subject to the study are exactly the contents of the
parameter registry (Section 22.4):

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Parameter family        Examples                Registry reference
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Objective coefficients  λ_1 … λ_8 of the        Section 7.4
                          composite objective     

  Candidate-model         Shrinkage λ, CVaR       Sections 6.2–6.6
  parameters              confidence level, ERC   
                          tolerance               

  Signal construction     Horizon weights ω_k,    Sections 5.1–5.3
                          z-score windows, ε      
                          guards                  

  Ensemble mechanics      Temperature η, score    Sections 7.5–7.6
                          orientation, member set 

  Constituency            a_k scoring weights,    Sections 4.2, 4.5, 4.7
  coefficients            Q_{min}, hysteresis     
                          band                    

  Stability constraints   Envelopes, velocity     Chapter 8
                          limits, smoothing ρ     

  Execution parameters    No-trade thresholds,    Chapters 10–11, Section
                          urgency scaling, LCR    13.4
                          buffers                 
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Each parameter receives a robustness bound B_θ set by the Risk Council,
and the perturbation result must satisfy ΔPerformance ≤ B_θ. Parameters
that fail are not silently retuned: the parameter is re-derived, the
input is re-transformed, or the model is rejected (Section 4.5 records
this loop for the constituency engine; Section 6.2 records it for the
shrinkage estimator). Results enter the Robustness term of the selection
criterion (Section 23.7), so perturbation performance is not a side
report — it is a scored input to which model survives.

23.7 The Model-Selection Criterion

When several candidate models, parameterizations or methodology versions
compete, the winner is not the highest return [MS §85]. Return is not
the objective of a monetary reference; stability is. This section is
pinned because Section 6.7 references it as the reason drawdown behavior
is scored explicitly. The decision function is:

  Score = a(-Volatility) + b(-CVaR) + c(-MDD) + d(-PPError) +
  e(-Turnover) + f(-Cost) + g(Diversification) + h(Robustness)

with coefficients a through h determined through governance and research
[MS §85] — set by the Risk Council within constitutional envelopes,
published with the methodology version, and themselves subject to the
perturbation study of Section 23.6 so that the ranking of models is not
an artifact of the ranking weights. Every term is oriented so that
higher is more stable: volatility, CVaR, maximum drawdown,
purchasing-power error, turnover and cost enter negatively;
diversification and robustness enter positively.

Two terms deserve emphasis. The drawdown coefficient c is explicitly
non-zero because purchasing-power protection is experienced as drawdown,
not as variance — a unit whose value never oscillates but occasionally
drops 15% has failed its users even if its volatility statistics look
excellent (Section 6.7). The robustness coefficient h aggregates the
perturbation sensitivities of Section 23.6 and the Monte Carlo
dispersion of Section 23.5, so a model that scores well only on the
single realized history — the signature of overfitting — is penalized
exactly where it is weakest.

The criterion operates at three levels: choosing among candidate models
inside the ensemble (feeding the adaptive weights of Section 7.6, whose
Score_m is consistent with this function); choosing the ensemble
architecture itself; and promoting a new methodology version through the
gates of Section 23.12. At every level the comparison uses the purged,
walk-forward, out-of-sample record — never in-sample statistics, and
never a single favorable window.

23.8 Gold-Specific Stress Tests

Gold is the largest single component envelope in the basket (Section
8.1) and the component whose market structure is most layered — price,
liquidity, tokenized issuers, physical custody and oracles can each fail
independently. The modification review found the quantitative test suite
for this stack missing and made it mandatory [COO-21, MS §86]. The
complete suite is:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Scenario                Shock definition        Primary layers under
                                                  test
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Gold +50%               Sudden and gradual +50% Upper envelope,
                          gold price moves        velocity, index drift,
                                                  purchasing-power
                                                  tracking

  Gold +25%               Strong gold rally,      Reconstitution cost,
                          single- and             turnover budget, MARP
                          multi-quarter paths     gates

  Gold −10%               Mild gold decline       De-weighting response,
                                                  drawdown accounting

  Gold −20%               Severe gold decline     MDD envelope, CVaR
                                                  objective, crisis score
                                                  (Section 5.5)

  Gold −30%               Extreme gold decline    Constitutional MDD
                                                  bound, reserve
                                                  coverage, recovery
                                                  mechanism

  Sudden liquidity        Gold-market depth       Execution layer, LCR
  reduction               collapse, spread        (Section 13.4),
                          blowout                 no-trade thresholds

  Tokenized-gold issuer   A gold token issuer     Reserve substitution,
  failure                 becomes insolvent or    registry rules, oracle
                          fraudulent              independence

  Physical custody        Custodian halts         Reference continuity,
  interruption            redemption or           reserve reallocation,
                          attestation             legal segregation

  Oracle disagreement     Canonical gold sources  Oracle consensus,
                          diverge beyond          deviation handling,
                          tolerance               fallback chain (Chapter
                                                  17)
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The binding requirement is total: MTQ Σ must remain operational under
all defined cases [MS §86]. Operational means the index keeps publishing
from validated inputs, the weight pipeline keeps running inside its
constitutional constraints, the reserve stress equation of Section 23.11
is evaluated and reported, and no discretionary monetary manipulation is
required to keep the system coherent. Each scenario is now specified in
full: what is tested, the pass criteria, and the consequences of
failure.

23.8.1 Gold Price Shocks (+50%, +25%, −10%, −20%, −30%)

What is tested: the pressure each price path puts on the full weight
pipeline. A +50% or +25% shock tests whether the gold weight is pushed
against its 32% upper envelope, whether the velocity limits and the
projection of Section 8.1 keep the reconstitution gradual, how much
turnover and execution cost the correction consumes, and whether
purchasing-power tracking improves (gold rising usually means fiat
purchasing power falling) rather than degrades. The −10%, −20% and −30%
shocks test the drawdown machinery: the CVaR objective, the crisis-score
scaling of λ_{CVaR,t}, the stress-adaptive smoothing (ρ rising to 0.75),
and the constitutional MDD envelope of Section 6.7. Both instantaneous
and multi-quarter gradual variants are run, because the engine's correct
response differs by path shape.

Pass criteria: weights remain inside the envelopes at every step (or
leave them only through the governed projection path, never by breach);
drawdown stays within the constitutional envelope even in the −30% case
at maximum gold weight; turnover stays within the validated budget;
coverage under Section 23.11 remains at or above one; and index
publication is uninterrupted. Consequences of failure: the gold
envelope, velocity limits, smoothing parameters or crisis-mode
thresholds are revised through governance as a new methodology version —
a failed stress test changes the architecture, never the reporting. A
failure that cannot be repaired by parameter revision escalates to the
recovery-mechanism design of Section 23.11.

23.8.2 Sudden Gold-Market Liquidity Reduction

What is tested: a sharp collapse in gold-market depth — bid-ask spreads
widening severalfold, quoted size shrinking, settlement windows
stretching — with the price path left deliberately ambiguous, because
the hazard here is execution, not valuation. The scenario exercises the
no-trade threshold widening and execution-size reduction of crisis mode
(Section 13.7), the liquidity coverage requirement of Section 13.4, and
the MARP logic of Chapters 10–11 that must defer corrections into thin
markets rather than pay crisis spreads to hit a target weight.

Pass criteria: no forced execution into the thin market — MARP
demonstrably defers, and the deferral itself is logged with its
cost-benefit justification (Section 24.4); the reserve preserves its
liquidity buffers; the index continues to publish from validated oracle
consensus; and when liquidity normalizes, the deferred corrections
execute within the velocity limits. Consequences of failure: liquidity
buffers, thresholds or instrument eligibility are re-specified — if the
reserve holds an instrument whose market cannot absorb an emergency
reallocation, that instrument fails the eligibility framework of Chapter
15 and is replaced.

23.8.3 Tokenized-Gold Issuer Failure

What is tested: the decoupling that Section 3.4 built on purpose.
Reference-basket gold exposure is to the gold price reference unit
(Section 17.4), not to any token; a tokenized-gold issuer (an
insolvency, a frozen redemption window, a proof-of-reserves failure) is
a reserve-implementation event, not a reference event. The scenario
verifies that the canonical gold price continues from the remaining
independent sources (Chapter 17), that the failed issuer's price feed is
suspended from consensus without capturing it, and that the reserve
substitutes instruments under the Chapter 15 registry and Chapter 14
reserve rules, with the failed position marked at recovery value.

Pass criteria: the index and weights are unaffected in their computation
path (weights respond to the gold price and eligibility, not to issuer
identity); the reserve stress equation of Section 23.11 is re-evaluated
with the failed instrument at stressed recovery value and either
coverage remains at or above one, or the governed recovery mechanism of
Section 23.11 activates explicitly. Consequences of failure: the
instrument is delisted, the reserve is restructured toward more
resilient instruments or physical bullion, and if coverage cannot be
restored without discretionary action, the event enters the governed
recovery framework — the one place the architecture permits a shortfall
to be handled by rule rather than by improvisation.

23.8.4 Physical-Gold Custody Interruption

What is tested: the interruption class of the physical leg — a custodian
halts redemptions, an audit or attestation lapses, or a jurisdictional
event freezes access. The reference side is again expected to be immune:
canonical pricing runs on the multi-source oracle layer. The reserve
side is not immune: physical gold that cannot be delivered, audited or
reallocated is temporarily impaired even if title is legally clean, so
the scenario measures how long the reserve can operate with that leg
frozen, and whether the legal segregation specified in the custody
architecture holds the asset out of the custodian's estate.

Pass criteria: the reference methodology operates without interruption;
reserve coverage is evaluated with the physical leg marked at a stressed
liquidity discount, with the same coverage-or-governed-recovery rule as
Section 23.8.3; and the reserve's diversification across instruments
(tokenized, allocated physical, multi-custodian) keeps the interruption
from becoming a coverage failure. Consequences of failure: custody
arrangements are re-specified — additional custodians, stronger
segregation terms, or a larger tokenized allocation — as a governed
reserve-policy version, with the interruption scenario re-run before the
change is accepted.

23.8.5 Oracle Disagreement

What is tested: the canonical price mechanism itself. In this scenario
the independent gold sources diverge beyond their tolerance band — a
split market, a stale feed, or a manipulated source. The scenario
exercises the deviation detection, source weighting, staleness handling
and fallback chain of Chapter 17, plus the execution-layer rule that
pausing rebalancing is preferable to transacting on a contested price.
The 2015 franc depeg is the historical archetype: sources that usually
agree can violently disagree exactly when accuracy matters most.

Pass criteria: the published gold price remains a consensus of validated
sources with the disagreeing source identified, down-weighted or
suspended according to the oracle rules; the staleness rules of Section
5.3 (last known value, WATCH flag, rebalancing pause beyond five
minutes) hold; no single source can capture the canonical price; and
every step of the resolution is recorded with the OracleVersion of
Section 24.2. Consequences of failure: the source set, tolerances or
aggregation method are revised through oracle governance; a source that
demonstrably captured the price is removed from the eligible set, and
the oracle version increments so all downstream calculations remain
attributable to a definite configuration.

23.9 Currency-Specific Stress Tests

Each basket currency is shocked in isolation at −20%, and in
combinations, and the correlation structure is pushed toward the
degenerate limit [MS §87, COO-22]. This section is pinned because
Section 6.5 references it as the explicit test of the diversification
failure mode:

  ρ_(ij) → 1 for all pairs i ≠ j (collapse of diversification [COO-22])

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Scenario                Composition             What the result must
                                                  show
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  USD −20%                USD alone, others       Bounded index impact
                          stable                  (envelope-capped),
                                                  orderly de-weighting,
                                                  no panic turnover

  EUR −20%                EUR alone               Same bounds; EURC
                                                  reserve instruments
                                                  marked under Section
                                                  23.10

  JPY −20%                JPY alone               Same bounds;
                                                  registry-resolved
                                                  implementation
                                                  unaffected in the
                                                  reference

  GBP −20%                GBP alone               Same bounds; hysteresis
                                                  must not oscillate on
                                                  the stressed score

  CNY −20%                CNY alone               Same bounds;
                                                  CNH/renminbi liquidity
                                                  stress handled by
                                                  execution gates

  CHF −20%                CHF alone               Same bounds;
                                                  depeg-class event with
                                                  abrupt gap risk

  Combinations            Pairs, the              Envelope and MDD
                          dollar-crisis pattern   discipline under
                          (USD −20% with gold     correlated failure
                          +25%), all-currency     
                          stress vs gold          

  Correlation collapse    ρ_{ij} arrow 1 on every Graceful degradation of
                          pair                    the diversification
                                                  objective, not a corner
                                                  solution
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The isolated shocks establish the arithmetic bound the architecture must
honor: with component weights capped by the envelopes of Section 8.1
(USD at most 32%, every other currency lower), a −20% single-currency
shock produces an index impact strictly smaller than the cap times the
shock — the worst case is bounded before any adaptation responds. The
pass criteria are that this bound holds in simulation, that the
de-weighting of the shocked currency respects velocity limits and costs,
and that the coverage inequality of Section 23.11 survives the
reserve-side mark-downs. Combinations test the correlated cases, in
particular the historical dollar-crisis pattern in which the dollar
falls while gold rallies, which stresses the two largest envelopes in
opposite directions simultaneously.

The correlation-collapse scenario deserves its own statement, because it
is the falsifier of the whole diversification thesis: diversification
can disappear during crises [MS §87]. When every ρ_{ij} tends to one,
the maximum-diversification candidate of Section 6.5 does not break —
its objective degenerates toward equal weighting, which remains
admissible — and the shrunk covariance estimator of Section 6.2 is
already biased toward exactly such a structured target. The pass
criterion is graceful: the basket may lose its risk-reduction premium,
but it must not concentrate, must not breach the MDD envelope, and the
ensemble must not let one model's collapse propagate into the live
weights. The scenario is run jointly with the Monte Carlo
correlation-collapse family of Section 23.5 so that the evidence is
distributional, not anecdotal.

23.10 Stable-Value Asset Stress

Reserve implementation involves tokenized fiat and other stable-value
instruments — USDC and EURC at genesis, with successors admitted through
the registry — and these instruments carry a failure taxonomy of their
own. Any reserve implementation involving such assets must test the full
set [MS §88]:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Scenario                Meaning                 Primary defense under
                                                  test
  ----------------------- ----------------------- -----------------------
  Depeg                   Market price deviates   Marking discipline,
                          persistently from par   coverage evaluation at
                                                  market not par

  Redemption halt         Issuer suspends         Liquidity buffers,
                          conversion to fiat      instrument substitution

  Issuer failure          Insolvency or fraud of  Segregation,
                          the issuing entity      diversification across
                                                  issuers, recovery
                                                  values

  Custody failure         Failure of the          Legal segregation,
                          custodial arrangement   multi-custodian
                          holding the instrument  structure

  Liquidity collapse      Secondary-market depth  LCR (Section 13.4),
                          disappears              MARP deferral

  Regulatory restriction  The instrument becomes  Instrument eligibility
                          restricted or           (Chapter 15),
                          sanctioned              jurisdiction
                                                  diversification
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The governing sentence of this suite is verbatim from the specification,
and it is a constitutional constraint on reserve design, not a testing
preference: reserve policy must survive an individual instrument failure
without requiring discretionary monetary manipulation [MS §88].
Surviving means the reference methodology is untouched (currency
exposure is defined by the currency, Section 3.4, not by the token), the
reserve restructures under pre-existing rules, and the coverage
inequality of the next section is either maintained or handed to the
explicitly governed recovery mechanism.

Two design consequences are tested rather than assumed. First,
diversification across issuers and custodial arrangements must be real:
the scenario suite runs each failure with the affected instrument at
several plausible weights, so that the maximum exposure to any single
stable-value instrument is a validated bound, not a hope. Second,
marking discipline must hold under stress — a depegged instrument is
marked at its market and recovery value in every coverage calculation,
never at par, because a coverage ratio computed at par is not a coverage
ratio.

23.11 The Reserve Stress Equation

Every scenario in this chapter — every gold shock, currency shock,
stable-value failure, liquidity event and custody interruption — is
ultimately scored through one equation, supplied by the modification
review and binding on the whole architecture [MS §89, COO-23]. Under
scenario s:

  CoverageRatio_(s) = (ReserveNAV_(s))/(Obligations_(s)) for scenario s

CoverageRatio_(s) ≥ 1 for all defined acceptable stress cases

ReserveNAV_s is the net asset value of the reserve portfolio marked
under scenario s — applying the scenario's haircuts, depegs, liquidity
discounts and recovery values to each instrument, per the marking
discipline of Section 23.10. Obligations_s is the value of outstanding
MTQ Σ claims evaluated under the same scenario. The equation is tested
against every defined stress scenario in the suite [COO-23]: the gold
cases of Section 23.8, the currency cases of Section 23.9, and the
stable-value cases of Section 23.10, individually and in the
combinations that compound marks against each other.

The inequality is required for all defined acceptable stress cases;
where unavoidable tail events exceed the base reserve model, the system
must maintain an explicitly governed recovery mechanism rather than an
implicit one [MS §89]. That mechanism — funded, rule-based, and
activated by defined coverage thresholds rather than discretion — is
specified with the reserve architecture in Chapter 14. The equation is a
reserve property, not a weight property: it never justifies tilting the
reference basket to repair coverage, because the basket is a methodology
(Section 3.1) and the reserve is an implementation; conflating the two
is precisely the failure the three-layer separation exists to prevent
(Section 2.2).

  Reporting rule: every stress-test report in the validation program
  states CoverageRatio_s per scenario, the marking assumptions used, and
  — where the ratio falls below one — which governed recovery path
  activates. A stress report without its coverage line is incomplete and
  does not count toward the gates of Section 23.12.

23.12 Validation Gates and Production Preconditions

The program of this chapter consolidates into a gate sequence: an
ordered set of conditions, each of which must be satisfied and evidenced
before the next is meaningful, and all of which must be satisfied before
the adaptive architecture may govern live value at production scale. The
gate table itself — gate identifiers, owners, evidence artifacts and
sign-off records — is maintained in Section 25.5; this section states
the preconditions that the table enforces:

  --------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Gate                    Precondition               Evidence source
  ----------------------- -------------------------- -----------------------
  G1 — Historical         Backtest from 2010         Section 23.2
  evidence                complete, multi-horizon,   
                          full pipeline with costs   

  G2 — Out-of-sample      Walk-forward record        Sections 23.3–23.4
  integrity               complete; purging and      
                          embargo applied; zero      
                          leakage findings           

  G3 — Synthetic          Monte Carlo families pass; Section 23.5
  robustness              weight/turnover/drawdown   
                          distributions in bounds    

  G4 — Parameter          Perturbation study         Section 23.6
  robustness              complete; ΔPerformance ≤   
                          B_θ for every registry     
                          parameter                  

  G5 — Model selection    Selection criterion        Section 23.7
                          applied on OOS records     
                          only; coefficients         
                          published                  

  G6 — Stress suite pass  All gold, currency,        Sections 23.8–23.11
                          correlation and            
                          stable-value scenarios     
                          pass; CoverageRatio_s ≥ 1  
                          or governed recovery       
                          active                     

  G7 — Reproducibility    Independent party re-runs  Sections 24.1–24.2
  audit                   MASE from published        
                          versions and obtains       
                          identical weights          
  --------------------------------------------------------------------------

The status rule is strict. Testnet operation, simulation environments
and publication of methodology drafts may proceed before the gates pass
— that is the purpose of a testnet — but production deployment of the
adaptive weights may not, and any performance figure quoted before the
gates close carries the illustrative-prior label of Section 23.1.
Failure at a gate does not end the program; it routes back to the
specific architecture layer implicated (a G4 failure to the parameter
registry of Section 22.4, a G6 failure to the envelopes of Chapter 8 or
the reserve policy of Chapter 14) and the affected version re-enters
validation. The locked/not-locked status of every constant, parameter
and threshold touched by this program is tracked in Chapter 26.6, so
that at any moment the document can state which numbers are settled
evidence and which remain open research.

24. Transparency, Reproducibility and Publication

24.1 Deterministic Reproducibility

The transparency architecture rests on a single computational fact:
given identical inputs, the system must produce identical outputs. Given
identical data Data_t and parameters Parameters_t — and identical
methodology, model, parameter and oracle versions — the system must
produce W_t^{Live} identically [MS §92]:

  (Data_(t), Parameters_(t), Versions_(t)) identical Longrightarrow
  W_(t)^(Live) identical

This section is pinned because the MASE registry of Listing 2 depends on
it: the contract intentionally does not re-solve the optimization
on-chain, and that omission is safe only because anyone can re-run MASE
off-chain from the published versions and obtain the identical target,
which the contract then verifies against the constitution. Deterministic
reproducibility is what allows independent auditors to reproduce the
result [MS §92] — the auditor is not asked to trust the submitter, only
to execute the same deterministic function on the same versioned inputs.

Determinism is a system requirement, not a default. It imposes concrete
engineering rules, each of which is part of the methodology version:
random number generators are seeded with published constants (the Monte
Carlo families of Section 23.5 are thereby reproducible path-for-path);
floating-point operations are specified in a fixed order and precision;
no computation may depend on wall-clock time, network latency or
environment state; software versions are recorded; and input data is
snapshotted immutably under its DataVersion so that a later revision of
a source series cannot silently change what a past calculation consumed.
A re-run that diverges in even one weight at one timestamp is a defect
that blocks the G7 gate of Section 23.12.

24.2 Data, Model, Parameter and Oracle Versioning

Reproducibility requires that every calculation be attributable to
exactly the inputs, models, parameters and oracle configuration that
produced it. Every live calculation stores the full version tuple and
result record [MS §93, COO-26] — this section is pinned, referenced from
Sections 4.3 and 6.1, and is the backbone of institutional auditability
[COO-26]:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Field                               Meaning
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  DataVersion                         Immutable hash of the exact input
                                      snapshot consumed: price series,
                                      COFER and BIS observations, CPI and
                                      GPP inputs, oracle histories
                                      (Sections 4.3, 6.1).

  ModelVersion                        Identity of the candidate-model set
                                      and ensemble architecture: which
                                      models participate, in which
                                      mathematical form, with which
                                      covariance and objective structure
                                      (Chapters 6–7).

  ParameterVersion                    Identity of the parameter vector
                                      from the registry (Section 22.4):
                                      objective coefficients λ,
                                      thresholds, horizon weights,
                                      ensemble temperature, envelopes.

  OracleVersion                       Identity of the canonical oracle
                                      configuration: source set,
                                      aggregation method, tolerances,
                                      staleness rules (Chapter 17).

  Weight                              The computed weight vector itself —
                                      the numerical output posted to and
                                      enforced by the registry (Listing
                                      2).

  Output                              The full result record accompanying
                                      the weight: component scores,
                                      constraint-status flags, and the
                                      intermediate states of the pipeline
                                      of Section 24.5.

  DecisionID                          Unique identifier linking this
                                      calculation to the rebalancing
                                      decision log of Section 24.4,
                                      closing the loop between
                                      computation and execution.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

Together these fields create a complete audit trail [MS §93] — for a
single calculation, and across time, because version histories let an
auditor reconstruct the exact state of the entire system at any past
date. The registry events of Listing 2 carry the methodology and data
versions on every accepted update; the parameter registry of Section
22.4 carries parameter provenance and change history; the oracle layer
carries its configuration version; and the locked/not-locked ledger of
Chapter 26.6 records which of these values are constitutionally settled
and which remain validation-stage. The auditability is institutional in
the COO's sense: a regulator, an exchange, a reserve auditor or any
independent party can verify not only what the system did, but exactly
which versioned machinery did it.

  Version discipline: a version identifier changes only through the
  governed process for its layer — data revisions are new DataVersions
  (never silent overwrites, Section 4.3), parameter changes are new
  ParameterVersions through the registry, and oracle changes are new
  OracleVersions through oracle governance. Any calculation whose stored
  versions do not correspond to a published, governed version is invalid
  by construction.

24.3 Weight Publication

Reproducibility makes results verifiable in principle; publication makes
them verifiable in practice. This section is pinned, referenced from
Section 4.2, and it is also where the no-fixed-weighting principle of
Section 3.1 becomes operational: only the published W_t series describes
the composition of MTQ Σ, so the publication is, in the strict sense,
the definition of the basket's realized state. For each effective
period, the system publishes W_t together with [MS §94]:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Published artifact                  Content
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  Source data                         The versioned input snapshot
                                      (DataVersion) — prices,
                                      institutional data, oracle
                                      histories — sufficient to re-run
                                      the entire pipeline (Section 24.1).

  Methodology version                 The full version tuple of Section
                                      24.2 under which the weights were
                                      computed.

  Scoring tables                      Component-level scores:
                                      constituency eligibility scores Q_i
                                      (Section 4.2), candidate-model
                                      scores, ensemble coefficients α_m
                                      (Section 7.6).

  Eligibility decisions               The universe actually used:
                                      admitted, retired, monitored
                                      candidates, with thresholds and
                                      hysteresis state (Sections 4.2,
                                      4.7).

  Constraint status                   Envelope checks, velocity
                                      compliance, sum-to-one, smoothing
                                      state — pass/fail per constraint,
                                      per component.

  Deviation from prior period         W_t against W_{t-1} with the
                                      attributed causes (signal changes,
                                      regime shifts, score movements).

  Rebalancing decision                The MARP outcome for the period —
                                      executed corrections with
                                      DecisionIDs, or the logged no-trade
                                      decision with its justification
                                      (Section 24.4).

  Transaction-cost estimate           The modeled and realized cost of
                                      any weight movement, spread and
                                      slippage (Chapters 10–11).
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

The publication set is deliberately larger than the weight vector:
scoring tables and eligibility decisions make the constituency engine as
auditable as the weighting engine (Section 4.5), and the deviation
attribution connects composition change to its causes rather than
presenting it as unexplained drift. Because both the weight series and
the underlying price series are public, any auditor can perform the
decomposition of Chapter 9 — separating index movement into price effect
and composition effect — and verify that composition effects occurred
only through the logged, justified, velocity-bounded path. Publication
is what converts the deterministic function of Section 24.1 from an
internal property into a public check on a monetary reference.

24.4 The Rebalancing Decision Log

Weights describe state; the decision log describes action. For each
executed trade, the system assigns a DecisionID, and that record must
contain the complete economics of the decision [MS §95]:

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  Field                               Meaning
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  DecisionID                          Unique identifier of the trade
                                      decision; the join key to the
                                      version records of Section 24.2.

  TargetWeight                        The weight vector the pipeline
                                      called for (the smoothed target the
                                      trade serves).

  ActualWeight                        The live weight vector measured
                                      before the trade — the state the
                                      correction starts from.

  Drift                               The deviation between target and
                                      actual, per component, that
                                      triggered evaluation.

  RiskScore                           The risk state at decision time —
                                      crisis score, regime, liquidity
                                      conditions (Sections 5.4–5.5).

  ExpectedBenefit                     The modeled benefit of correcting
                                      the drift (tracking error
                                      reduction, risk reduction).

  ExpectedCost                        The modeled cost — spreads,
                                      slippage, market impact — of
                                      executing the correction.

  ExecutionAmount                     The size actually traded, per
                                      component, after threshold and
                                      sizing rules.

  ExecutionPrice                      The realized prices achieved,
                                      against which the cost model is
                                      later scored.

  PostTradeWeight                     The weight vector after execution —
                                      the new actual state, and the next
                                      period's starting point.
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

This makes the system auditable [MS §95]. The log captures the full
decision logic: the inputs (target, actual, drift, risk score), the
thresholds that determined whether action was warranted, the urgency
classification that determined timing, the cost-benefit outcome
(ExpectedBenefit against ExpectedCost — the MARP test of Section 10.8),
and the execution facts (amount, price, post-trade state). The same
record structure is written for no-trade decisions, with the threshold
comparison and justification in place of execution fields, so that
inaction is as explainable as action — silence in the log is never
ambiguous.

The log feeds the audit chain in both directions. Upstream, each entry
carries the version tuple of Section 24.2, so every trade is
attributable to the exact machinery that motivated it; downstream, the
aggregate record becomes attribution evidence for the performance
reporting of Chapter 9 and a validation input — realized versus expected
costs calibrate the execution-cost models of Chapters 10–11, and
realized slippage under stress feeds back into the stress scenarios of
Sections 23.8–23.9. Entries are append-only; the decision log is a
permanent, hash-chained record whose integrity is checkable against the
published weight series.

24.5 The Final Live-Weight Equation

The complete conceptual pipeline — the single equation that summarizes
the entire architecture from data to executed weights — is [MS §96]:

  W_(t)^(Live) = Execute[
  Smooth(Constrain(Ensemble(Constituency(Data_(t)))))]

Read from the inside out, the equation names the four layers in
execution order: the constituency layer decides who is in the universe,
the ensemble layer decides how much each component should weigh, the
constraint layer makes the result constitutionally admissible, the
smoothing layer makes it temporally stable, and the execution layer
decides what actually trades. The equation is expanded in four explicit
steps, and each step is implemented by the chapters this document has
already built.

Step 1 — the target. From the versioned data, through the eligible
universe and the multi-model ensemble, MASE produces the target weights:

W_(t)^(Target) = MASE(Data_(t), W^(Prior), Risk_(t), CVaR_(t), PP_(t),
Liquidity_(t), Regime_(t), Cost_(t))

This is the work of Chapters 4 through 7: the constituency engine
filters the universe (Chapter 4), the input signals and regime model
condition the state (Chapter 5), the candidate models propose solutions
(Chapter 6), and the composite objective with the adaptive ensemble
synthesizes them (Chapter 7), with W^{Prior} entering only as the soft
anchor of Section 7.1. The target is an unconstrained-in-spirit optimum
— it expresses what stability mathematics wants, before the constitution
speaks.

Step 2 — the constraint. The target is projected onto the admissible
set:

W_(t)^(Constrained) = Projection_(Ct)(W_(t)^(Target))

This is Chapter 8: the constitutional envelopes, sum-to-one and
non-negativity, the weight-velocity limits, and the eligibility bounds
define the set \mathcal{C}_t, and the projection is applied after the
ensemble average (Section 7.5) because an average of admissible
solutions is not automatically admissible itself. The projection step is
where the constitution has the final word over the mathematics — no
optimizer output reaches the smoothing layer without passing it.

Step 3 — the smoothing. The constrained target is blended with the
previous live state:

W_(t)^(Smooth) = ρ_(t) W_(t-1) + (1-ρ_(t)) W_(t)^(Constrained)

This is the stress-adaptive smoothing rule of Chapter 8 (Section 8.4):
ρ_t = 0.50 in normal conditions and rises to 0.75 when the crisis score
is elevated, so that the basket moves deliberately in calm markets and
even more deliberately in turbulent ones. The smoothing rule, the
velocity limits and the rebalancing direction lock (Section 11.5)
together implement the stability-in-time requirement — and the
distinction between W_t^{Target} and W_t^{Smooth} is one of the four
weight states of Section 2.3, which are never equivalent.

Step 4 — the execution. The smoothed vector is given to the execution
engine against the actual state:

W_(t)^(Execution) = MARP(W_(t)^(Smooth), W_(t)^(Actual), Cost_(t),
Liquidity_(t), Reserve_(t))

This is Chapters 10 and 11: MARP compares the smoothed target with the
actual weights, applies the no-trade thresholds and the cost-benefit
test, scales and times execution against liquidity and cost, and
respects the reserve state — producing what actually trades, logged
field-by-field under its DecisionID (Section 24.4). W_t^{Execution}
becomes the actual weight vector of the next period, closing the loop;
and because every stage consumed versioned inputs under the discipline
of Sections 24.1–24.2, the entire composed map — from Data_t to executed
weights — is deterministic, published, and reproducible by anyone.

  The final equation is the audit trail in miniature: Data_t (Section
  24.2 versioning), through Constituency and Ensemble (Chapter 4;
  Chapters 5–7), through Constrain and Smooth (Chapter 8), to Execute
  (Chapters 10–11) — with every state transition published (Section
  24.3) and every executed movement logged (Section 24.4). Validation
  (Chapter 23) establishes that the pipeline deserves to run;
  transparency (this chapter) establishes that its running can always be
  checked.

25. Claims and Honest Status

25.1 Purpose — The Honesty Architecture

  This chapter is the honest status declaration of the MTQ Σ protocol.
  It explicitly states what the protocol can and cannot claim, based on
  the current state of the specification, the implementation and the
  institutional validation — and it makes honesty an enforced
  architectural property rather than a marketing preference [BP §15.1].

The declarations in this chapter are binding and non-negotiable. Any
external communication that contradicts them is a misrepresentation of
the protocol's true state. The core principle is carried verbatim from
the blueprint:

  Never claim what you cannot prove. Never promise what you cannot
  deliver. Never market what you have not tested.

The honesty architecture has four enforcement points. First, the
document itself: constitutional invariant I10 (Section 2.6) makes honest
status publication a hard rule, and the status declaration of Section
1.7 binds every reader of this specification. Second, the validation
program: Chapter 23 defines the research discipline — rebuilt backtests,
walk-forward evaluation, leakage control, parameter perturbation and
robustness scoring — that any performance, optimality or robustness
claim must survive before it can be made. Third, the transparency layer:
Chapter 24 publishes weights, data versions and rebalancing decisions,
so a false claim is always contradicted by the public record. Fourth,
the smart contracts: Section 25.7 places the status declaration and the
validation-gate state on-chain, where anyone can query them at any time
and where no marketing document can override them.

The chapter therefore carries, in order: the claims that are supported
by the specification (Section 25.2); the claims that are explicitly not
supported and must be removed from public materials (Section 25.3); the
honest status table (Section 25.4); the validation gates on the path to
production (Section 25.5); the honest messaging guide (Section 25.6);
and the on-chain implementation of all of the above (Section 25.7).

chapter that constitutes the supporting evidence. Rows carried from the
blueprint have been updated where the v1.2 evidence referenced the
fixed-quantity GFB Index: under v2.0 the same claim is supported by the

A claim belongs on this list only while its evidence chapter remains
part of the locked specification. If a methodology revision removes or
weakens the underlying mechanism, the claim must be re-examined before

— for example, a volatility figure after independent model validation —

before.

The last three rows are architectural rather than evidential: no amount
of validation can make them true, because they contradict the definition
of the unit itself. The first eight are evidential — they may become
supportable after the corresponding validation gates pass, at which
point the honest status table (Section 25.4) and the on-chain status
functions (Section 25.7) are updated through governance, never by silent
editing of marketing materials. The discipline is symmetric in both
directions: an unsupported claim is not merely omitted from
communications, it is actively refuted — the on-chain status string
denies fixed composition, and the publication layer (Chapter 24)
publishes the actual live weights that falsify any quoted percentage. A
project that will one day be able to prove its performance claims must
first be scrupulous about the claims it cannot yet prove, because the
credibility of the validated claim is built on the discipline of the
rejected one.
